P R E F A C E

a 2

## TO ALL
*Who love true*
# PIETY
*and*
# PATRIOTIC FREEDOM

Wishes prosperity and salvation

->JACOBUS VERHEIDEN.<-

---

<image-desc>A large ornamental historiated drop cap 'D' depicting a muscular figure wrestling with a lion, set within a landscape border of flowers and scrolling leaves.</image-desc>
The highest of all virtues is piety <note>The Dutch *Godt-vruchticheyt* implies both a fear of God and a life that bears spiritual fruit</note>: a good beyond all value is freedom. The latter <note>freedom</note> brings exceedingly great fruits in this present life, the former <note>piety</note> in the life to come. So that I may speak here of the goodness of piety and freedom among those who love these things, reason demands it, the current subject requires it, and the circumstances and the times also urge and desire it. Piety unites us with God; freedom unites us with Nature. Piety is above all pleasing to God; freedom makes a pleasant and lovely assembly—

<term>Piety</term> (Godt-vruchticheyt), <term>Freedom</term> (Vryheyt), <term>Patriotic</term> (Vaderlandtsche), <term>Jacobus Verheiden</term>, <term>Prosperity</term> (VVEl-varen), <term>Salvation</term> (Salicheyt)

<summary>Jacobus Verheiden opens his preface by praising piety and freedom as the two most essential virtues, framing them as necessary for both spiritual salvation and the preservation of the Dutch fatherland.</summary>
<keywords>Piety, Freedom, Patriotism, Jacobus Verheiden, Dutch Republic, Virtue, Salvation</keywords>

# P R E F A C E

society of men, and maintains it. Since man is made by Nature not only to exercise Reason, but also for Society <note>The original Dutch *Versamelinge* refers to a gathering or social assembly, here used in the sense of a structured civil society</note>; what, tell me, can be as firmly bound to Nature as this Freedom? For this reason, we shall first speak briefly of Piety, and thereafter of Freedom, as well as those things that conflict with both, and finally of the Preservation of both and their Harmony, without which no Republic or Society can endure or exist. All of this we shall relate fittingly, as it serves our purpose: so that the lovers of true Piety and Patriotic Freedom may understand what sincere cause I have for dedicating this work of mine to them.

The Foundation of all virtues, then, is Piety; if this is taken away, it is necessary that all Loyalty and the society of men, as well as that preeminent virtue, Justice—as Cicero <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BCE), the Roman statesman and philosopher often cited by Renaissance humanists</note> writes—be taken away as well. 

I do not speak here of the Piety or Religion of the Heathens, namely the Egyptians, Greeks, Romans, and others: nor do I wish to remark here on what has been written by Hermes Trismegistus <note>A legendary Hellenistic figure associated with a syncretic blend of Greek and Egyptian philosophy</note>, Plato, Cicero, and many others, who were all "Christians without Christ"—that is, those who in some ways were not far from the understanding that Christians have of God. Although they spoke of God in their own manner, they nevertheless did not please God. For no one can please God without God. No one has God without—

# P R E F A C E.

**a 3**

having known **Christ**. Therefore those people wander most foolishly who, speaking excellently of Divine things according to their human understanding, seem to place the Heathens among the number of the Godly. That is the true **Religion**, which binds us to **GOD** through **CHRIST**: from which Christ, as our Head and Sovereign, we are called Christians. This true **Religion** and **Faith**, from where else is it to be drawn than from **GOD’S WORD**? Where else is it to be found than by Him who has clearly spoken there: **SEARCH THE SCRIPTURES** <note>original: "ONDER-SOECT DE SCHRIFTVREN"; a reference to John 5:39, a cornerstone of the Reformation's emphasis on individual Bible reading</note>: he who strives against this, makes it known by deed that he is no Christian: and with such a person—as one who rejects or knows not the foundations or principles <note>This refers to the classical philosophical maxim *Contra negantem principia non est disputandum*—"one cannot argue with someone who denies basic principles"</note>—one should not dispute, as has been said of old. For the **Scripture** is like a **Plumb-line** <note>A "Recht-snoer" is a builder's tool used to ensure a straight line; here it means the absolute standard for truth</note>, according to which everything concerning Religion must be drawn. The **Scripture** is the **Touchstone** <note>A stone used to test the purity of gold and silver; metaphorically, a test for the truth of a doctrine</note>, which reveals and rejects everything that is false. It rejects human understanding, reasons, wisdom, and all fabricated and self-willed forms of worship, which we commonly call **Superstitions** or false belief, all of which originate from human ingenuity. Thus far concerning Religion or Piety. Now it remains to present something regarding **Freedom**. Nothing accords so well with **Nature** as **Freedom**, which is also given by **Nature** to the mute beasts. This is desired by the wicked as well as the good, the faint-hearted and the base along with the courageous: who

# P R E F A C E.

<meta>catchword: som-</meta>

—no one who is pious loses it, except with his own life, just as the wise and excellent historian **Sallust** <note>original: "Sallustius"; Gaius Sallustius Crispus was a Roman historian famous for his moralizing accounts of the Roman Republic's decline</note> writes, who is a brave and stern rebuker of vices. According to this **Freedom** we live as we wish, unless **Reason** or **Law**, and also **Decency**, desire otherwise. We are also forbidden by force to live according to Freedom. But of the freedom that is violated by force, as is well known, nothing is spoken here. True **Freedom**, namely that which is armed with **Laws**, is loved and embraced by the good. To which **Laws** so much is to be attributed, that those are considered truly free who, in the midst of their **Freedom**, acknowledge and confess to live according to the **Laws**. So that **Freedom** is to serve the **Laws** <note>This echoes the classical and Renaissance legal theory that true liberty is found within the rule of law—libertas per legem—rather than in lawless license</note>. It is therefore necessary that **Freedom** stands on the firm ground of **Laws** and **Virtues**.

This is a brief account of the very great good of life-awakening **Godliness** and **Freedom**. Which, if everyone together understood, embraced, and received with reverence, and also honored and maintained with good conduct; truly all things would be better and more steadfastly established in this unstable world. But since Good (as the ancient Sages have said) lives near to Evil: and the more glorious any Good is, the more heavily it is contested by enemies; so we want to look at what and of what kind the enemies are, who contested **Godliness** and **Freedom**, and labor to bring these down. There are then various kinds of enemies. Some openly with weapons, som-

<summary>This page defines "true freedom" not as lawlessness, but as the voluntary service to laws and virtues, citing the Roman historian Sallust. It argues that society would be stable if these principles were embraced, but warns that such virtues are constantly under attack by various enemies.</summary>

# P R E F A C E.
<meta>catchword: lovers</meta>

—some craftily oppose these with devious practices: then again there are others, who, as if ignorant and unwise, contend for **Godliness** and **Freedom** against **Godliness** and **Freedom** through their lives and conduct. Public enemies are the **Tyrants**: those who, when they combat **Religion** and **Freedom**, wage war against **Heaven** and **Nature**; they are rightly said to fight against **God**. A **Tyrant** is such a beast as is most hateful to **God** and to men. The world has always produced such untamed and monstrous creatures in great numbers, and today still brings them forth and sustains them. 

No examples are needed here. I believe that today the **Spaniard** <note>The author is writing during the Dutch Revolt (the Eighty Years' War), where the Netherlands fought for independence from the Spanish Crown.</note> is sufficiently evident to us, that most grasping and ambitious beast: of whose terrible **Greed** and **Pride** (which give birth to **Tyranny**) **History** and both worlds <note>This likely refers to the Old World (Europe) and the New World (the Americas), where the Spanish Empire was then expanding.</note> let us hear: and let also the writers, kings, princes, and republics of future times speak, when that Spanish tyrannical empire—acquired through wicked schemes—shall begin to crumble. 

How terrifying a hellish enemy the **Pope** is, we know well enough: he who promotes and supports not only his own **Antichristian Religion**, but also the ambition of the **Spaniard** and others, out of hatred for the true **Christian Religion**: he whose breast is full of villainy and who is a master of a thousand deceits. It is better to say little of his godlessness and roguery. There are now also very many who boast of being great lovers

<summary>The author identifies the primary enemies of Godliness and Freedom as "Tyrants," specifically identifying the Spanish Empire and the Papacy as monstrous forces driven by greed and deceit.</summary>

# PREFACE.

[...lovers] of **Religion** and **Freedom**, but *by their manner of life and the practice of virtues, they do not show this.* Truly, we err greatly if we presume that true **Religion** and the **Freedom** of our Fatherland can remain firm and long-lasting without the practice of the **Virtues** which are set before us by the rules and laws of both. **Religion** is most supported and preserved by **Piety**, and **Freedom** by **Virtue**. **Virtues** are increased, strengthened, and preserved by **Virtues**. Just as **Virtues** are obtained through **Virtues**, so it is also necessary that they be lost through Vices <term>Ghebreecken: moral failings or vices</term>. 

But what shall I say of Vices? These can much more easily be condemned than corrected: unless someone wishes to completely and entirely change this world of ours, in which the desire for Vices prevails. To this state have the nature and customs of **Europe** or **Christendom** now come (God help us), so that brave, wise, and upright men lament that the ancient **Discipline** and customs have fallen away: that the practices of **Piety** and the zeal for the **Virtues** are no longer as abundant and alert as they used to be. 

Behold the Kingdoms, Dominions, and Republics of **Europe**: in which the old and laudable ways of our ancestors have changed, or are changing daily more and more; those ancient ways upon which the Ancients <note>A reference to classical Greek and Roman philosophers and statesmen, whose works provided the foundation for Renaissance political thought.</note> spoke with all truth that the Republic, or the government of the Land and Cities, stood firmly. Let

<summary>The author argues that Religion and Liberty cannot survive without personal virtue and piety, lamenting a perceived moral decline across the nations of Europe compared to ancestral and classical standards.</summary>

where the *Italian*, the *German*, the *Frenchman*, the *Englishman*, the *Netherlander*, and many others are asked: they shall testify to the same. I mean among these nations those men who are wise and who have made virtue their own. For the common people often consider those things to be virtues which wise and upright men hold to be failings <term>Ghebreecken: moral flaws or vices</term>. This is testified to us through their sentiments and writings by many learned and wise men: among whom is Augerius Busbequius <note>Ogier Ghiselin de Busbecq (1522–1592), a Flemish diplomat and herbalist who served the Habsburgs in the Ottoman Empire and France.</note>, whom we, for his singular wisdom and experience, may rightly call the Comines <note>A reference to Philippe de Comines (c. 1447–1511), a writer and diplomat whose memoirs were considered the gold standard for political analysis in the Renaissance.</note> of our time, as one who also far surpassed Comines in terms of scholarship. This man writes vehemently against the corrupt manners of Europe, and that in writing wherein the excellence of his judgment is apparent. So too Hotman <note>François Hotman (1524–1590), a prominent French legal scholar and writer associated with the Huguenots.</note>, a very learned man in many excellent things, and likewise a very upright and courageous jurist, who speaks manfully against the corrupt manners and failings of his *Frenchmen*. In like manner does Friedrich Tilemann <note>Friedrich Tilemann (c. 1545–1596), a German legal historian.</note>, a man experienced in Law and History, who, being a *German*, does not express the best opinions regarding the manners of his *Germany*, as well as of Europe, in his Political Disputation on the Downfall of Republics: whose judgment, learning, and wisdom, being drawn from the very heart of history, show themselves to be singular. So too the *Englishman*, the *Pole*, and many others (I mean excellent men of the

# PREFACE.

*...lovers of ancient ways*) greatly despise the newly-corrupted customs of their Fatherland. But what shall we now say of the Swiss, whose Republic is praised above all, and who are held to be brave champions of their Liberty, as well as earnest pursuers of ancient ways? We still remember, when we dwelt among them, that we heard excellent, learned, and wise men judge not much good regarding certain new or foreign Swiss manners, creeping into the Republic from time to time. And Zwingli <note>Huldrych Zwingli (1484–1531), the primary leader of the Reformation in Switzerland, who often preached against foreign mercenary service and moral decay.</note> in his time was greatly disturbed, not suffering these same manners; so he also often diligently exhorted his people to the adoption of the ancient ancestral customs and practices. 

What Josias Simler <note>Josias Simler (1530–1576), a Swiss theologian and classicist whose work 'De Republica Helvetiorum' became the standard text on the Swiss government.</note> wrote about thirty years ago concerning the change in his ancestral manners in the Preface to his books which he published on the Swiss Republic, we may understand from his Eulogy <term>Lof-spreuck: a formal speech or writing of praise, intended here as a commendation of the Swiss state's virtues.</term> included here in this book on Folio 129. Which I, for the sake of those who love Liberty, to whom these Eulogies are also dedicated, have wished to place here. 

What judgment is now to be made of the Netherlanders? I mean especially those who are free there. That their manners are easier to criticize (as has been said) than to improve at this time, let the pious and wise men judge. This is presented here by one who loves true Piety and the Liberty of the Fatherland.

<meta>catchword: having.</meta>

He concludes by inviting the reader to consider the moral state of the free Netherlands.</summary>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from the previous page regarding the "Liberty of the Fatherland," concluding the thought that the author is someone who values both piety and freedom.</meta>

# PREFACE.

having. What shall I now say of Religion? If Liberty is not maintained with its virtues, and evil manners are allowed to progress, how shall we follow those ways that Religion requires? I find that to be true which sits in the mouths and stands in the books of some, and which has come forth from a man (a human being, I should say) of great name, namely: **"Nor does this present Time please me enough, in which we see (I shall make bold to say it) that there has been no time more pregnant with Religions, and less fruitful in Piety."** <note>This quote likely refers to the proliferation of various sects and dogmas during the Reformation, contrasting the outward profession of faith with the actual practice of a godly life.</note> Truly, right Religion requires Piety; and Piety requires Virtues: through Virtues, both Religion and Liberty must be maintained and preserved. 

Those who separate Liberty from Religion, and Religion from Liberty, act wrongly. Those also speak wrongly who call every person’s private opinion "Religion": and likewise those who honor <term>Licentie: unrestrained or lawless behavior</term> or the improper will to act as they please with the name of Liberty or Freedom. We have now (alas!) come to those times in which evil things are given good names, and good things are given evil names. There is but one true Religion, which is contained in the Holy Scripture. Liberty is enclosed within the Justice of the Laws. Now I turn to the Netherlanders <note>original: "Neer-landers"</note> who love Religion and Liberty, to whom I, as a Netherlander, have dedicated this Work.

<summary>The author argues that true Liberty and Religion are inseparable and must be rooted in virtue, law, and Holy Scripture, rather than personal opinion or lawless license. He specifically addresses his work to his fellow Netherlanders who value these principles.</summary>
<keywords>Religion, Liberty, Piety, Virtue, Netherlands, Holy Scripture, Rule of Law, Moral Decay</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory arguments from the previous page, shifting from the general relationship between Piety and Liberty to the specific political threat facing the Netherlands—the Spanish crown and the Roman Catholic Church.</meta>

# PREFACE.

*Consider here for a little time, and accept what is worthy of your thoughts.* Our Enemy <note>The author refers to King Philip II of Spain, the ruler against whom the Dutch were revolting during the Eighty Years' War.</note> (it is not necessary that I name him, as he is well enough known) who has been fighting against our Liberty for so many years now—by what right has he sought to deprive us of our Liberty? Is it not under the cover of Religion? By what right does he occupy and miserably torment the New World <note>The Americas</note>? By the cover of Religion, through the generosity of the most powerful Pope Alexander VI Borgia, one of his own, having been a Spaniard: who is regarded not as a man, but as a monster, as the historians testify to us. <note>Pope Alexander VI (Rodrigo Borgia) issued the 1493 bull *Inter caetera*, which granted Spain rights to lands in the Americas. Protestant writers often portrayed him as a symbol of corruption.</note> In the same way, he has worked marvelously, and still does, to occupy England, France, and other Kingdoms, being armed with his Religion, standing upon the powerful Bull of the Roman Bull-man. <note>A wordplay on the "Papal Bull" (an official decree) and the Dutch term *bullebak*, meaning a terrifying creature or bogeyman.</note> 

Therefore, he hides under the cover of Religion his Greed, Ambition, and his Tyranny, which arises from the aforementioned vices. Just as in former times the proud Xerxes <note>A Persian King</note> shielded himself against the heat of the Sun under the shadow of the great, beautiful Plane Tree <note>original: "Platani"</note> which spread its branches wide: so also does this man cover the heat of his burning Ambition under the shadow of Religion. In order to draw us under his yoke, he has left nothing unattempted that might serve him. Force and schemes have not been lacking there. Sometimes he has set out to subdue us with weapons: sometimes to draw us to his will through Discord or Dissension. Again he has

<summary>The author denounces the King of Spain for using Religion as a "cover" to hide his greed and desire for world-wide tyranny, specifically citing the colonization of the Americas and threats to European kingdoms.</summary>
<keywords>Philip II, Pope Alexander VI, Spanish Tyranny, New World, religious hypocrisy, Xerxes, Dutch Revolt</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that the Spanish King uses the "cover of Religion" to mask his political ambitions. The previous page ended with an analogy to Xerxes hiding under a plane tree; this page completes the thought by describing the King's specific tactics against the Dutch.</meta>

...thought it good to lure us to him through a false Peace: again through treacheries, clever deceit, and the enticement of some, he intended to carry out his affairs. He has also often attempted to remove the heads of the Government. <note>This likely refers to assassination plots against Dutch leaders, most notably William the Silent, who was assassinated in 1584.</note> He also considered it serving his interests to exclude the Dutch merchants from the ports of the kingdom of Spain. But what more? He also thought it good to drive out Religion with Religion. <note>The author suggests the King uses a distorted version of Catholicism to suppress the "true" Reformed religion.</note> Let us pay attention to this. For in this, our enemies seem to labor bravely. 

Just as the Sicilian Tyrant Dionysius <note>Dionysius I of Syracuse, often cited in Renaissance literature as the archetype of a cruel and suspicious tyrant.</note> had his, so also our Spaniard has his Sophists, whom we call Ignatians or Jesuits, who set fires all around: who, through their numbers, arts, and the sciences which they misuse, possess great power. They write, they trot, they run, spying out all places far and wide: now with sweet-talk, now with threats they persist. That which is at rest, they stir into unrest. These things move us to embrace and maintain Religion in its entirety, not only as Religion—that is, for the sake of God’s honor—but also through the love of Liberty. 

Which is why what we set before you here was not only rightly conceived some years ago, but also ingeniously expressed by a Coin <note>original: "Penning". These commemorative medals, or *geuzenpenningen*, were used as propaganda and symbols of resistance during the Dutch Revolt.</note> in the Netherlands. On one side of this Coin, one sees two Hands firmly grasping each other, holding arrows tightly gathered and pressed together between them, with such an Inscription: **WITH GOD'S HELP** <note>original: "DEO IVVANTE"</note>: On the other side stands a Column, where

<summary>The author describes the deceptive tactics of the Spanish King and the Jesuits in undermining Dutch Liberty, concluding with a description of a commemorative coin symbolizing unity and divine aid.</summary>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of a commemorative medal (penning) mentioned at the end of the previous page, using its iconography to explain the essential relationship between religious stability and political liberty in the Dutch Republic.</meta>

# PREFACE.

*On the base or foundation is a Book, named* Religion. *On the top of this* column *is placed a* Hat, *as a sign of* Liberty. <note>The "Hat of Liberty" (vrijheidshoed) was a common symbol in the Dutch Republic, representing freedom from Spanish tyranny.</note> *This* column is held by the hands of the Lords States of the United Netherlandish Provinces, *with this inscription:* THIS WE DEFEND: ON THIS WE LEAN, <note>original: "HANC TVEMVR: HAC NITIMVR"</note> *which is to say,* WE PROTECT LIBERTY: ON RELIGION WE LEAN. *Which both sides of this medal express with this meaning:* We, together and in unity leaning upon Religion, preserve our Liberty. *Very well. For without a foundation, the* Republic <note>original: "Republijcke"; referring to the newly independent Dutch state.</note> *cannot exist. Let then* Religious Liberty *stand firm, and* Religion [remain] free; *protected, that is, and being free from those things which might hinder both.*

*Let us then now not only avoid the Spanish* Sophists, <note>The author uses "Sophists" as a derogatory term for Jesuit scholars and Catholic polemicists who used clever arguments to oppose the Reformation.</note> *but also others, among whom are those who (I am reluctant to say) decorate [their faith with] new, strangely-fetched Christs, in mocking the martyrs of* Christ, *and in* simulating *and* dissimulating *place religion alongside godlessness: who, perhaps hoping for some* confusion, <note>original: "confusie"</note> *think that they will then be in order. # PREFACE.

...[men who] have fought the **ANTICHRIST** <note>In the context of the Reformation, Protestant writers frequently identified the Pope as the "Antichrist" described in biblical prophecy.</note> with great earnestness. *This is the Tempest or Storm-master, who sits there like a Jupiter on his Capitoline Hill,* <note>original: "Campidolischen berg"; referring to the Roman hill where the Temple of Jupiter stood, here used as a metaphor for the Vatican's power.</note> *moving everything with his thunder and lightning, tearing kings and princes apart from one another, closing and commanding throughout the world whatever he wishes, suffering no one who might judge him on this earth.* With these men then let us delight our eyes and minds: likewise also heartily pray to God, that the Kingdom of Christ, His Son, our only **Mediator** and **Savior** <note>original: "Middelaer ende Salichmaecker"</note>, may be daily more and more established, and that the Antichrist, as well as all Tyrants, may be cast down and brought to shame: That the Service of the Divine Word and of the Schools be maintained, from which those may come forth who might, in due time, protect and preserve Religion and our Fatherland’s Liberty <note>original: "Vader-lantsche-Vryheyt"</note> against the tyrants of souls and bodies.

The 20th of July, in the year after the Birth of our Lord Jesus Christ, 1602.

# TO THE GOD-FEAR-
### ing Observer.

You behold here, engraved from life, the Likenesses of some exceptional *Theologians* <note>original: "Theologische"; referring to scholars of divinity.</note> or men experienced in God’s Word, who have very vividly depicted for us the **ROMAN ANTICHRIST**. I say *Figures* or Likenesses of some—namely, those who have now died, and whose *Likenesses* we have been able to obtain. If one were to add other more excellent men who existed to these now standing before our eyes, there would be a great multitude; if they were all to be seen here, there would be an infinite number. For such a great multitude has existed who have contested the **Roman Antichrist**. These men, even if they were not known to us, would nonetheless be famous from the writings, *Censures*, and *Book-Registers* <note>original: "Boec-Registers"; likely referring to the *Index Librorum Prohibitorum*, the list of books banned by the Catholic Church.</note> of our adversaries. Good-hearted Reader: *Regarding the order of this work as proposed by us, you shall understand this. We have not always followed the chronological order of time. Not that we are unaware of the time in which each person lived; for we indicate in the <term>Eulogies</term> <note>original: "Lof-spreucken"; refers to the laudatory verses or biographical sketches accompanying each portrait.</note> how many years they lived, and in what year each one died. Rather, it is so that we might better understand the affairs conducted in the Church, as well as the studies and manners of life of many. Thus, we have placed together those who either had great fellowship with one another, or who were of one mind in all things, or who approached the <term>Roman Antichrist</term> <note>A common Reformation-era term for the Pope or the Papacy.</note> with similar weapons <note>meaning theological or intellectual arguments.</note>. # TO THE SINCERE-
## OBSERVER.

That we have brought forth in some *Eulogies* <note>original: "Lof-spreucken"; biographical tributes or panegyrics written to honor the deceased.</note> certain passages from the writings of those whom we praise—and that this is not the usual custom—should be understood as not having occurred without reason. For *Writers* <note>original: "Scribenten"</note>, when they speak for themselves, are considered to speak better than their interpreters or those who proclaim their praise. Furthermore, we have only done this when the matter and *History* <note>original: "Historie"</note> required it and when it was beneficial for our own times. Now, as for our frequent use of words and phrases taken from the *Art of Warfare* <note>original: "Krijsch-handel"</note>; let no one find fault with this. For we speak of those who have fought against the *Roman Antichrist* <note>A common Protestant designation for the Pope during the Reformation era.</note>, who is entirely occupied in defending his cause not only with *Papal Bulls* <note>original: "Bul-brieven"; formal proclamations issued by the Pope, named after the "bulla" or lead seal attached to them.</note> and deceptions, but also with wars. Who can then object if the sentiments of the combatants and those who stubbornly resist are aptly expressed through these *Military Metaphors* <note>original: "Krijchsh-spreucken"</note>? Furthermore, someone might wonder why the *Spaniard* <note>original: "Spaingniaert"; referring to the Spanish Empire, which at the time of this writing was the primary political and military opponent of the Dutch and the Reformation.</note> is so often attacked in this work. But one must consider that it is a wonder

# SONNET.

See here, *beloved observers*, depicted from life <note>original: "naer t' Leven"; a common phrase in 17th-century art and literature indicating that portraits were based on real likenesses.</note>
*The Godly Men*, who, chosen along with Paul <note>A reference to the Apostle Paul, suggesting the reformers share his divine calling.</note>
As the Trumpets of God’s Mouth, upon Zion’s watchtower <note>Zion is used here as a metaphor for the true Church; the "watchtower" implies their role in guarding the faith.</note>
Are driven by the LORD’S SPIRIT to warn us.
So that we may depart from BABYLON <note>In the context of the Dutch Reformation, "Babylon" was a common pejorative for the Roman Catholic Church, referencing the "Whore of Babylon" from the Book of Revelation.</note>, and live
According to GOD’S WORD, like newborn children
Zealous in LOVE, far from hate and wrath.
Therefore, read well through the short account described here.
Then you shall truly find them to be no simple laymen,
Pilferers, or skinners, as has been said (out of hatred) of them <note>original: "Pelfers, Velsers"; these are derogatory terms used by contemporary critics to suggest the reformers were low-born thieves or common laborers rather than legitimate scholars.</note>:
But Men as learned and pious as were ever found.
Yet not all who wrought such great work are shown here:
But only those whom we had at hand and quickly considered,
Though many more sorts of MEN we could have brought forward.

# BERENGARIUS

->*What do you point to with your right hand? Why do you gaze at high heaven?*<- <note>original Latin: "Quid dextrâ ostendis, cœlum, Quid suspicis altum?"</note>
->*Tell us, Berengarius: what is your intention here?*<- <note>original Latin: "Dic BERENGARI? Quis tuus hic animus?"</note>
->*I teach that Christ has been taken up into heaven in his Body.*<- <note>original Latin: "In cœlum doceo sublatum Corpore CHRISTVM."</note>
->*Thus, Christ in his Body is far removed from the Mass.*<- <note>original Latin: "Sic procul à Missa Corpore CHRISTVS abest."</note>

<note>Berengarius of Tours (c. 999–1088) was a medieval theologian whose views on the <term>Eucharist|The Christian ceremony commemorating the Last Supper, in which bread and wine are consecrated</term> were highly controversial. He argued against a physical "Real Presence," suggesting instead that because Christ’s physical body ascended to heaven, it cannot be physically present in the bread and wine of the <term>Mass|The central act of worship in the Catholic Church</term>. His ideas were precursors to later Protestant views on the sacrament.</note>

# BERENGAR,
### ARCHDEACON OF ANGERS.

Behold here the famous **Berengar**. Regard him as great, and marvel at such a man, who, beholding the Heavens and pointing to them with his hand, seems to indicate the place to which *Christ* was bodily and truly taken up and ascended in the sight of his Apostles: from whence he shall come again, just as they saw him ascending into Heaven. Observe this man, I say, who stands here as if on the threshold of this Holy Gallery <note>original: "Thoon-plaetfe"; literally a 'place of showing' or exhibition, referring to the book of portraits the reader is holding.</note>, in which are beheld (drawn from life) the likenesses of those who sought and taught Heavenly things: who preached the spiritual Kingdom of *Christ*, and with all diligence fought against the Kingdom of the *Antichrist* <note>In Reformation-era writing, "Antichrist" was a common pejorative for the Papacy.</note>. This is *Berengar*, Archdeacon of *Angers*, a Frenchman, who was a very fierce opponent of the natural or fleshly presence of *Christ* in the Lord's Supper: who, being well-versed in Holy Scripture and a diligent researcher of the writings of the Early Church Fathers, taught with a brave heart and spiritual zeal that, in the Sacrament of the Lord's Supper, *Christ* is not in the Bread in a natural way, but according to the manner and nature of a Sacrament—namely, as a sign. Therefore, because he could not suffer the fleshly presence of *Christ* in the Holy Supper, he seemed to throw the entire Kingdom of the Roman Pope (who seeks everything that is fleshly and earthly) into total turmoil. For this man's teaching, being confirmed by Holy Scripture and by the authority and testimonies of the true Early Church Fathers, was spread far and wide through Italy, Germany, France, and other lands, around the year of Christ's Ascension <term>1020</term> <note>original: "M. XX." The author appears to be dating Berengar’s influence to the early 11th century, though his primary conflicts with the Church occurred between 1047 and 1080.</note>. For that reason, a Council was called together by Pope *Leo* the <term>IX</term> at *Vercelli* (a city situated at the entrance of Italy near Piedmont): where the teaching of *Berengar* concerning the Sacrament of the Supper (in which he did not wish the Bread to be worshipped in place of God) was condemned. Likewise, some years later, a Council was gathered within *Rome* by <note>The text ends here; the catchword "Nico-" on the original page indicates the next page begins with Pope Nicholas II.</note>

<summary>This page introduces Berengar of Tours (c. 999–1088), an early medieval theologian who challenged the literal presence of Christ in the Eucharist. He is presented here as a precursor to the Reformation, whose scriptural arguments against the Papacy spread throughout Europe.</summary>
<keywords>Berengar of Tours, Eucharist, Lord's Supper, Archdeacon of Angers, Pope Leo IX, Council of Vercelli, Transubstantiation, Early Church Fathers</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, which introduced Berengar of Tours as a French archdeacon who challenged the doctrine of the physical presence of Christ in the Eucharist, sparking a major theological conflict with the Roman Church.</meta>

# BERENGAR
<meta>catchword: blanching-</meta>

To Nicholas II, who was crowned Pope in the year of our Lord *Christ* 1057 <term>M. LVII. (1057)</term>. At which Council *Berengar* was summoned: where, through the terrible threats of the Pope, he has (witness here the weakness of man) recanted his teaching. At which the Pope was wonderfully gladdened, and sent the Recantation to the Cities of Italy, Germany, France, and many other lands: as is evident from that most well-known chapter of the Popes' *Decrees*, "I, Berengar" <note>original Latin: "Ego Berengarius. de Consecrat. Distinct. II." This refers to a specific section of Canon Law containing his forced confession.</note>. In which these words are written, being full of horrific blasphemy, which were then read according to the agreement, judgment, and instruction of the Council, namely: *I believe that the Body of our Lord* JESUS CHRIST *is sensually (sensualiter) and in truth handled and broken by the hands of the Mass-Priests, and crushed into pieces by the teeth of the faithful.* Which words the clumsy Papal *Commentary* <note>original: "Glossa"; refers to the *Glossa Ordinaria*, the standard medieval commentary on law and scripture.</note>, noting and rebuking them, warned the Reader in this manner: *Unless you understand these words of* BERENGAR *entirely correctly, you shall fall into greater heresy than* BERENGAR *ever advocated.*

Henceforth the Papists may not accuse your teaching, *Berengar*, of novelty, for it is that which you defend with the word of God (which is the oldest teaching) and confirm with the ancient Fathers, especially with *Augustine’s* writings and opinions. This is also very clearly understood from the opposition of *Lanfranc*, who was a very harsh adversary to you. This *Lanfranc*, beloved observer and Reader, was from *Lombardy*, born in *Pavia*, being a man <note>original: "gast"; literally 'guest' or 'fellow'.</note> who was cunning and quick of mind, who at that time was very famous for his Sophistical tricks, and all kinds of subtleties and little arts: from which *Scholastic Theology*—that is, the school-sharp-wittedness of the Popes in Divine things—was called *Lanfranciana*: by which, at that time, the Theology of *Augustine* and *Jerome* was not much esteemed.

This *Lanfranc*, inflated by a mixture of subtlety and ambition, and fashioned according to the Pope's own mind, has knightly fought for the Fleshly Presence as if for a Roman *Helen* <note>A reference to Helen of Troy; the author suggests the doctrine of the physical presence of Christ was the "prize" over which this theological war was fought.</note>, against *Berengar*. Which champion, through his valiant services, was installed as Archbishop of *Canterbury* <note>original: "Cantelberg".</note> in *England*: before whom also *Alexander* II, successor to *Nicholas* II, stood up with reverence (which is something new for a Pope): and that through the love of the beautiful future bride, *Transubstantiation*, who, being yet without a name, was by *Lanfranc* through the...

<summary>This page describes the forced recantation of Berengar of Tours under Pope Nicholas II and introduces his primary intellectual rival, Lanfranc of Pavia. The text portrays Berengar's forced confession as "blasphemous" and credits Lanfranc with developing the Scholastic foundation for the doctrine of Transubstantiation.</summary>

<keywords>Berengar of Tours, Pope Nicholas II, Lanfranc of Pavia, Transubstantiation, Scholasticism, Eucharist, Council of Rome, Augustine, Canterbury</keywords>

## ARCHDEACON OF ANGERS.
**A ij**
<meta>catchword: -ing</meta>

the whitewash <note>original: "blancketſel"; a cosmetic used to hide flaws, here used metaphorically for deceptive arguments</note> of his Sophistry had now already seemed to give it some color. Previously, there were also *John Scotus*, a man of outstanding learning, a disciple of *Bede*, a fellow teacher of *Alcuin*, and *Bertram*, who at that time had a great name for his science and experience in the Holy Scriptures: who wrote against this bodily or Fleshly Presence, as it was not yet well known at that time: and their judgment was generally accepted, without there being any who publicly punished them. And that happened two hundred years or more before *Berengar* was born. Even today, the book of *Bertram* is found in the hands of many, having been frequently printed, written to *Charlemagne* <note>original: "Carolum Magnum"</note>: by whose command he made this same book, and also dedicated or assigned it to the Emperor *Charles*. Both of these, namely *Scotus* and *Bertram*, were very highly regarded by *Charlemagne* for their learning and godliness. But so that the Pope might better maintain his Bread-God <note>original: "Broot-Godt"; a polemical term used by reformers to mock the belief that bread becomes God in the Eucharist</note>, *Transubstantiation*—that is, the Change of Essence, or the translation of one Essence into another—as if born at Rome, also ran through the Roman world about a hundred years after *Berengar’s* death or thereabouts. Which also another *John Scotus*, surnamed *Duns*, a disciple of *Alexander of Hales* (which *Scotus* is commonly called the *Subtle Doctor* <note>original: "Doctor Subtilis"</note>, who died in the year of our Lord 1308) seems to acknowledge, writing on the fourth book of the *Sentences* of *Peter Lombard*, which is like a whetstone for the minds of the Papal School-Theologians. Likewise, there have been many other Papal Doctors who, not understanding the manner of this *Transubstantiation* or Change of Essence, and finding it alienated from the Holy Scripture, took refuge, as if to a sheet-anchor, in the Roman Church as the mother of this fine daughter. Again, there are many others who forge now this, now that manner of *Transubstantiation*, and are at odds with one another in this labor. So great is the labor and strife of the Roman Cyclopes <note>A mythological reference to the one-eyed giants, here implying the theologians are blind or single-minded in their work for the Pope</note> in fighting for this God-and-man-patchery <note>original: "Godt-en-menſch-flickerye"; likely a derogatory term for the doctrine of the physical presence of God in human form within the bread</note>. Which is also no wonder. For this *Transubstantiation* (since it is the soul of the Mass, which Mass is a summary of the Papal Religion) the Popes have seen fit to maintain and confirm with all their might, in all kinds of ways

<meta>catchword: -ing</meta>

<summary>The author argues that Berengar’s views were not a novelty, citing earlier scholars like John Scotus and Bertram who also opposed the doctrine of "fleshly presence" long before the term Transubstantiation became official Roman dogma.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the doctrine of Transubstantiation, moving from its 11th-century origins with Berengar of Tours to its formalization and ritualization under the medieval Papacy.</meta>

# BERENGARIUS
**A**
<meta>catchword: That is:</meta>

to decorate and to worship. So that this *Transubstantiation* would then have its full honor and reverence, Pope *Urban* IV, in the year of our Lord <term>1264</term>, instituted the *Feast of the Sacrament*, or Bread-God <note>original: "Broot-Godts"; a mocking term used by Protestant reformers to describe the consecrated host</note> (which they call the Body of Christ). This day was to be held as a formidable day, on the fourth day after the feast of the Trinity: honoring the Bread-God through processions, candles, torches, costly canopies <note>original: "verhemelde coſtelijcke tenten"; literally "heaven-clothed tents," referring to the baldachins used to cover the host during processions</note>, banners, solemn Masses, and Octaves. Furthermore, he proclaimed great Indulgences, so that this Roman God might be served and honored above all gods. Indeed, he finally gave orders to *Thomas Aquinas*, in honor of this feast, to gather some things together from the figures and shadows of the Old Testament: which *Thomas* has been regarded as a foster-father of *Transubstantiation*. This Feast has been so honored by subsequent Popes, and especially in our own times, that it is understood that through this idolatry, being complete, no greater idolatry could ever be added to it. I shall perhaps be rebuked by some here, that I (who should be speaking of the praise of *Berengar*) relate too many things here: but so that the diligence and very useful Teaching of *Berengar* might be better noticed, I have presented the *Mass* as a brief summary of all Heresies, which receives its spirit or life from *Transubstantiation*.

Let the miserable and hideous Mass-priests curse you then, *Berengar*; you who have *Christ* with you, sitting in Heaven at the right hand of his Father: you who have had repentance for the Recantation <note>Berengar was forced to recant his views on the Eucharist under threat of death or excommunication in the 11th century</note> to which you were forced by terrible threats, and have left behind an example to stand up for the Truth of Christ.

Further, Onlooker, know that we have obtained this *Effigy* or likeness of *Berengar* through the favor and love of Master *Francis van Molin*, Dean of *St. Savior's Church* at *Blois* in France, a man who is learned and experienced in Antiquities. He says he found this Figure, which you see here, in the year of our Lord <term>1568</term> in a very old book, resting in the Library of *St. Martin of Tours*: with the addition of these Verses, which make known to the Onlooker and Reader the singular honor and praise of *Berengar*. Which read as follows:

->*Whom [the world] now admires, etc.* <note>original: "Quem modo miratur, &c."</note><-

# ARCHDEACON OF ANGERS.
**A iij**
<meta>catchword: Upon</meta>

### That is:

*He who now makes the world wonder shall always do so,*
->This **BERENGARIUS**, who suffers an immortal death.<-
*He who trod the heavy path of Faith until the end,*
->On the first day of January <note>original: "Iani eerste dach"; referring to the month of Janus</note> we were deprived, to our discontent:<-
*That day, cursed day, and the treacherous world,*
->Which destroyed the ornament and form of all things,<-
*Which [laid down] the spiritual estate, virtue, hope, and glory,*
->Who laid down the Cord of Justice <note>original: "s'Rechts Snoer"; a metaphor for the divine standard of law</note>, and Justice itself, perishing,<-
Whatever the *Philosophers* or *Poets* once sang
->Vanishes through his Spirit, and the sweet-speech of his tongue.<-
*Whom the rough-woven garment covers: and beyond the need of thirst,*
->He never sought drink, and without hunger, neither food nor bread.<-
*When God gave us this pious, chosen man,*
->He spoke: "While others die, this one is born to Me."<-

### B E R E N G A R I U S,
### Archdeacon of Angers.

**Why do you lift your hand and look toward the eye of the Firmament?**
->**Tell us, Berengar? What is your meaning in this?**<-
**I teach that Christ’s body is in the high Heaven.**
->**Therefore, Christ’s true Being is far from the Mass.**<-

**BERENGARIUS** is given to the Christians,
->Whom he truly preached and taught to know Christ.<-
And he denied that **CHRIST'S** body lives in the Bread.
->Not as food for the belly: but as food of the Spirit, he honored Christ’s body.<-

Register of the Books of Berengar.
<meta>catchword: IOAN-</meta>

## On the abominable Transubstantiation, or the changing of the bread into the flesh and blood of Christ.

> Words odd in number, mumbled and marked with signs of the cross,
> The Mass-priest <note>original: "Mispaep"; a derogatory term used by Reformers for Catholic priests</note> uses like magic to conjure Christ together;
> But just as his Monster <note>referring to the physicalized host</note> distorts nature,
> So too can such raving have no place in my verses.

*Of a great Catalog or Register of the books made by Berengarius, we cannot recount anything remarkable:*

It is well to be thought that he, being well-learned and eloquent, must have written many diverse books: of which this is known:

Concerning the Body and Blood of the Lord.

In which book (writing against the fleshly presence of Christ in the Lord's Supper) he makes known that he is well-practiced in the Holy Scriptures and the books of the ancient Fathers: as can be clearly perceived from the counter-writings of Lanfranc <note>Lanfranc of Bec (c. 1005–1089), the Archbishop of Canterbury and a major theological opponent of Berengar</note>.

Berengarius, now having repentance for the retraction of his teaching which occurred in the Roman Council, has also written:

Against the Roman Council, and namely against Cardi-

<column-break/>

nal **Humbert** <note>Cardinal Humbert of Silva Candida, who took a hard line against Berengar at the Council of Rome in 1059</note>; *by whose prescription the form of the retraction in the Council was established.*

In the book *Concerning the Body and Blood of the Lord*, *he writes thus of the Antichristian Church:*

Truly, *he says*, the Truth has not perceived that the Church is holy, but rather that it is the Church of the wicked, and a dwelling place of Satan.

Which is also witnessed by Cardinal Benno, Arch-Priest of the Roman Church, who (during the life of Pope Hildebrand, later named Gregory VII, an ambitious Monster <note>The author uses "Monster" to describe the Pope, reflecting the intense religious animosity of the period</note>, by whom it is thought that Nicholas II was poisoned) writes thus of that same time: The religion of the Clergy at that time brought forth nothing other than treachery and greed.

# JOHN WYCLIFFE
### ENGLISHMAN.

Here is this WYCLIFFE shown, as the one who, among all the learned and godly chosen men of former times, first in *England* uncovered the mysteries of the Kingdom of wickedness and deceit. This Island and Kingdom, flourishing above others, has produced for us many kinds of men skilled in the arts and brave and experienced in the word of God, who spoke out against, opposed, and rejected the misunderstandings, blindness, and corrupt morals (which had greatly increased in the Church during their time) through both preaching and writing. Among these (held as the most prominent) are *Bede, Alcuin, John of Salisbury, Giraldus, Nigel, Neckam, Sewal, Bacanthorius, Ockham, Hampole,* and *the man of Armagh*: <note>original: "Beda, Alcuinus, Ioannes Carnotensis, Giraldus, Nigellus, Neckamus, Sevallus, Bacanthorius, Ockamus, Hampolus ende Armachanus." These refer to historical English and Irish scholars like the Venerable Bede, William of Ockham, and Richard FitzRalph.</note> whose godly teachings and faithful judgments our *Wycliffe*, searching into them well and following after them, confirmed and further explained. And among their other writings, a certain book against the religion of the <term>Mendicant Orders</term> <note>Religious orders such as the Franciscans and Dominicans who relied on alms (begging) for support.</note> was held in very high regard; it was published at that time by *Richard of Armagh*, Primate and Archbishop in *Ireland*, a man very learned in the Law and the word of God. He was held in great esteem during the time of the man whom we show here and remember to the honor of God: being a man truly of great understanding and excellent learning, who at *Oxford* in *Merton* College <note>original: "Oxfort int Collegie van Marton"</note> took up and mastered the Liberal Arts (as the learned call them) with singular diligence and boldness. Because of this, he showed himself above others in <term>Scholastic Theology</term> <note>A method of critical thought which dominated teaching in medieval universities, combining religious dogma with Aristotelian philosophy.</note> through the sharpness of his spirit and his esteemed wit, and was beheld with wonder for his judgments, good character, and learning. And having now been brought into prominence, he stepped before the eyes of those powerful and numerous swarms of Begging Monks (as the aforementioned *Richard of Armagh* had done before him). To clash with these monks was, in the opinion of many, harder than to clash with the Pope himself. And not leaving it at that (as one who now had more power through the favor and goodwill of many), he attacked the head himself—from whose brains, like a Roman <term>Jupiter</term>, <note>A mythological reference to the birth of Minerva from Jupiter’s head; the author suggests the Pope "birthed" the Mendicant orders from his own will.</note> these filthy, idle Begging Monks, the bilge-water stench <note>original: "kiel-stanck"</note> of all ships, in dissatisfaction... <meta>catchword: over-</meta>

<summary>A biographical introduction to John Wycliffe, positioning him as the successor to a long line of English scholars who opposed Church corruption and specifically criticizing the Mendicant Orders.</summary>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of John Wycliffe, focusing on his theological challenges to the Papacy and the posthumous treatment of his remains. It follows the description of his education at Oxford mentioned on the previous page.</meta>

# JOHN
<meta>The header "IOANNES" is the continuation of his name "IOANNES WICLEFUS" from the previous page's section header.</meta>

(which had overflowed) he boldly attacked: and would not recognize or permit the Pope to be the Head of the Churches: but instead declared him to be the true *Antichrist*. The Bulls, Indulgences, Masses, and their other theatrical plays <note>original: "Battement-spelen," referring to the ritualized and performative nature of the late medieval liturgy which reformers viewed as hollow theater.</note>, as well as whatever else they muttered under their breath and had introduced without the authority of God’s word, he rejected and refuted. He likewise rejected and despised that horrible *Transubstantiation* (the changing of the bread into the Body of Christ), the magical anointing, the tyrannical <note>original: "Beulachtige," literally "executioner-like" or "cruel."</note> interrogation of consciences (or auricular confession), the vain and foolish works of satisfaction, the first, second, and extreme unction, along with all other superstitions or fabricated religious services. In short, he showed and confirmed that everything must be measured and judged according to the plumb line of the Gospel, and not by the decrees or the Pope’s ordinances; according to the words of the Lord Christ, and not the words of the Pope. 

And because of this, he preached the Gospel with great fruitfulness under *Edward* III. Under the highly praised virtue of this King, *Wycliffe* stood secure; through the King's power and influence, the Roman Pope at that time lost the right to choose bishops, as well as the governance and control over the receipt of spiritual tithes, such as Peter’s pence <note>An annual tax of one penny from every householder in England paid to the Papal See.</note>. *Polydore Vergil*, looking back on this later, took it so poorly that he reviles *Wycliffe* in his chronicle as a shameful and stubborn heretic. Therefore, we should not wonder that the Pope (claiming to be a follower of the fisherman *Peter*) has sought after the English Island’s rich "fishery"—because of the great revenue it brought in—with such zeal, and has sought to recover it, and still seeks it today. 

Under King *Richard* II, who reigned afterward, this pious man was sent into exile, yet he remained steadfast in everything. Having recently returned from there, he died in England in the year of the birth of Christ, whom he preached, on the last of December, <term>1387</term> <note>original: "M. CCC. LXXXVII."</note>, and was buried in his parish church at *Lutterworth*. 

Afterward, in the year <term>1428</term> <note>original: "M. CCCC. XXVIII."</note>, which was <term>41</term> <note>original: "XLI."</note> years after his death, his bones—under Pope *Martin* V, following the Council and the Decree of *Siena*—were dug up and burned to ashes as the remains of a horrible and cursed man. Thus, even after his death, he experienced the extreme fury of those whom he had preached against as being cruel above all others during his lifetime. According to the testimony of *Aeneas Sylvius* <note>The secular name of Pope Pius II (reigned 1458–1464), who was also a noted historian.</note>, he...

<meta>catchword: Silvii, (referring to the continuation of the name Aenea Silvii on the next page)</meta>

These are the Books, indications, or titles through which he most vehemently attacks the Pope.

Moreover, he has written many books on Philosophy, and many through which he explains Holy Scripture, the Register of which can be seen in the *Centuries* <term>BALEUS</term> <note>original: "BVLAEI." This refers to John Bale (1495–1563), an English historian whose "Centuries" (Catalogus) of British writers was a foundational text for Protestant bibliography.</note>: who, following the custom of *Johannes Trithemius* in his *Register of Ecclesiastical Writers*, has indicated the first words of the Prefaces, and almost all the chapters of the books.

Wycliffe also translated the Bible into the English language, and attached to each book his Preface and arguments.

He also translated the twelve books of *Clement*, Priest of the Church of *Lanthony*, titled *The Unity of the Evangelists*, with many of the older Doctors' treatises or explanations. And from this, one can sense the divine and resourceful intellect of <term>WYCLIFFE</term>, having produced so many books for the Church.

# JOHN WYCLIFFE
### ENGLISHMAN.

**How great your Papal power and your raging hate,**
**Your executioner-like murderousness, was in that time:**
**The bones of the saints, which you would not let rest.** <note>Wycliffe died in 1384, but at the Council of Constance in 1415, he was declared a heretic. The council ordered his remains to be exhumed, burned, and cast into a river, a sentence carried out in 1428.</note>
**That same act, to your shame, still reproaches you today.**

*The King of the Shaven <note>original: "Gheſchoren"—a derogatory reference to the tonsured heads of Catholic monks and priests</note>, seeking only the earthly,*
*Has filled the world with deceitful teachings:*
*But Wycliffe, like God’s angel, seeking the pure heavenly things,*
*Says to the English: Flee the contentious host of the Shaven.*

## On the Cruelty and Madness of the Papists.

Long after Wycliffe’s bones had been buried,
That cruel Papist people consumed them with fire and flame,
And through such a ghastly deed, like mad Papal slaves,
(In the scattering of the ashes) the Christian people were awakened.

### JAN HUS, THE BOHEMIAN. <note>Jan Hus (c. 1372–1415) was a Czech theologian and philosopher who became the most prominent figure of the Bohemian Reformation. He was influenced by Wycliffe’s writings and was eventually burned at the stake for heresy.</note>

->The Emperor’s promise was broken for this man; you were condemned unjustly,<- <note>original: "Cæsaris huic violata fides". This refers to the "safe-conduct" granted by Holy Roman Emperor Sigismund, which was supposed to guarantee Hus's safety at the Council of Constance but was ultimately ignored by the Church authorities.</note>
->A man of piety and a lover of the true religion.<-
->Did he not say: "After a hundred years have passed,<-
->Must you, O wicked crowd, give an account to God?"<- <note>original: "Nonne (inquit) lapsos post centum iugiter annos / Danda deo ratio est impia turba tibi?" This refers to a famous prophecy attributed to Hus at his execution: "Today you roast a goose [Hus means 'goose' in Czech], but in a hundred years a swan will come whom you will not be able to roast." This was later interpreted by Protestants as a prediction of Martin Luther.</note>

**Jan Hus:** <term>Jan Hus</term> (the Latinized version of his name). **Bohemian:** <term>Bohemus</term> (a person from Bohemia, a kingdom in the Holy Roman Empire, now part of the Czech Republic). **The Emperor's:** <term>Caesaris</term> (referring to the Holy Roman Emperor). **Promise:** <term>fides</term> (literally "faith" or "trust," here meaning the pledge of safe-conduct). **Religion:** <term>religio</term>. **A hundred years:** <term>centum annos</term>.

<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of John Wycliffe’s legacy in England to his influence on the European continent, specifically in Bohemia (the modern-day Czech Republic). # JOHN HUS,
## A BOHEMIAN.

Truly incredible indeed was the power and might of the Gospel spread by the aforementioned *Wycliffe* in *England*. Its strong growth and advancement were hindered neither by the wide, intervening Atlantic Ocean, nor by far-stretching lands, so that this mighty word could not be stopped from passing through the high mountains and the thick, dark forests (with which the Kingdom of *Bohemia* seems to be enclosed and encircled as if by walls). Behold there, **John Hus** of **Prague**, in the midst of the land of *Bohemia*, as well as in the aforementioned capital city of the Kingdom and its widely-famed University <note>original: "hooge Schole"—literally "High School," but referring to the Charles University in Prague, founded in 1348</note>, became a preacher of this Gospel. He strongly confirmed the writings and propositions of the aforementioned *Wycliffe*, as well as his own already preached and increasing doctrine.

Being now a shepherd of the *Bethlehem* Chapel <note>The Bethlehem Chapel in Prague was a significant site for the Bohemian Reformation; it was unique because preaching was conducted in the Czech language rather than Latin.</note>, and intending to feed the flock of God with the Heavenly bread, he did so (not according to the Pope's decrees or institutions—those useless secretions—nor according to the corrupt brains of a human head, the husks of outward works, nor the superstitious or ornate leaves of fabricated religious services). For that is to properly perform the office of a true *Bethlehem* Shepherd: namely, that he should rightly divide the heavenly bread (through which souls are nourished for eternal life) in God’s house and faithfully distribute it to the sheep of Christ.

This godly man, having been condemned as a heretic by Pope *Alexander* V, appealed (through a public writing) to Christ, the true Judge. At that time, things were going very poorly for the Roman Church, for the Cardinals (as a notable punishment from God, well to be noted in the changing of these ages) were in conflict and divided among themselves, having elected three different Popes. The Kings (seeing the Roman Church in such a meager state and decay) took this very poorly. Therefore, some requested a *Reformation* of the Church; *Pierre d’Ailly* <note>original: "Petro van Alliaco"</note>, Cardinal of *Cambrai*, himself insisted upon it. In order to provide for this and to set Roman affairs right, the Council at

<meta>The previous page ended mid-sentence, discussing the "Bethlehem Shepherd" (John Hus) who "rightly divides the heavenly bread (through which souls are nourished)." This page continues with the historical account of Hus’s trial and execution at the Council of Constance.</meta>

# JOHN HUS

Once the Council of **Constance** was established in Germany in the year 1414, *John Hus* was also summoned there. He arrived at the aforementioned *Constance* under the protection and safe-conduct of Emperor *Sigismund*. Despite this, he was thrown into the dungeon, and the Emperor’s assurance was violated and broken by that overly-arrogant assembly. It was proposed and decided by the *Council* that **no faith should be kept with heretics** <note>original: "Den Ketteren gheen gheloof gehouden te wefen" — This refers to the decree *Haereticis non est servanda fides*, which the Council used to justify ignoring Hus's legal safe-conduct</note>. Meanwhile, the Bohemians pleaded in vain against the injustice of this act. Finally, these "zealous fathers" were ignited with such great and raging wrath that they understood and judged that the aforementioned *Hus*, along with all his books, must be cast into the midst of the fire.

But in you, *Hus*, the Holy Spirit at that time kindled and lit other fires. The Bishops degraded you <note>A formal ceremony stripping a priest of his holy orders</note>; they stripped you of the *Antichrist’s* tunic, you who were destined to climb to Heaven to Christ clothed in that glorious garment of Faith. And as some of them approached you with a razor to flay the tonsure from your head <note>The tonsure is the circular shaved patch on a priest's scalp; the bishops were arguing over the ritual method of removing his clerical status</note>, and others with shears to skin it, and they fell into a dispute over it, you (turning toward the Emperor) said these words, not without joy: *“Oh, what little unity there is among these Bishops; and it is a wonder indeed that while they are all equally cruel, they cannot agree even in the manner of their cruelty.”*

Now, wearing a paper crown upon your head <note>Hus was forced to wear a tall paper hat painted with devils and the word "heresiarch" (leader of heretics) as a form of public shaming</note>, you far surpassed in dignity and blessedness the one who wears the triple crown of gold and precious stones with such pride <note>A reference to the Papal Tiara, the three-tiered crown worn by the Pope</note>. And so, this highly gifted man was burned on the sixth of July the following year. 

*Hus* (whose name means "Goose" in the Bohemian language) prophesied that in his time he would bring forth a Swan in Saxony <note>This is a famous legend: Hus reportedly said, "Today you bake a goose, but a hundred years from now a swan will arise whom you will not be able to silence." This was later interpreted as a prophecy of Martin Luther, whose coat of arms often featured a swan.</note>. He foretold that the whiteness and the sound of the Swan's voice would greatly unsettle and terrify the black Ravens and the unclean creatures nesting in the high crags—those *Harpies* or "fair-faced" birds of prey who defile everything they touch. 

*Hus* having now been taken into Heaven on the wings of divine mercy and Faith, the Bohemians in those lands waged heavy but successful wars against the Roman *Cyclopes* (those one-eyed giants who fight against Heaven). These Bohemians followed *Ziska* <note>Jan Žižka (c. 1360–1424), a brilliant Bohemian general and leader of the Hussites who never lost a battle</note>, the Emperor’s chief banner-bearer, who displayed a Goose and a Chalice <note>The Chalice became the symbol of the Hussites because they demanded "Utraquism"—the right of the laity to receive both the bread and the wine (the cup) during Communion</note>. Beholding these symbols, they shed their blood in defense of the teaching of the one who defended the Chalice as a true sign of the blood of Christ shed for us.

<summary>This page recounts the betrayal, trial, and execution of John Hus at the Council of Constance in 1415, his famous prophecy regarding a "Swan" (Luther), and the subsequent Hussite Wars led by Jan Žižka.</summary>
<keywords>John Hus, Council of Constance, Emperor Sigismund, martyrdom, Jan Žižka, Hussite Wars, the Chalice, Protestant prophecy, Martin Luther</keywords>

# THE INDEX OF THE BOOKS
->published by John Hus.<-

**John Hus’s** works, which are still available today, were printed together at Nuremberg in the printing house of Montanus and Neuber, in the year 1558. In this order:

The explanation of the Faith <note>The Apostles' Creed</note>, the Ten Commandments, the Lord's Prayer, along with several other writings on Mortal Sin, Marriage, and the knowledge and love of God.

On the three kinds of people, and the seven deadly sins. On the <term>Sacrament</term> of the Body and Blood of Christ. On the partaking of the Blood of Christ under the form of wine by the Laity <note>This refers to the practice of Utraquism—giving both bread and wine to the congregation—which Hus advocated against the prevailing Catholic custom of the time</note>.

On the perfection of the Law of Christ for the governance of the Church.

A discourse on the declaration of the Faith.

A discourse on the words "Peace be to this house."

Two Letters of Peace [No.] 68. By Hus and several others, written during the Council of Constance.

An account of the beginnings and the progress of John's struggle with the Pope's Faction or Court <note>original: "Bourt" — referring to the Papal Curia or inner circle</note>.

The contents of the appeals from the Archbishop of Prague to the Pope, against the burning of the books of John Wycliffe <note>John Wycliffe (c. 1320–1384) was an English theologian whose reformist ideas served as a primary inspiration for Hus</note>.

Letters written during the time when John Hus was driven out of Prague by the <term>Ban</term> <note>A sentence of excommunication or legal outlawry</note>.

<column-break/>

On the reading of heretical books.

A formal act in defense of John Wycliffe’s book on the Trinity.

A reply against the Englishman John Stokes, the slanderer of Wycliffe.

A defense of several of Wycliffe's articles.

On the removal of temporal goods from the Clergy.

John Hus on Tithes.

A reply against a disguised enemy.

A reply against a preacher of Plymouth <note>original: "Plemuyen"</note>.

A question regarding the rebuking of the Clergy for their preaching.

The conclusion of the dispute, with his short treatise on the most glorified blood of Christ.

A treatise on the body of Christ.

A treatise on the three doubts that occurred in Helmuth <note>Likely referring to Olomouc (Olmütz), where Hus had theological influence</note>.

A dispute concerning the Faith.

The <term>Bull</term> <note>An official papal decree sealed with a "bulla" or lead seal</note> of <term>Indulgences</term> <note>Certificates sold by the church to reduce temporal punishment for sins</note> of Pope John XXIII regarding the aid against Ladislaus, King of Naples <note>original: "Apulien" — Southern Italy</note>.

Another Bull and Commissions granted regarding the raising of the Cross <note>The proclamation of a crusade</note> against Ladislaus.

A dispute concerning the Indulgences or the crusades of John XXIII.

A booklet on the six [errors] fused together <note>original: "sesse t'samen gesmolten" — refers to Hus's famous work "De sex erroribus" (On Six Errors), which was also inscribed on the walls of the Bethlehem Chapel in Prague</note>.

<meta>This page continues the catalog and poetic tribute to the Bohemian reformer John Hus, following the bibliography of his works. # JOHN HUS OF BOHEMIA

Wycliffe’s disciple, Hus, stays in the heart of the forest, <note>Likely a reference to Hus’s period of exile in the Bohemian countryside between 1412 and 1414, where he continued to preach and write.</note>
*As* Wycliffe did on the Island, which the Ocean surrounds. <note>Referring to England, the home of John Wycliffe.</note>
Both praising Christ, where the Tyrant of Souls <note>The Pope.</note> feels distressed,
And for God’s word, fire and water, they stand as friends. <note>This imagery links the two reformers through their post-mortem fates: Hus was burned at the stake (fire), while Wycliffe’s exhumed remains were burned and the ashes cast into the River Swift (water).</note>

### On the Cruelty and Faithlessness of the Papists.

Hus and Wycliffe both consumed by flaming fire:
One shows the Pope’s Cruelty, the other his Treachery,
Revealing what Faith is, and the "Kindness" of old Rome;
Now no Glory remains in Rome, but the fullness of evil. <note>The poet uses a wordplay on the Dutch words "Roem" (glory/fame) and "Room" (Rome), suggesting that Rome has traded its former spiritual glory for wickedness.</note>

### Against the Roll of the Apostolic Chamber and the Roman Bull-Market. <note>A critique of the "Bullen-craem," the trade and sale of Papal bulls, indulgences, and ecclesiastical offices managed by the Apostolic Camera.</note>

*Anser* <note>original: "Anser" — Latin for "Goose."</note> in Rome is Hus, according to the Bohemian tongue. <note>In the Czech language, "Husa" means goose. Hus famously joked about himself as a "goose" that would be followed by a "swan" (later identified by Protestants as Martin Luther).</note>
By Geese, Rome once remained standing free. <note>A reference to the historical legend of the sacred geese of Juno on the Capitoline Hill, whose honking alerted the Romans to a night attack by the Gauls in 390 BC, saving the city.</note>
Why is this Goose now against Rome? Her many failings
Are the cause, as wickedly Wholly as ever Goose-pens wrote. <note>The original Dutch text plays on the similarity between "Gans" (Goose) and "Gants" (Entirely/Wholly). The "Goose-pens" refers to the quill pens used by Hus and other reformers to document the Church's corruption.</note>

<meta>This page continues from the discussion of Jan Hus, transitioning into a biography and defense of his closest associate and fellow reformer, Jerome of Prague. It emphasizes Jerome's intellectual prowess and the coordinated academic opposition he faced at the Council of Constance.</meta>

HIERONYMVS
C iij
<meta>catchword: so</meta>

# JEROME
## OF PRAGUE.

See here the excellent countenance, forehead, eyes, and gravity of JEROME OF PRAGUE. In short, in all your being and appearance, you are, Jerome—according to the general judgment of the physiognomists <term>physiognomists: scholars who claimed to judge a person's character and soul by their outward physical appearance</term>, or those who study the human form (as such things are attributed to them)—exactly as you have shown yourself to be in your deeds. Through his courage and virtue, he has proven himself, beloved viewer, to be an upright man.

For when he stood up for his fatherland, as well as for John Hus—who had been unworthily and by improper means seized and oppressed by many slanders—he traveled to Constance. Staying quietly at Überlingen <note>A town on the northern shore of Lake Constance.</note>, which is an Imperial city located a mile from there, he requested public safe conduct from the Emperor but could obtain none. While he was demonstrating and proving the innocence and defense of the aforementioned Hus, as well as his own, throughout his fatherland against godless accusers and slanderers, he was taken prisoner through certain ambushes planned for him along the way and brought bound to the aforementioned Constance.

Here the accusers swarmed together like hornets and attacked Jerome with a great clamor. Gerson <note>Jean Gerson (1363–1429), the powerful Chancellor of the University of Paris and a central figure at the Council of Constance.</note>, the Chancellor of the University of Paris, well known to everyone, recalled and cast before him in their midst that which Jerome had formerly said and proposed in Paris in a certain new manner—as one who, with his arguments and syllogisms, had thrown the academy or university there into turmoil. Likewise, a certain presumptuous theologian from Cologne presented to the slanderers the oration that this Jerome had delivered in Cologne in times past. And similarly, a "Master of Ours" <note>original: "Magister noster" — a formal academic title for a Doctor of Divinity.</note> from Heidelberg, seated high in his chair, also fiercely attacked Jerome and brought forward what he had observed and noted about him in Heidelberg; and so did many others. And because Jerome was a man of spirit and body, appearance, learning, virtue, and eloquence, most excellent, and richly gifted with that which best becomes a man,

HIERONYMVS
<meta>Catchword: have</meta>

Thus everyone paid more attention and cast their eyes upon him. Therefore, those pale-faced and effeminate <term>effeminate: translated from the Dutch "Vvyfvische," a derogatory term used by reformers to suggest that the celibate Roman clergy were weak or unmanly</term> pursuers of Roman harlots assigned and overwhelmed such a great and excellent man with certain death. This man shouted and railed much against the corrupt customs of the Popes, their pride, greed, whoring, and their unspeakable unchastity (as *Hus* and *Wycliffe* had done before). Finally, he was condemned to the fire and brought to the famous burning-place of *Hus*: by whose memory the flame of godliness and virtue was all the more ignited and kindled in his soul. The strength of his spirit and his contempt for death were so wondrous and great in him that when he stood at the stake, one could see that with a more righteous spirit, he considered the extreme torments to be small and light. So that even when the Executioner <term>Executioner: translated from "Scherp-rechter," literally meaning "sharp-judge," the officer responsible for carrying out capital punishment</term> wanted to light the fire from behind his back, unobserved, he said to him: *“Bring it here, Executioner, and light the fire before my eyes: for if I had feared to see that, I would never have come to this place (which I could easily have avoided and fled).”* The whole city of *Constance* looked upon this holy Jerome’s true manly virtue and constancy with wonder. Just as *Poggio* <note>Poggio Bracciolini (1380–1459), an Italian scholar and papal secretary who witnessed the trial and was deeply moved by Jerome's eloquence and bravery.</note> (as an eyewitness) expressed that same man's boldness and steadfastness through a great, very learned, and eloquent letter. Concerning this, *Aretinus* <note>Leonardo Bruni (c. 1370–1444), an influential Italian humanist and historian often called "Aretino."</note> writes in the fourth book of his Letters to *Poggio*: *“Three days ago, we learned of the last judgment of* JEROME *from the copy of your letter sent to us by Barbara; the beauty of which letters I must rightly praise. Yet you seem to attribute more to him than I would wish to do: and although you often excuse your judgment, nonetheless I do not know what greater inclination of spirit hides beneath it. I am of the opinion that one ought to write concerning this with caution.”* Yet you, *Poggio*, need not be ashamed of that letter, nor regret having written it, since it contains more virtue, worthiness, and true learning than five hundred of your humorous books <note>original: "cluft-boecxkens." This refers to Poggio's *Facetiae*, a collection of witty and often ribald or scandalous anecdotes that were very popular at the time.</note> which you have publicly poured out like the scum and filth of your spirit.

Whether anything was written by Jerome of Prague and left to posterity is unknown to me.

<column-break/>

Yet it is believable that such a learned and eloquent man, especially in those dark times, [would] have [written] something excellent...

> When the cruel flame consumes the man's bent knees.
