# JEROME
### SAVONAROLA.

Following Jerome of Prague in due order here is *Jerome of Ferrara* <note>original: *Hieronymus van Ferraren*, referring to Girolamo Savonarola (1452–1498), the Italian Dominican friar and reformer</note>. In names, in the pursuit of things, and in the matters they dealt with, both were holy. Equal in greatness of heart: equal in learning, in freedom of speech, in eloquence, in steadfastness, in their cause, and in the manner of their deaths; both strictly punishing the godlessness, negligence, pride, unchastity, and especially the adultery <note>The Dutch *hoerdom* literally means "whoredom" or "fornication," often used in this period to denote both moral corruption and spiritual infidelity</note> of the Popes and Clerics. But this man was a Monk of the Dominican order; the former [Jerome of Prague] swore no allegiance to any laws of the hooded, shaven, or anointed <term>hooded, shaven, or anointed: a derogatory reference to the traditional marks of the Catholic clergy—the monastic cowl, the tonsure (shaved crown), and the anointing of priests during ordination</term>. Having now become a preacher, *Jerome of Savonarola* used and observed his preaching office and good gifts well, through which his prestige and influence among the people of Florence was very great. Because he was a sharp and strict rebuker and punisher of faults, he uncovered many grave matters and abuses within the clergy and the Roman Papacy, and by proclaiming these openly, he predicted and promised great punishments, even the very uprooting of Rome.

In the year 1490, while explaining the Revelation of John <note>The Book of Revelation</note>, he applied and interpreted the greater part of it toward the depiction of the Pope and the description of his downfall: he also taught many things in matters of Faith against the Pope and his Primacy or Overlordship. Furthermore, when Charles the Eighth, King of France, undertook his expedition through Italy to wage war against the Pope, the Emperor *Maximilian*, and the Venetians, *Jerome* often preached and predicted in his sermons that this same Charles was raised and called by God to invade and destroy Italy: as a public rod <note>A "rod" is a biblical metaphor for an instrument of divine punishment</note> over those who lived and moved in all kinds of godlessness and unchastity, also so that he might bring the clergy within bounds and limits, and punish the Tyrants of the Church. If not, he warned that God would punish Charles himself for his negligence. With such great vehemence was this zealous Preacher driven and broke out against the pride, unchastity, and grave faults of the Priests.

<summary>This page introduces Girolamo Savonarola, comparing his zeal and martyrdom to Jerome of Prague and detailing his critiques of the Papacy in Florence. It highlights his prophetic view of the French invasion under Charles VIII as a divine punishment for Church corruption.</summary>

<keywords>Savonarola, Florence, Dominican Order, Charles VIII, Papacy, corruption, prophecy, Jerome of Prague, Italy</keywords>

# HIERONYMUS

...ions; meanwhile the Florentines remained devoted to *Jerome* and King *Charles* <note>Charles VIII of France (1470–1498), who invaded Italy in 1494 and was seen by Savonarola as a God-sent reformer</note>. Noting that the King’s intention was to improve and restore the rule and order of the Spiritual State <note>The Church</note>. *A laudable conception and intention was that of Charles, says Commines, but in the execution of it there lay much difficulty.* <note>Philippe de Commines was a diplomat and historian whose memoirs are a primary source for this period</note> Matters having now changed due to the King’s death, and the Florentines having fallen into heavy strife and discord among themselves, *Jerome’s* prestige and power among them diminished daily. Moreover, the Pope incited the Florentines and promised them many wonders if they took his revenge and punishment upon *Jerome*. Many already hated him, especially a certain Monk of the Franciscan order—the Dominicans generally being their rivals—who scolded *Jerome* as a heretic. Because of this, a certain disturbance and riot of the common people occurred, which the new magistrates, turning a blind eye, allowed; they seized him and inflicted great torments upon him. Finally, he was burned with two of his fellow brothers in the year <term>1498</term> <note>original: *M. CCCC. XC VIII.*</note>. At the fifty-fifth year of his age, of which *Commines* writes thus: *In morals and manner of life he was irreproachable, as far as one could observe in him; teaching and instructing, he brought many here to understanding and the improvement of life.* Likewise, the famous historian *Guicciardini* <note>Francesco Guicciardini, a major political writer of the Italian Renaissance</note> felt the same regarding *Savonarola*. From the aforementioned *Charles*, *Louis the Twelfth* received the Kingdom, and with the Kingdom, the will toward the destruction of Babylon <note>A common Protestant and reformist metaphor for the perceived corruption and spiritual exile of the Papacy in Rome</note>, which his still-extant golden Medals, with this inscription, testify: <term>LV D. XII. D. G. REX FRANC. DVX MEDIOL.</term> <note>Latin: *Ludovicus XII Dei Gratia Rex Francorum Dux Mediolani*, meaning "Louis XII, by the grace of God, King of France, Duke of Milan"</note>. The meaning of which is: *Louis XII, by God’s grace King of France, Duke of Milan*, and on the other side of the Medal <term>PERDAM BABYLONEM</term> <note>Latin: "I will destroy Babylon"</note>, which is to say: *I shall bring down Babylon.* Happy were you also for this reason, *Louis*, who found *Philippe de Commines*—a choice historian for the sake of noble truth, great understanding, and judgment—to be the proclaimer of your virtues. He [Savonarola] is also called a holy man by *Marsilio Ficino* and *Flaminio*, and was defended by *Francesco Pico* <note>Gianfrancesco Pico della Mirandola, nephew of the famous philosopher Giovanni Pico della Mirandola</note>, Count of Mirandola, through an excellent writing or booklet against the Pope. This was when the Pope was *Alexander the Sixth Borgia* <note>Infamous for his corruption and the scandals involving his family</note>. This Spaniard, out of ambition, no less than greed and shameful unchastity—a monster blown together and abandoned to vice—out of his own accord, and (as he says) out of the fullness of the Aposto- <meta>catchword: Apostolic power/authority</meta>

### FLORENTINE.

Jerome <note>The Latinized form "Hieronymus" refers to Girolamo Savonarola</note>, when the fierce fire fed upon your knees,

# ERASMUS OF ROTTERDAM
### THE DUTCHMAN <note>original: *Batavus*; a reference to the Batavi, an ancient Germanic tribe of the Rhine delta. Renaissance humanists used this term to refer to the Dutch people and to imbue their national identity with classical dignity.</note>

> You honor your homeland with your genius, and your homeland with your virtues,
> And you do not simply become a Batavian; you define what it is to be one. <note>original: *Et fieri Batavus non potes, ipse facis*; this implies that while one might be born a "Batavian," Erasmus’s greatness is what truly gave the name its prestige.</note>
> Just as fortune spreads your name, so may the divine powers preserve your countenance,
> That your remarkable faith might be known throughout the whole world.

<note>This short poem is a typical humanist tribute, emphasizing the connection between Erasmus's personal virtue and the honor he brought to the Netherlands. # ERASMUS OF ROTTERDAM, THE DUTCHMAN <note>original: *Erasmus Roterodamus Batavus*. The term "Batavian" refers to an ancient Germanic tribe of the Rhine delta; Dutch scholars used this name to link their heritage to classical antiquity.</note>

> The vast world echoes with the fame of the great Erasmus;
> This small portrait <note>original: *picta tabella*; literally "painted tablet."</note> shows you only half of him.
> But why not the whole man? Cease your wonder, reader,
> For the entire earth itself is not large enough to contain him.

<note>This four-line poem is a famous epigram often attributed to the poet Theodore Beza. It employs a common Renaissance "conceit" (a clever poetic metaphor): the idea that a person's physical likeness captures only their body, while their intellectual and spiritual influence is so vast that even the physical world is too small to hold it.</note>

<vocab>
**Erasmus of Rotterdam:** The most famous scholar of the Northern Renaissance (1466–1536), known for his wit, his new translation of the Greek New Testament, and his calls for church reform.
**Reader:** <note>original: *lector*</note>; the poem addresses the viewer directly, inviting them to move beyond the image to find the "real" Erasmus in his writings.
</vocab>

<summary>This frontispiece features a formal portrait of Desiderius Erasmus accompanied by a Latin epigram explaining that while the engraving shows his face, his true genius is too great to be captured by art or contained by the earth.</summary>

# DESIDERIUS ERASMUS
## OF ROTTERDAM.

Let **Desiderius Erasmus** of **Rotterdam** also have his place here: the fine one, I say, if indeed he has any. The Papists <note>The author uses this term to refer to the Roman Catholic Church</note> do not acknowledge him as one of their own; the Evangelicals <note>A term used during the Reformation for Protestants, particularly those who prioritized the authority of the Gospels</note> consider him partly theirs and partly a stranger. Do you ask, then, why *Erasmus* stands in this order among those who openly and with all their might continually fought the Roman *Antichrist*? This shall serve as your reason and answer:

Namely, because he was the one who exposed, rebuked, and contradicted Scholastic Theology <term>Scholastic Theology: A medieval school of thought emphasizing logic and dialectical reasoning, which Reformers often dismissed as over-complicated "sophistry"</term> as a form of *Sophistry*, or the subtle hair-splitting of the priests. He also criticized the hooded friars <note>original: *ghecapte Broerkens*; a mocking reference to monks</note> and the many vain superstitions, false beliefs, and fictional devotions of many others. He did this in many ways and to the great delight of the common people; indeed, he showed many the way by which they could uncover and discover the hiding holes, disguises, and hidden cunning of the defenders of the *Tangled Kingdom* <note>original: *Werre-Rijcx*; likely referring to the perceived worldly confusion and corruption of the Roman Church hierarchy</note>. Thus, he is regarded by the Papists as being both left- and right-handed—equally quick or ready with both—and as a result, he became the intermediary who opened the window for the Evangelicals, through which they later threw open the great wide gates of **Rome** and let in their acquaintances in great numbers.

This is exactly what a certain Spanish monk and preacher tried to convince his Spaniards of—those who held the garrison at Rotterdam (at the time when the Duke of *Alba* <note>Fernando Álvarez de Toledo, 3rd Duke of Alba, the Spanish governor-general notorious for his "Council of Troubles" and harsh suppression of the Dutch Revolt</note> practiced his tyranny in the Netherlands). These soldiers had previously honored *Erasmus's* statue, which was carved from blue stone and set up high on a certain bridge in the marketplace, a notable place. However, later moved by the shouts and warnings of that same preacher, around evening—when the Spaniards were accustomed to going on watch—these soldiers shot bravely at that statue of *Erasmus* with their muskets and firearms <note>original: *roers*; a 16th-century term for a musket or long gun</note>. Attacking and overwhelming it with a great noise, they finally pushed it down from above into the harbor. They did not wish to see any other statue of honor in the Netherlands held in higher regard than that of the aforementioned *Alba*, as monuments to Spanish pride and cruelty...

<summary>This page discusses Desiderius Erasmus's ambiguous but critical role in the Reformation, describing how his critiques of Scholasticism "opened the window" for Protestantism. It also recounts an incident where Spanish soldiers, incited by a preacher, destroyed Erasmus's statue in Rotterdam.</summary>
<keywords>Desiderius Erasmus, Rotterdam, Scholasticism, Reformation, Duke of Alba, Dutch Revolt, Iconoclasm, Spanish Garrison, Catholic-Protestant conflict</keywords>

# DESID. ERASMUS

...nor had yet exalted. But the **Magistrates** <note>The city administrators or regents of Rotterdam</note> (after the City was freed from the Duke of **Alba's** <note>Fernando Álvarez de Toledo, the Duke of Alba, was a Spanish governor-general notorious for his harsh suppression of the Dutch Revolt</note> Tyranny) have erected another **Statue** for **Erasmus**: which is truly worthy of being among the city’s foremost ornaments and magnificent buildings, made from a better material. It was raised and displayed through a different spirit of art (for the sake of his excellent intellect, learning, and all kinds of arts). For indeed the **Republic** can extend and spread her splendor and the praise obtained through **Erasmus of Rotterdam** by all people, to a greater and more long-lasting Honor and Fame than her own outgoing shipping to both the Poles, or the significant navigations through the New World that perish and press on today.

Furthermore, for the sake of his Name, **Erasmus** is as much valued and beloved by us as we see today the sharp-witted and meddlesome monster of the **Jesuits** <note>A polemical reference to the Society of Jesus, who were often the primary intellectual opponents of Erasmus's followers and the Reformers</note> (according to the hatred they bear him) everywhere cutting through and scratching out the Name and writings of this same **Erasmus**. Yet he was one of the foremost from whom, as if from an abundant fountain, **good letters** <term>Good letters: Referring to "Bonae Litterae," the humanist study of classical literature, grammar, and rhetoric</term> (now common among the learned) have strongly flowed; and these ungrateful slanderers have drawn from and sucked them out of him. 

They only intend and strive for this: that they might exalt themselves above others and cause themselves to be praised throughout all the world. Thus, they thrust upon the world **Possevino** <note>Antonio Possevino (1533–1611), a prominent Jesuit diplomat and bibliographer</note> and other boasters and all-knowers in place of the aforementioned **Erasmus**, hoping by that means to snatch from the hands of the lovers of his spirit an countless heap of books. They especially target his **Corrections**, **Revisions**, and **Scholia** <term>Scholia: Explanatory notes or commentaries written in the margins of ancient texts</term> or interpretations, which he produced through an incredible labor—greater even than that of **Hercules**—concerning the entire works of **Jerome**, **Augustine**, **Ambrose**, **Cyprian**, **Hilary**, **Irenaeus**, and many other Latin **Fathers**: as well as in the remaining works of **Origen**. 

They do this so that they may afterwards put their own **Corrections** and **Censures** <note>Refers to the "Index Librorum Prohibitorum" and the "Index Expurgatorius," which censored or banned books</note> or judgments into the hands of the inexperienced buyer and make them more common. For we see how they defile with dirty hands and contaminate with their fierce and sharp nails—scratching and tearing—the writings of the holy **Ancient Fathers**. And this they do under the cover of being armed and provided with the Pope’s **Bull-Privileges** <note>Official decrees issued by the Pope, often granting exclusive rights or authority</note>, thus presuming over these Godly and Venerable **Fathers** everything they boldly and shamelessly desire and dare to undertake. These are the Jesuits, who, as the community well...

# FROM ROTTERDAM.

...[counter]feit, <note>The text begins with a fragment of the word "conterfeyt," meaning portrait or depiction, continuing from the previous page</note> judging everyone, yet wanting to be judged by none themselves: those who take their name from the Name of **JESUS** <note>A critical reference to the Society of Jesus, or Jesuits</note>, considering themselves holier and also wiser than the true Christians. Even the very learned man **Benedict Arias Montanus** <note>A Spanish orientalist and editor of the Antwerp Polyglot Bible</note> gave such a judgment, not without sadness, regarding these same Jesuits. *Oh, if only you, great **Desiderius Erasmus of Rotterdam**, had been a steadfast and certain companion of the **Evangelicals** <note>The early Protestant Reformers</note>, and your wavering faith had not been noticed by both your friends and your enemies!* But you, looking as if to your own Name—which is said to mean Desire and Love—wanted to be **Desiderius**, which is "the desirable one," and the other, **Erasmus**, which is "the lovely one." From this it followed that no one could say you were whole or true: at times this person, at times that person called you an enemy, and thus they made quite a mockery of you in matters of **Religion** (which is nothing to be mocked). Likewise, the sharp-witted **Epigram** <note>A short, pithy poem</note> or inscription by **Theodore Beza** <note>A major French Protestant theologian and successor to John Calvin</note>, standing here before your image—*Great [monument] for a Great [man]* <note>original: "Ingens Ingentem"</note> etc.—expresses his judgment or feeling. The learned will please wait for the life of this great man, through the favor of **Otto Werckman of Nijmegen**, an intelligent man and our good friend: who intends very shortly to bring to light the *Life of Erasmus*, never before published in print, written in **Erasmus’s** own hand, which he has preserved like a costly jewel.

<column-break/>

The books of **DESIDERIUS ERASMUS** were all printed in **Basel** by **Hieronymus Froben** and **Nicolaus Episcopius**. In the year 1540, in **folio** <note>A large format of book where sheets are folded only once</note>, and in **nine volumes** or parts.

I. The **First Volume** or part contains within it all that pertains to the understanding of the **Latin** and **Greek** languages: various translations of Greek Authors, Orators, Poets, Tragedians, Dialogists, and Philologists: some **Morals**, **Parables**, **Colloquies** <note>Conversational dialogues used to teach language and ethics</note>, **Declamations**, **Epigrams**, and many other such things.

II. *The **Adages** <note>A collection of Greek and Latin proverbs</note> or common sayings, arranged in **Thousands** <note>original: "Chiliades"</note>. This is a wide field here, filled with all kinds of choice flowers of learning, all pleasantries, all elegance, or loveliness itself, delights, enjoyments, desires, jests, proverbs, tales, and insights: in short, so that the proverbs may be thoroughly understood according to their nature, it is an Athenian song, a fountain flowing with every kind of delight and understanding.*

III. *Containing all the common **letters** that the Author ever published, or intended to be published:*

has: to which are added the prefaces that he wrote or noted for all kinds of writers.

IX. *Contains the defenses against those who, regarding certain passages in his books, have slandered him without enough respect-*

<column-break/>

-fully, or with deceit, as the inscription or title-page describes.

There are many defenses in this ninth volume which (had Erasmus refrained from writing them) would have caused the man’s name to be held in even higher esteem.

If anyone were now to wish to review and read with care the authors, both spiritual and secular, whom Erasmus has corrected—being nearly countless—they would not be able to look upon his learning, care, vigilance, immense labor, and the excellence of his spirit without wonder and great notice.

As for the Censors, correctors, or examiners of his books; these are those who, with the pointers of their corrections, improvements, or purgings, pitifully haul over, plague, and martyr Erasmus: and although we see many errors and quite noticeable deletions in *Erasmus’s* writings through their painstaking scrutiny and crossing-out, there are nonetheless many that they have blindly passed over, as not a few people are able to notice.

The *Indices* <note>The "Index Librorum Prohibitorum" or list of prohibited books, which sought to censor "heretical" passages</note> or guides to the purging of these fine books are two, of which one was issued in the Netherlands in the year [15]71 by the order and authority of the Catholic King <note>King Philip II of Spain</note>, as also

OF ROTTERDAM.
E ij
<meta>catchword: Very</meta>

—also according to the Council and command of the Duke of *Alba* <note>Fernando Álvarez de Toledo, the 3rd Duke of Alba, was the Spanish governor of the Netherlands known for his iron-fisted rule</note>, as the title states, printed and joined together.

The other was published by order of *Gaspar Quiroga*, Cardinal and Archbishop of Toledo, and General of the Spanish Inquisition; it was issued by command of the Supreme Council of the same holy and universal Inquisition in Madrid, Spain, in the year 1574. These were brought to light this past year through the vigilance and diligence of the Lord of *Plessis* <note>Philippe de Mornay, Seigneur du Plessis-Marly (1549–1623), a prominent French Protestant leader and writer</note>, the stern resister and fighter of the Antichrist in France.

And although many things in these *Indices* <note>original: "Indices"; refers to the *Index Librorum Prohibitorum*, the list of books banned by the Catholic Church</note> or guides are indicated as evil or contemptible, nevertheless not all those who dispute or disagree with the Pope’s opinion are expressed therein, as we can clearly perceive.

<column-break/>

I believe that these many-eyed *Argus* <note>original: "Argos"; in Greek mythology, a giant with a hundred eyes, here used to describe the watchful censors of the Inquisition</note> and guardians of the monster of the Inquisition also saw those things that were left out; but they passed over them intentionally or willingly, so that they should not appear to have entirely rejected so great a man as *Erasmus*, whom they (as mentioned) count somewhat as one of their own.

The Jesuits today, being the most cunning breed of monks, who claim all glory for themselves, strive to scratch and pluck *Erasmus* out of human memory as being more offensive and harmful than *Luther* and *Calvin*. These sophists and hypocrites have their Jesuitical reasons for this. Therefore, the more they say that *Erasmus* is rejectable, the more earnestly we should insist that he must be read.

**How great the praise of the great Erasmus is throughout the world,**
**His portrait’s half-length figure <note>original: "Groot-half"; likely referring to a bust or a half-length portrait</note> here clearly shows you.**
**Ask not then why he stands here incomplete?**
**The earthly globe was too small to contain such a man entirely.**

->**Desiderius Erasmus of Rotterdam.**<-

> *Very great was the name of this Erasmus to behold:*
> *But why is he called Great, who has such a small body?*

> *Make him so very great, and give him an eternal name.*

### Concerning Erasmus gnawing at the Mendicant Friars. <note>original: "Bedel-broeders"; religious orders such as the Franciscans or Dominicans who relied on alms</note>

Those who look upon the body say that the small **Erasmus**,
Humble and lowly of stature, is but a little man <note>original: "Mantken"; a diminutive of man</note>: but
The Friars Minor <term>Friars Minor: The Franciscans, a Catholic religious order known for their vow of poverty; from the Latin *Fratres Minores*</term> cry out that you were a great **Mouse**.

# Martin Luther

<meta>This page continues the discussion of influential figures of the Reformation, shifting focus from Erasmus of Rotterdam to Martin Luther. The previous page ended with witty epigrams about Erasmus’s small stature but great intellect; this page introduces Luther as the primary catalyst of the Reformation, emphasizing his early opposition to the sale of indulgences.</meta>

# MARTIN
## LUTHER.

Who shall be able to worthily enough praise or fully honor you, great **Luther** (who at first preached the Gospel with such steadfastness)? You who—when the Christian, or rather the Roman World, lay smothered and sunken in the deepest and thickest darkness—then illuminated the same through the torch of the Gospel, and ignited the hearts that were nearly perished in love and had grown entirely cold. You have truly revealed the Roman Idolatry to the Christian world with greater joy and exceptional courage than those who, near that same time, were looking upon the New World from *Spain* <note>A reference to the discovery of the Americas (the "New World") by Spanish expeditions, suggesting Luther’s spiritual discovery was even more significant.</note>. 

God awakened this man when, in His great mercy, He intended to use this chosen Doctor or teacher to spread the Gospel of His son Christ: exactly at the time when the Roman *Antichrist* <note>A common Protestant designation for the Pope during the Reformation era.</note> was most furious and exercising the extreme tyranny over the Church of God. Also at a time when the art of money-making, gold, and silver were most highly esteemed and in vogue, but Christ’s blood (compared to which nothing is of equal value) was disregarded and held as a slight thing; when the *Dispensations* <term>Dispensations: In the Catholic Church, these are official exemptions from a rule or a vow, often granted by the Pope.</term> and the Year-markets of Indulgences <term>Indulgences: Documents sold by the Church that were believed to reduce the temporal punishment for sins; their sale was a primary trigger for the Reformation.</term> forgave all sins and villainies, or allowed them to go unpunished, and Heaven (through the Pope’s full power, so they say) was promised and firmly pledged to those who gave the most money for it. 

*Luther*, then practicing the office of Preacher in Saxony, undertook the first disputation concerning Indulgences (which were being fully preached and announced everywhere with such diligence and labor) quite quietly and modestly, as one who still doubts and wishes to probe and know the truth more closely. His *Theses* <note>The 95 Theses, famously posted in 1517.</note> or articles against the Indulgences and their salesman or market-peddler *Tetzel* <note>original: "Teccelium"; referring to Johann Tetzel, a Dominican friar whose preaching on indulgences provoked Luther.</note>, were accepted with great pleasure by many learned men devoted to the Church as well as the Fatherland. Among these was the aforementioned *Erasmus* of Rotterdam himself, who at that time (oh, if only he had always done so!) greeted *Luther* through his letters and strengthened him in this; as did *Albert Krantz* <note>A German historian and theologian.</note> (who the Popes'

<summary>This page introduces Martin Luther as a divinely appointed figure who challenged the Roman Catholic Church's "darkness," specifically critiquing the sale of indulgences and the corruption of the Papacy. It notes that his early efforts were supported by other scholars like Erasmus and Albert Krantz.</summary>

<keywords>Martin Luther, Reformation, Indulgences, Johann Tetzel, Erasmus of Rotterdam, Saxony, Roman Catholic Church, Papacy, Albert Krantz</keywords>

# MARTIN
## LUTHER.

<meta>The text begins mid-sentence, continuing a thought from the previous page regarding those who recognized the merit in the Reformation's early stages.</meta>

...recorded [Erasmus’s] constant praise and practices in his fine histories) also accepted them as good: yet nevertheless worrying for **Luther** <note>original: "Lutero"</note> because of the power and arch-cunning of the Pope. And Doctor **Staupitz** <note>original: "Stupicius"; Johann von Staupitz was Luther's superior in the Augustinian order.</note> who (at the urging and advice of the Elector of Saxony <note>Frederick the Wise.</note>, as well as the entire Augustinian Order and the Academy) had made **Luther** <note>original: "Luterum"</note> a Doctor, said laughingly to **Luther** (who did not initially accept such an honor): *“It already appears as if God intends to undertake wonderful things and dealings in Heaven and earth, wherein He seems to want to use the labor of learned men; therefore God shall also put your service to work.”* And the Emperor **Maximilian** <note>Maximilian I, Holy Roman Emperor.</note> when he had read **Luther’s** <note>original: "Luteri"</note> Disputations against this money-hunting mule-driver <note>original: "Muylftooter"; a derogatory term for a crude or stubborn person.</note> and indulgence-seller **Tetzel** <note>original: "Teccelium"; Johann Tetzel, whose sale of indulgences prompted Luther's 95 Theses.</note>, he charged Doctor **Pfeffinger** (the legate of the Duke of Saxony) to deliver this command: That one should take this Monk into safe-keeping, for they would before long have need of him against the injuries and the bold behavior of the Romans. 

But **Luther** daily ascended to greater things: when he had blown away and destroyed those indulgences and Bulls—like eye-pleasing and quickly-vanishing bubbles—through his exceedingly great spirit, he then approached the "Bulle-man" <note>A pun on 'bulleman' (bogeyman) and the Papal 'Bulls' (official edicts).</note> of these indulgences and the Father of all trickery, the Pope himself. He fearlessly attacked the triple-crowned head, and expressed, depicted, and described both with tongue and pen the ugly, godless, and horrific barking and powerless blowing of this **raging Cerberus of the Capitoline** <note>original: "Campidolifchen wutenden Cerberus"; Cerberus is the three-headed dog of Hades, here used to mock the Pope's triple crown and his residence in Rome (the Capitoline hill).</note>. Calling him now the **Antichrist**, and then the **Babylonian Dragon**, so that through his thundering voice and powerful writings, Rome and all Italy became stirred and terrified, and the whole world wondered at **Luther's** teachings and his fortunate growth and increase: so much so that his name and fame reached the ends of the earth. 

Oh, the exceptional steadfastness and courage of the great **Luther**, where you (as you traveled to the Diet <term>Diet: A formal deliberative assembly of the princes of the Holy Roman Empire.</term> at **Worms**, even though your friends warned you and your enemies laid cunning snares for you) did not let the footsteps of **Hus** <note>original: "Huffi"; Jan Hus, a Czech reformer who was burned at the stake for heresy in 1415.</note> frighten you away or turn you back. And when you departed from the assembly of **Schmalkalden** <note>original: "Smalcald"; the Schmalkaldic League was a defensive alliance of Lutheran princes.</note>, and took leave of your beloved ones, you used these very words: *“God fill you with His blessing, and with hatred against the Pope,”* and to others wishing them all happiness: *“That they, above all, should learn to know the Pope.”* And if God granted that the same...

--- Page 71 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Martin Luther, moving from his early recognition by figures like Erasmus and Emperor Maximilian to his prophetic warnings about internal strife within the Reformation and the political fate of the Netherlands under Spanish rule.</meta>

-># MARTIN<-
->## LUTHER<-

...the same cunning, fox-like nature and evil deeds, which those who today vainly boast of your name, O Luther, did not see or notice. This man of God foresaw many things which we know have either happened or are currently coming to pass; among others, he said this to ***Dr. Schnepf*** <note>original: "D. Schnepfium"; Erhard Schnepf (1495–1558), a German theologian and reformer.</note>: *“After my death, few among our theologians will remain steadfast; and what the Turk <note>The Ottoman Empire, often viewed as a divine scourge in 16th-century thought.</note> and the Pope do not accomplish, we must expect from our own people.”* And truly, godly and prophetic Luther, this has followed accordingly. For we now see many fighting for **Luther**, and yet (under such a cover) actively fighting against him, overturning—yes, almost uprooting—the most important foundations laid by him. This is caused by a bitter hatred they hold against those who support the **Reformed Religion** and the true **Confession of Augsburg** <note>Presented in 1530, this is the primary confession of faith for the Lutheran Church.</note> and who speak against the **Ubiquity** <term>original: "Over-al-wefen"; the theological doctrine that Christ’s body is present everywhere, particularly in the bread and wine of the Lord's Supper.</term> of Christ’s body. This point is regarded as idolatrous; it was first brought into being by ***Brenz*** <note>Johannes Brenz (1499–1570), a Lutheran reformer who developed the doctrine of Ubiquity.</note> and was filed and polished by ***Andreae*** <note>original: "Smedelino"; Jakob Andreae (1528–1590), a primary author of the Formula of Concord. The Dutch text calls him a "clever smith," a pun on his Latinized name.</note> like a clever smith, while the Papists meanwhile laugh and hope for their own advantage against them. Luther also said: *“If our Emperor Charles <note>Holy Roman Emperor Charles V.</note> undertakes anything detrimental against the Gospel, it is certain that he shall lose his entire reign in the Netherlands.”* 

In this, he was not far from the truth, for his son ***Philip*** <note>Philip II of Spain.</note> has not only moved against the Religion, but also—following the **Spanish Council** and its practices—has sought with all might and force of arms to root out and destroy the entire Netherlands. Because of this, the same Netherlands (after they had accommodated him in all things and were oppressed by unbearable burdens and violence) were at last forced to set themselves against this tyrant over the body. He is one who, under the cover of Religion, rose up through his bottomless desire for domination and sought to destroy and bring to nothing the country’s **Privileges and Rights** <note>The traditional laws and liberties of the Dutch provinces that limited the monarch's power.</note>. According to the courageous nature of their pious ancestors, the people stood for and maintained the freedom of the Fatherland with equal power, and accordingly declared him an enemy of the **Republic**. With this Spanish nation, the **United Netherlands** have now been waging war for many years—a war that grows more steadfast and harder by the day—for the good of themselves and all Christian Princes, indeed, the whole world. For if these Netherlands were brought low, then that would come to pass which they may now rightly foresee and sense.

<column-break/>

F
<meta>catchword: LVTERVS</meta>

<summary>This page recounts Luther’s prophecies regarding the internal division of the Reformation and his warning that Emperor Charles V would lose the Netherlands if he opposed the Gospel. It links these religious tensions to the ongoing Dutch Revolt against Philip II of Spain.</summary>

-># MARTIN<-
->## I<-

<meta>catchword: Lou-</meta>

Luther wrote a great deal in the Latin and High German languages, in various places and at different times; these were first printed individually, and thereafter brought together and published in several *Tomos* <term>original: "Tomos"; large, multi-volume sets of collected works.</term> or great volumes. Beyond these, an *Index* <note>original: "Elenchus"; a systematic list or catalogue.</note> of all **Luther's** books was published by *Sigismund Suevus*, in which it is indicated in which volume and page of each printing his writings—both German and Latin—may be found.

We shall not recount all of **Luther's** writings here (for the sake of brevity), as they are many: but we shall only list those which were printed specifically against the Roman **Antichrist** <note>In Reformation polemics, the office of the Pope was frequently identified as the "Antichrist" described in the New Testament.</note> and his co-helpers, following the order of time.

*Propositions on Penance and Indulgences.* <note>These are the famous 95 Theses of 1517 which sparked the Reformation.</note>

*Written by Johannes Annius of Viterbo concerning the Pope's Monarchy or Imperial Disputation in the Year 1481, with the preface by Luther, and the Letter to the Reader against Jacob van Hoogstraten.* <note>Jacob van Hoogstraten (c. 1460–1527) was a Dominican inquisitor and a leading opponent of the Reformers.</note>

*A letter to Sylvester Cajetan and others.* <note>Cardinal Cajetan was the papal legate who famously interrogated Luther at Augsburg in 1518.</note>

*Luther's Notes against the scribblings of Eck.* <note>Johann Eck (1486–1543), Luther's primary theological rival, famously debated him at Leipzig.</note>

*Against the Scholastics.* <term>The "Scholastics" were medieval theologians who used Aristotelian logic to defend church doctrine; Luther frequently attacked their "man-made" reason.</term>

*Defense against Eck's false accusations.*

<column-break/>

*A letter to J. Eck, regarding the purification of Eck.*

*On the Babylonian Captivity of the Church.* <note>One of Luther's most important 1520 treatises, in which he attacked the sacramental system of the medieval church.</note>

*Letters to those in Bremen, to the brothers in Holland, Brabant, and Flanders: to Charles V, the Duke of Saxony, and many others.*

*Confirmation of Luther's Articles rejected by the last Bull of Leo the Tenth.* <note>Referring to the papal bull "Exsurge Domine" (1520) which threatened Luther with excommunication.</note>

*The Answer to the articles which "our masters" of Leuven and those of Cologne condemned as heretical.*

*Against the Bull of the Antichrist.*

*The books of the Roman Pope and his disciples: why they were burned by Luther.* <note>In 1520, Luther publicly burned the Papal Bull and the volumes of Canon Law in Wittenberg.</note>

*Second appeal from the Pope to the Council.*

*The articles and errors of the books of Canon Law.*

*The appeal from the Pope to the Council.*

*A ready answer to the articles which "our masters" had gathered from the Babylonian [Captivity] and their confirmations.*

*Answer to the book of Ambrosius Catharinus, with the explanation of the vision of Daniel chapter 8 concerning the Antichrist.*

### ij 7

<meta>catchword: Nie-</meta>

The entire Bible translated from the Hebrew language into High German: through which translation he has been of great service to the church.

There are many other writings that have appeared after his death; among which is a large book of his **Table Talk** <note>original: "Diſch-redenen"; these were informal, often earthy conversations recorded by Luther's students at the dinner table</note>, in High German in folio format.

It would have been better if this **Table Talk** had never been brought to light; I say this with the agreement of those who are too much taken by their love for *Luther*, which possibly even his enemies have encouraged <note>The author suggests that Luther's critics published these informal, sometimes crude remarks to damage his reputation</note>. If not; let those who exalt themselves so highly in the Lutheran name consider how they can defend the integrity, the judgment, the esteem, and the dignity of *Luther*,

<column-break/>

because there are in the same, I say not many, but very many things which godly and disciplined ears would not permit or allow to be said, not only at the meals of preachers and theologians, but even in farces and stage plays <note>original: "battament en Camer-ſpelen"; the author refers to the popular, often ribald theatrical performances put on by the Chambers of Rhetoric</note>. Whoever thinks that this **Table Talk** should be accepted as if it had come from the brain of Jupiter <note>A mythological reference to the goddess Minerva being born fully formed and perfect from the head of Jupiter; the author warns against treating Luther's casual remarks as divine revelation</note>; we shall answer them with what a certain select man wrote to a highly esteemed theologian, namely: those who love *Luther* so much that they almost think one should worship *Luther’s* slippers. Let those go, then, who fight for the "great Luther" against Luther's own interests, to the great disadvantage of Christendom.

> Rome tames the world, the Pope makes Rome his subject /

> His pen shows him and her: which tames them in every place.

<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of Martin Luther's early struggles against the indulgence system to the introduction of his primary collaborator, Philipp Melanchthon. The text emphasizes their contrasting temperaments and how their partnership shaped the Reformation.</meta>

# PHILIP
## MELANCHTHON.

It is very fitting that **Philipp Melanchthon** here joins **Luther**; he took the Gospel Torch kindled by *Luther* and provided its fuel. By orderly arranging its substance and nourishment, he caused it to shine more clearly and rise into higher flames. *Luther* could not have had a more faithful or steadfast companion for spreading the light of the Gospel. *Luther* was vehement, *Melanchthon* was mild-mannered; *Luther* was courageous, *Melanchthon* was timid <note>original: "bloodich"; in this context, it refers to a shy or hesitant disposition rather than being "bloody"</note>. The former excelled in voice and speech, the latter in writing and reasoning; the former was powerful in preaching, the latter in teaching within the Schools. The former despised his opponents and the Sophists <note>The "Sophists" refers to the Scholastic theologians of the medieval tradition whom the Reformers accused of using circular logic</note>, while the latter disputed with them for the sake of honor and respect. The former was content with Theology as a master; the latter desired to see Theology adorned by Philosophy (as its handmaid <note>A reference to the Latin concept "ancilla theologiae," where philosophy serves to clarify divine truths</note>). *Luther* was somewhat more severe, while *Melanchthon* was more moderate in all things. Therefore, he earned great praise from all who were zealous in matters of Religion.

Now, as far as the learning of *Melanchthon* is concerned, it was so vast that, beyond his knowledge of divine things, he was also highly experienced in the *Encyclopedia* <term>Encyclopedia: here meaning the "circle of knowledge" or the complete system of liberal arts and sciences</term> or the deep complexities of Philosophy. These subjects were brought to light and taught by him with great fruit. Through the help and advancement of these studies, he explained and interpreted Theology so clearly that those who were learning could very easily understand and grasp it; thus, those who devoted themselves to Theological study were able to aptly explain its obscurities when teaching in public.

He was the first to teach by what means Theology could be joined with Philosophy (to which he seems, in some matters, to have attributed too much); he also pointed out and described a certain path and order that true Students of Theology should maintain and follow in their presentations, writings, and disputations. Through these two great men, you, **Wittenberg**—I hardly know whether to call you a small town—acquired a greater name and reputation at that time than ever before.

<summary>This page introduces Philipp Melanchthon as the intellectual and moderate partner to Martin Luther, highlighting his role in organizing Reformation theology and integrating it with classical philosophy at the University of Wittenberg.</summary>
<keywords>Philipp Melanchthon, Martin Luther, Wittenberg, Scholasticism, Reformation education, Philosophy and Theology, Gospel Torch, moderation</keywords>

--- Page 78 ---
<meta>This page continues the praise of Melanchthon’s educational influence, comparing his "school" at Wittenberg to the prestige of ancient Rome, and details his diplomatic efforts and the theological conflicts that followed Luther's death.</meta>

# PHILIP

...knowledge, as the ruler and Queen of all cities, Rome, has been able to boast. Such a gathering and residency of students; such a multitude of church ministers and schoolmasters have come forth from the single school of *Melanchthon*, that the great and wide Germany, and the surrounding kingdoms, have had learned and godly preachers and schoolmasters everywhere in their midst through the benefits and advancement of *Melanchthon*. So much so that he is not unjustly called the father of preachers and teachers. Among foreigners, and even the enemies of the Religion, he was gladly heard (on account of his singular learning, moderation, and skill in all things). By his great reputation and fame, *Francis I*, King of France (whose honor and name shall remain in eternal memory among learned men), was moved to request *Melanchthon* through letters and his Envoy, so that he might use his advice and counsel in unifying and moderating religious matters. So great was the kindness of this man’s spirit that, even when dealing with his enemies, he received praise from the Papists <note>A term used by Reformers to refer to Roman Catholics loyal to the Pope</note>, even when he publicly disputed with them regarding Religion. Thus, not a few of *Melanchthon’s* adversaries judged him more fairly than some of his own disciples, who already stood ungrateful against their Master as Doctors, *Aristarchs* <note>original: "Aristarchen"; a reference to Aristarchus of Samothrace, used here to mean severe or nitpicking critics</note>, or heel-treaders <note>original: "hiel-treders"; those who follow so closely they trip up or dog the footsteps of their teacher</note>. With what study and labor he endeavored to remove, or rather to extinguish, the dispute—or more truly the war—that arose over the matter of the Lord’s Supper <note>The "Nachtmaal"; a central point of contention between Lutherans and Calvinists regarding the physical presence of Christ in the bread and wine</note>, his writings and letters sufficiently testify. These are contained in the Book of Epistles of *Calvin* the theologian (with whom he lived in friendship), though all too much in vain, since *Luther*, full of his own opinion, seemed unwilling to tolerate anyone above him. Now that *Luther* has passed away, a heavy war has arisen on the point of the Lord’s Supper, but after *Melanchthon’s* death, it became the heaviest of all. Other weapons were sought, new and never before used, to advocate for and maintain *Ubiquity* <term>Ubiquity: the Lutheran doctrine that Christ's human body is omnipresent, or "everywhere," even in the bread and wine</term>, or "being everywhere," which is nowhere but in heaven. They present this so ostentatiously that they feel it must be worshipped in a certain way, quite following the example of the Papists. Oh, if only that war had never arisen, or having arisen, had never been conducted with such concord or "unity" (which is the mother of all discord or division)! Then the Pope, who more truly "Everywhere"...

<meta>catchword: fine</meta>

<summary>This page describes Melanchthon’s immense influence as an educator across Europe and his role as a moderate diplomat who even gained the respect of King Francis I and Catholic opponents. It also laments the bitter theological "wars" over the Lord’s Supper and the doctrine of Ubiquity that intensified after his and Luther's deaths.</summary>

<keywords>Philipp Melanchthon, Wittenberg, Francis I of France, John Calvin, Martin Luther, Lord's Supper, Ubiquity, religious diplomacy, Protestant Reformation</keywords>

->MELANCHTHON.<-
G
<meta>catchword: Een</meta>

...[he] has long since employed fine weapons and artful deeds against us, which (had God so willed) would have been betrayed and cast down from the cliff of his pride, to the great benefit and profit of all Christendom. But *Luther’s* teaching must live, which the false *Lutherans* <note>Referring to the Gnesio-Lutherans, a strict faction that opposed Melanchthon’s more conciliatory approach</note> (through the hatred and envy they bear toward the name of the *Reformed*) undermine and weaken. And likewise, *Melanchthon’s* moderation must have a place among those who screech and shout more with words than rouse the people with true love and reasonableness: so that we may finally and with joined hands lay hold of the *Antichrist* (against whom *Luther* fought so bravely and steadfastly) and see him brought down through God’s power.

Melanchthon wrote books without end: printed in many places, yet his writings were published together in the year 1541, printed by *Herwagen* <note>Johann Herwagen, a prominent printer in Basel</note> in Basel, and in five volumes <note>original: "Tomen"</note> in the year 1546.

Oh, if only the former disciples of Philippus Melanchthon judged their Master with more respectful love, and judged his writings more justly: or if those who seem to love him did not at least defile his worthy writings; then they would show themselves to be grateful disciples, faithful theologians of the Church who love peace, and true opponents of the Pope's teaching, and would also be found to be so in their deeds.

# JOHANNES BUGENHAGEN OF POMERANIA <note>original: "IOANNES BVGENHAGIVS POMER:"</note>

->*Your white hair and sincere faith, Doctor of Pomerania,*<-
->*Are as much to be honored by all the devout,*<-
->*As you, a faithful witness, scattered far in foreign lands*<-
->*The pure seeds of the Gospel with your voice.*<-
<note>This Latin verse praises Bugenhagen (1485–1558) for both his character and his missionary-like work. Known as "Doctor Pomeranus" after his birthplace, he was a primary architect of the Reformation, famous for drafting new church orders not only for German cities but also for the kingdoms of Denmark and Norway. The "white hair" (canities) symbolizes the wisdom of his old age.</note>

<meta>This page transitions from the poetic tribute to Philip Melanchthon on the previous page to a biographical account of Johannes Bugenhagen. It maintains the theme of the "Wittenberg Triumvirate" (Luther, Melanchthon, and Bugenhagen), shifting the focus from the school to the organizational and pastoral leadership of the Church.</meta>

# JOHANNES BUGENHAGEN.

Just as **Luther** was joined in his school by **Melanchthon**, so too did he have **Bugenhagen** beside him in the Church. This outstanding "organ pipe of God" made the wonderful unity of what Christ—our source and master of Salvation—taught us, resound and be heard. If *Luther* was like a loud-sounding Gospel Trumpet, he was followed by *Melanchthon* and *Bugenhagen* with a very sweet and lovely, flexible quality of voice, harmonizing those sounds; through their lovely resonance, they were able to draw in and delight many sensitive ears. These men, acting as the organs or instruments of God’s Church, spread and poured out the sound of the heavenly Gospel through all lands.

In *Bugenhagen* there was a singular kindness and modesty of nature, which the expression of his being seems to indicate clearly enough; the character of his spirit was estranged from all worldly ambition. For many years, he stood at his post and calling—preaching, keeping watch, and guarding the Wittenberg flock—as a faithful shepherd. Denmark also felt and enjoyed the benefit of this Doctor's exceptionally faithful works in laying the foundation of the Christian Religion, under the leadership of the best of Kings, *Christian* III. As befits a Christian King, he caused the Kingdom of Christ to be preached and promoted with all his might throughout his kingdoms and duchies, diligently persevering in this task. He also restored and renewed the Academy (or university) of Copenhagen—originally founded by King Frederick, his paternal grandfather, but which had since fallen into decay—and applied and funded everything that was in any way possible for the blessed building up of the Evangelical schools. 

Therefore, Denmark, and in its midst noble Copenhagen, holds this *Pomeranian* <note>original: "Pomeranum." Johannes Bugenhagen was commonly known as "Doctor Pomeranus" because he was born in Wollin, in the Duchy of Pomerania.</note> as their own, always honoring and remembering him as one whose scholarly labor and fatherly instructions they greatly loved and followed. Above all, this *Pomeranian* earned himself a name through his commentary on the Book of *Psalms*, which he...

<summary>This page describes Johannes Bugenhagen’s role as a "faithful shepherd" and musical "organ pipe" alongside Luther and Melanchthon, highlighting his instrumental role in establishing the Reformation and restoring the University in Denmark under King Christian III.</summary>

<keywords>Johannes Bugenhagen, Martin Luther, Philipp Melanchthon, Wittenberg, Denmark, Christian III, Copenhagen, University of Copenhagen, Pomerania, Psalms commentary, Reformation history</keywords>

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Johannes Bugenhagen, focusing on his written contributions to the Reformation. -> # JOHANNES <-

...first in German, and afterwards in Latin, he published: and having revised the same several times, he brought it to light with great satisfaction at that time. Certain and firm teachings and consolations are taken from the Prophetic and Apostolic Scriptures here, which have so wonderfully strengthened and uplifted the simple and downcast <note>In this context, "downcast" refers to those suffering from spiritual anxiety or "trials of the soul," a common theme in Reformation pastoral care.</note> Christian hearts. Likewise, his labor and diligence served to greatly edify and refresh their souls (especially in the initial building-up of those Churches). We still have that exquisite judgment of Luther, which he gave regarding this explanation of the Psalms by *Bugenhagen*: which one may read in the first edition, dedicated to *Frederick*, Duke of Saxony. I consider it not out of place to set it down here to the praise of the same *Bugenhagen*. These are Luther's own words: *“For so that one may compare the commentaries or explanations of both ancient and new writers—and although it is not truly pleasant to judge anyone—yet I dare say of no one whose books still remain and who has explained the Psalms of David: among whom this Pomeranus <note>Pomeranus is a nickname for Bugenhagen, referring to his birthplace in Pomerania.</note> is worthy to be called and said to be the first true interpreter <note>original: "uyt-legger"</note>. Almost all others have held and gathered so many of their own opinions, and those mostly uncertain, regarding this exquisite book. Here truly shall the certain judgment of the Spirit teach wonders.”* This is a great praise, which, being given by such a great and esteemed man, can also carry more weight.

### JOHANNES BUGENHAGEN.
#### THE POMERANIAN. <note>original: "Pomeranus." Bugenhagen was frequently identified by his region of origin, Pomerania, to distinguish him from other reformers named Johannes.</note>

O grey-haired Pomeranian, your Faith
Has been so steadfast/ that the pious must honor you:
In that you have far and faithfully spread God’s mighty word/
Pouring out the Seed of life through the pure teachings of your mouth.

*God’s will makes Luther and Pomeranus Friends:*
*One preaches God’s Command, which the other thunders out.*
*The Pope recoils and strikes with lightning, Melanchthon comes to join them.*
*Wittenberg triumphs, the Seven Hills <note>original: "Sevenberch." This refers to the Seven Hills of Rome, a common rhetorical shorthand for the Papacy.</note> perish and go under.*

-># JUSTUS JONAS <-
<note>Justus Jonas was a German reformer and lawyer. His name "Justus" is a Latinized form of his original name, Jodocus (Jobst) Koch.</note>

> *Law, Wisdom, eloquence, JONAS, and the Muse besides,* <note>original: "Ius, Sophia, eloquium... Musa." These terms highlight Jonas's multi-faceted career as a jurist (Law), a theologian (Wisdom), an orator (eloquence), and a poet/translator (the Muse).</note>

-> H ij <-
<meta>Catchword: and</meta>

# JUSTUS JONAS.

Rightly has LUTHER said that JUSTUS JONAS was his own, as he was one who never wavered from him in the building up of the Wittenberg Church; he was also the one who later translated several of Luther’s commentaries and treatises into Latin. Having studied Law and made great progress in it, he held the pursuit and knowledge of divine matters in much higher esteem than anything human wisdom had discovered or brought together. Yet, his learning and experience in the Law were not useless to him, as was often seen in the office and the duty laid upon him. For when handling Church matters in Synods, assemblies, and gatherings of Princes, he brought them to a good conclusion much more skillfully and successfully than others, thanks to the advantage and help of those legal studies. And through the experience in many things that he had acquired in handling religious matters, he was very beneficial to the Church, so that one could hardly say what the labor of such a man—who heard and saw almost all matters occurring in the Reformation—can achieve and bring about in difficult and obscure ecclesiastical affairs.

Justus showed himself to be an excellent advocate and defender of the lawful marriage of Priests against Johannes Faber, the defender and patron of all fornicators. <note>original: "hoer-jageren" – a derogatory term used by Reformers to imply that those defending clerical celibacy were actually protecting immorality.</note> This Faber, as well as Eck, is the subject of Erasmus’s common joke which is still remembered: *The poor and lowly Luther makes many rich.* <note>Erasmus was poking fun at Catholic apologists like Johannes Eck and Johannes Faber, who received significant financial rewards and patronage from Princes for writing against Luther’s "lowly" movement.</note> For these men sought and also received a magnificent gift from the Princes for their writing and refuting of Luther.

This Justus, being an eyewitness and listener to the things that happened in Germany regarding religious matters and being present for them, also saw and heard the final words that Luther gave and spoke to his friends in Eisleben, his birthplace, when he—as a Prince and strong champion of Christ—prepared to exchange this life for death. Thus, this Justus is a certain and principal witness of the holy labor and faithful care that Luther exercised through all his life...

<summary>This page introduces Justus Jonas, highlighting his transition from legal scholar to a pillar of the Wittenberg Reformation and his role as a defender of clerical marriage. It emphasizes his skill in diplomatic and legal ecclesiastical matters and notes his presence at Martin Luther's deathbed.</summary>
<keywords>Justus Jonas, Martin Luther, Wittenberg, Reformation history, Johannes Faber, Johannes Eck, Erasmus, Eisleben, clerical marriage, Synods</keywords>

-> JUSTUS JONAS. <-
-> CAS- <-

and also brought about in his death and carried over the Church of God. Regarding this, our Jonas (as well as two other special friends) testifies that these words were spoken by Luther not long before his death: *Pray,* he said, *to God, that He may teach us to preserve His Gospel. For the Pope and the Council of Trent <note>The Council of Trent (1545–1563) was the Catholic Church's formal response to the Protestant Reformation.</note> intend horrible things.* And feeling that the end of his life was now approaching, he prayed thus: *My Heavenly Father, God and Father of our Lord Jesus Christ, God of all comforts, I thank you that you have revealed to me your Son Jesus Christ, whom I have believed, whom I have preached, whom I have loved, whom I have exalted, and whom the Roman Pope and his godless crowd persecute. I pray you, Lord Jesus Christ, receive my soul, etc.* Speaking these last words in the presence of Justus Jonas, he commended his dying soul to God as if into a bosom. Jonas saw Luther die with great sadness, but also with exceeding great joy, hearing him speak so faithfully from the heart.

By Justus Jonas, these books were published:

*Defense of the marriage of Priests, against Johannes Faber, patron or protector of all whoremongers.* <note>Johannes Faber (1478–1541) was a Catholic bishop and fierce opponent of the Reformation; the term "hoer-jageren" (whoremongers) was a standard piece of Reformist polemic against clerical celibacy.</note>

*An Oration on the Study of Theology.*

*Annotations on the Acts of the Apostles.*

<column-break/>

*On the Private or "Grease-mass" and the Anointing of Priests, a booklet by Martin Luther translated by him from High German into Latin.* <note>The "Private Mass" refers to the practice of a priest performing the Mass alone, which the Reformers rejected. "Slick-misse" (Schleifmesse) was a derogatory term used for these rites.</note>

*Luther's Declaration on Jonah, translated by the same.*

*Also translated Luther’s book on the Jews and their lies into Latin.* <note>This refers to Luther's 1543 treatise "Von den Juden und ihren Lügen."</note>

*Against Wicelius.* <note>Georg Witzel (1501–1573), known as Wicelius, was a theologian who attempted to find a middle ground between Catholicism and Lutheranism, eventually returning to the Catholic Church.</note>

*On the Death of Luther.*

### JUSTUS JONAS.

> Law, wisdom, eloquence, Jonas, and most of all the arts
> Exalt your name above the clear light of heaven.
> But Godliness, as Queen of such a spirit,
> And holy Faith, praise you far above them.

Justus Jonas, an eyewitness to Luther, calls him lion-hearted, both in death and life. He manfully challenged the Pope from all sides, and remained the same in death as he was in life.

# CASPAR CRU-
## CIGER.

<image-desc>Ornate woodcut decorative initial 'C' featuring botanical flourishes.</image-desc>CASPAR CRUCIGER, born in Leipzig, a Minister of God’s word in the Church of Wittenberg, shall rightly be given a place here: both in respect to his worthy Office, as well as his exceptional learning and his great judgment, who helped *Luther* <note>Martin Luther (1483–1546), the central figure of the Protestant Reformation.</note> not a little in the building up of Christ’s Church. This man was highly esteemed for his great experience in languages, through which he—as if in the infancy of the first-born Church—stammered and reached out those words and manners of speaking which could readily bring her to a fit and clear expression of God’s word. Also, it was no small praise for *Cruciger* when they say that *Luther*, in translating the Bible from the Hebrew into the High German tongue, often made use of this man’s counsel and approval, and relied upon his judgment. It was truly a gift and wonder of God that in the rebirth of the Church, the holy language <note>Hebrew</note> was also, as if reborn, brought to its interpretation through *Luther* (the most learned German translator) and was read and preached by the Germans. And just as *Luther* immediately became very famous through that heavy and Divine work; so also must *Cruciger* not be withheld his praise, since *Luther* himself ascribes so much to him in this work. Furthermore, being very experienced in the Mathematical Arts: eventually leaving behind the speculations and proofs of the same, he fervently embraced and preached the Christian faith: which assures us no less of the hope of our salvation than our eyes can perceive the certain rules of those same arts. In the knowledge of natural things, as also in Medicine, he had likewise greatly increased: But to the Creator of all things, and Christ the Physician of Souls, he finally surrendered and dedicated himself entirely. Among other fine Studies for which one must give him honor, this is said in his praise: that when he was called to the Assemblies first at Worms, and thereafter at Regensburg, he had a private conversation with *Calvin* <note>John Calvin (1509–1564), the French theologian and reformer in Geneva.</note> concerning the Lord’s Supper, and having understood his knowledge and sentiment,

<summary>This page provides a biographical sketch of Caspar Cruciger, a key collaborator of Martin Luther, highlighting his linguistic expertise in the German Bible translation and his transition from science to theology.</summary>
<keywords>Caspar Cruciger, Martin Luther, Wittenberg, Bible translation, Hebrew, Mathematics, Medicine, John Calvin, Reformation, Worms, Regensburg</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the biographical account of Caspar Cruciger the Elder, a close associate of Martin Luther and Philipp Melanchthon.</meta>

# CASPAR

party, has allowed and also confirmed. The eloquent Beza <note>Theodore Beza (1519–1605), a French Protestant theologian and successor to Calvin</note> in the *Life of Calvin* <note>original: "in d’leven Calvini"</note> seems to wish to interpret this to the praise of them both. The one was exceedingly dear to the other; it were to be wished that many others were as inclined to be taught themselves as they are to teach! Truly, the affairs of Christendom would stand in a better and more righteous state today: and the power and practices of the *Antichrist* <note>A common Reformation-era term for the Papacy</note> would without doubt not be able to accomplish so much. But the Church did not enjoy the faithful service of this pious man for many years, for in the 45th year of his age, his soul was transferred to that place where the Lord Christ truly ascended with his body, and where he truly remains with that same body. He died at Wittenberg, in the Year of our Lord 1548.

# GEORGE, PRINCE
## OF ANHALT, COUNT
### OF ASCANIA.

It is unnecessary that I should rouse you, beloved viewer, to look upon this man: since he is one of the number of Princes, and as such is beheld with great desire by every man. This is the *Prince of Anhalt*, and *Count of Ascania*, godly and learned: I say an exceedingly good Prince, and an exceedingly good Shepherd <note>The term "Shepherd" here refers to his role as a spiritual leader or bishop within the church.</note>. Seldom are Princes counted among the Evangelical <note>In this context, "Evangelical" refers to the early Protestant or Lutheran movement.</note>: but even more seldom are they found to be both Evangelical and shepherds. From illustrious Princes, and from an ancient Noble house and lineage, was born *George* by the grace of God, Prince and Shepherd, whose father was *Ernest*, Prince of Anhalt, Count of *Ascania*, Lord of Bernburg and Zerbst. From the lineage of *Ascania*, highly praised in virtue, glory, and fame: this *George* has so glorified and illuminated it through his new and holy studies, that it seems nothing more could have been added to expand and adorn this Noble lineage. When he had successfully exercised himself in good letters <note>The "humanities" or classical education.</note> at *Wittenberg*, and had acquired the three principal languages <note>Latin, Greek, and Hebrew.</note> in a princely manner; he then devoted himself entirely to the study of Theology, and was chosen as the first provost of the churches of Magdeburg and Meissen, whom (as that which we most admire in good Princes) he did not only preside over, but also advanced. For that is a true Shepherd’s office: to so preside over his own that he may be of benefit to them: and to so stand before the flock of the Lord, that he, as an exceedingly faithful Shepherd for the same, protecting them, may be seen to stand; which Prince and Shepherd, governing the people according to the Laws, and feeding the same with the doctrine of Salvation, may be named and held as the second *Ezra* <note>original: "Esdras"; a reference to the biblical priest and scribe who restored the law to Israel.</note> of our time. To whose praise this is yet to be said, namely: that when the German churches stood in a very sober and sorrowful state, he, strengthened and confirmed by God’s spirit, nevertheless faithfully fulfilled his office, and did not desert his post: but rather the affairs of the same Christian Church, to the Princes, as

<summary>A biographical tribute to George, Prince of Anhalt, highlighting his rare dual role as both a secular ruler and a learned spiritual leader (Shepherd) during the Reformation.</summary>
<keywords>George of Anhalt, House of Ascania, Wittenberg, Theology, Magdeburg, Meissen, Ezra, Protestant Reformation, Prince-Bishop</keywords>

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographical account of George, Prince of Anhalt, focusing on his diplomatic efforts, his character as a "Prince-Prophet," and a bibliography of his published theological works.</meta>

-># GEORGE, PRINCE OF ANHALT<-

He also presented and urged these matters before Emperor Charles <note>Emperor Charles V (1500–1558)</note> (who was threatening the Evangelical cause with the severest punishments). His house stood open at that time as a haven and refuge for the miserable and the fugitive: he was most outstanding in generosity and hospitality toward strangers. He bore a singular love for *Melanchthon* <note>Philipp Melanchthon (1497–1560), a key German Reformer and collaborator of Luther</note> and his companions in labor and danger. Because of this, a place has been assigned to *Anhalt* himself among those with whom he most dearly lived and most diligently studied. Having left behind his fine example as a Prince and shepherd of the people to other Princes, he has departed for Heaven, the dwelling place of the King of all kings and the Shepherd of all princes. Having well-taught and faithfully preached that Divine matters must be preferred over human ones, and Heavenly matters over Earthly ones, he has taken up his inheritance in that Kingdom of which there is no end. He departed from this life in the 46th year of his age, and in the year of our salvation obtained in Christ, 1553. Those subjects are to be considered exceedingly happy who obtain such a great Prince, whom they may acknowledge, honor, and follow as a shepherd and caretaker of their souls as well as their bodies.

From George, Prince of Anhalt, these writings have come down to us:

A comforting Oration on the saying of *Christ*; "No one shall pluck my Sheep out of my hand," etc. Delivered at the Merseburg provincial assembly.

A sermon delivered at Torgau at the wedding of *Augustus*, Duke of Saxony, and *Anne*, daughter of the King of Denmark.

Four Sermons on both forms of the Sacrament <note>Refers to the practice of "Utraquism," giving both bread and wine to the laity during Communion</note>, in which the objections of the adversaries are dismantled and refuted with strong arguments.

<column-break/>

His Consolation to his brother *Wolfgang*, who was ill.

Two Sermons concerning false Prophets, and the true use of the Christian Religion: In which the errors of *Sidonius*, Bishop of Merseburg, are rejected.

Sermons and various writings comprising the entire true doctrine established in the pure Church.

A Treatise on the Apostolic use of ordination maintained in the purest churches adhering to the *Augsburg Confession* <note>The primary confession of faith of the Lutheran Church, 1530</note>.

His own views on the articles currently in dispute.

# ERASMUS SAR-
## CERIUS.

<image-desc>Large decorative woodcut initial 'S' featuring elaborate scrolling foliage and a stylized mask at the bottom center.</image-desc>See here is **Erasmus**, that **Sarcerius Annemontanus** <note>Annemontanus refers to his birthplace, Annaberg</note>, who, after he had excellently practiced in the liberal arts and the study of all virtues, and having now acquired human learning and arts, most ardently embraced the study and investigation of the Holy Scriptures. And as he had successfully learned the same at Wittenberg (which at that time was like an Evangelical Athens <note>A metaphor for Wittenberg as the intellectual center of the Reformation</note>, where students of the Holy Scriptures flocked from all sides and from far-flung kingdoms), he was placed among the first disciples who began to spread and sow the teachings of the Gospel everywhere through preaching and writing. He was held in that time to be a very select preacher and a most capable teaching theologian, as one who, through the service of philosophy, presented and explained God's word very capably and orderly. From this it followed that at that time the *Neophytes* <term>Neophyti: young newcomers or recent converts to the faith</term>, who through *Sarcerius* their master, considered themselves very capable and learned to preach God's word, greatly loved his fine writings. He was a very eloquent preacher, first at Leipzig, a widely renowned, pleasant, and very clean city in the land of Meissen, which listened with wonder to the very sweet and pure sermons of this same *Sarcerius*. And thereafter, in the very renowned County of Nassau, he was a shepherd and select leader. This man, under *William*, Count of Nassau—father of that great *William* <note>Referring to William the Silent, leader of the Dutch Revolt</note>—proclaimed the Gospel with exceeding diligence and faith, teaching, writing, and concerning himself with that which brought and guided the churches and their ministers onto the right way and path of the Lord. He greatly praised and confirmed the civil policy and spiritual discipline or instruction, established according to the ancient canons <term>Canons: ecclesiastical rules or laws</term>. *Sarcerius*, having now found such a godly, kind, and very virtuous Prince, namely **William** of Nassau, as a foster-father of the churches, expanded the pure word of God under him with great fruitfulness. It is for the Princes a
->great<-

<summary>This page provides a biographical account of the Reformer Erasmus Sarcerius, detailing his education in Wittenberg and his influential ministry in Leipzig and Nassau under Count William the Rich.</summary>
<keywords>Erasmus Sarcerius, Wittenberg, Leipzig, Nassau, William of Nassau, Protestant Reformation, Theology, Church Discipline</keywords>

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographical sketches of notable figures, transitioning here to the life and legacy of Erasmus and the religious reformers associated with the House of Nassau.</meta>

-> E R A S M U S <-

It is a great praise to love godliness, and to place the same above all worthy and highly esteemed things. But as godliness is threefold, consisting in the love of God, of the fatherland, and of one's parents; we see these as inherited and innate qualities assigned and left to the noble House of Nassau. Whose godliness has appeared and been expressed above all in William, surnamed the Great <note>William the Silent (1533–1584), Prince of Orange and Count of Nassau</note>, who by means of his heroic virtues was declared: *A father of the Fatherland, a champion of liberty, and a patron and protector of religion.* Who, out of necessity, along with the other Netherlandish Princes (having first attempted and proposed everything which would not have been done by others), being compelled, has shown by deed that the fatherland was dearer and more worth to him than the Spanish tyranny—which rules over property, blood, and souls. His manly virtue has left behind his own image, indeed himself, as an inheritance. Him also the whole world must praise and love, who feared the Almighty Spaniard through his ambition, greed, and raging, frantic tyranny. 

And so that I may return to the previous discourse, the widely-famed County of Nassau did not wish to leave you, **Sarcerius** <note>Erasmus Sarcerius (1501–1559), a Lutheran reformer and theologian</note>, in the dark (although you are otherwise sufficiently renowned through your learning). You lived under that Prince who, like the others, was very devoted to God's word, establishing famous schools, out of which especially those might spring and be brought forth who, through preaching, would exalt Christ's Kingdom and root out that of the Antichrist. Just as we still see some today who have come forth from them, who, sheltering under this Doctor Visscher <note>Likely a reference to a specific contemporary theologian or mentor</note>, swim in the wide, wild, desolate sea and among the turbulent people of this world, very skillfully enticing many with the bait of godliness, and catching and bringing them to Christ, the Lord of all Lords. 

When Sarcerius had now won very great honor and high thanks in the Church, and had preserved its state in the County of Nassau in its entirety, he departed to the blessed dwelling place of the elect, and passed from this life at Magdeburg. In the Year of Salvation 1559, and of his age 59.
