## The Books published by Sarcerius.

Expositions on Jesus Sirach <note>Also known as Ecclesiasticus, a book of the Apocrypha.</note>.
Expositions on the Epistles for Sundays and Feast Days, arranged in a sufficiently complete manner and order.
Faithful exposition on the four Evangelists <note>The Gospel writers: Matthew, Mark, Luke, and John.</note>, according to the sequence and meaning of the text.
Exposition on the Acts of the Apostles, quite broad or complete.
Expositions on the Epistles to the Romans, to the Galatians, to the Ephesians, and the Philippians.
Methods, or short ways of teaching, on the principal passages of the Holy Scripture, set down as clear instructional rules for the use of Theologians.
A dialogue explaining the cause of the ancient Synods or assemblies, both general and provincial: Likewise the examinations, and the recently held Synod and examination concerning the Pastors of the County of Nassau, involving the Lord Count William <note>William the Rich, Count of Nassau-Dillenburg (1487–1559), father of William the Silent.</note>, also expressing the acts of the same examination, the report of which can be very beneficial to other lands.
Catechism, or oral instruction, concerning all the disputes and circumstances that may occur in legal proceedings, for the use of Preachers.

<column-break/>

On the comparison or the agreement of the true Church and the holy Fathers, but primarily of St. Augustine, on the principal articles of the Christian Religion.
Sermons on the signs and causes which—unless it turns to repentance—foretell many punishments and evils for Germany.
Sermons on Repentance, the manner of restoring discipline, on Councils and Assemblies, on the visitation of Parishes, on the Office of Magistrates and of Subjects, on the Righteousness that avails before God, and good works.
On avoiding the crudities of the old Papists, and the cunning snares and errors of the new Sophists.
Expositions on nearly all the books of the Old and New Testament.
On the Cross or temptation, with the preface of Melanchthon <note>Philipp Melanchthon (1497–1560), a central figure of the German Reformation and collaborator of Luther.</note>.
The Opinion of ancient and modern writers on marriage and the divorce thereof.
The Great Book on the Laws of Marriage.
On the vanity of Scholastic Theology <note>The medieval system of theology and philosophy taught in European universities, which Reformers like Sarcerius often critiqued as overly speculative.</note>, through which the people in times past justified their Religion and teaching; a book arranged in a suitable and common order.
On Brotherly Reconciliation.
A comforting writing concerning his children, to Count Christian.
On the knowledge of the Truth.

<meta>This page continues the biographical accounts of prominent Reformers, transitioning from the previous section to focus on the Swiss Reformer Huldrych Zwingli.</meta>

# HULDRYCH
## ZWINGLI.

<image-desc>A large decorative historiated initial 'G' featuring ornate foliate scrolls and architectural elements.</image-desc>
Just as Germany held her Luther, so too has Switzerland held her Zwingli in great esteem. While the sound of Luther’s Evangelical Trumpet spread far and wide across the vast German lands, Zwingli’s voice—hemmed in between the Alpine mountains as if between two walls—could not at first be easily carried to the open plains. But after these cloisters or confining walls were burst through everywhere by this powerful Word, that same sound penetrated the high Alpine peaks (which serve as the walls of Italy) and finally expanded into Italy, France, and the surrounding Kingdoms. At nearly the same time, they both proclaimed the soul-saving doctrine of the Gospel: the latter at Zurich, the former at Wittenberg. 

But Zwingli (as he himself testifies in the book of his articles, through a very learned and excellent Writing) began to preach the Gospel in the Year of Salvation 1516, after he had previously taught that the trade in Indulgences <note>The practice of selling the remission of temporal punishment for sins, a major catalyst for the Reformation.</note> was nothing other than pure deceit and extortion. He drew this from a certain theological disputation which the learned man, Doctor Thomas Wittenbach of Biel—who had been Zwingli’s master—had held some years prior at Basel in the public assembly of the theological High School. 

Zwingli was a man thoroughly and very learned in all kinds of science; through the help and advancement of the three primary languages <note>original: "dryer hooft-talen" referring to Latin, Greek, and Hebrew.</note>, which he knew well, he searched and handled the Holy Scriptures very usefully. In his own tongue, namely in the Swiss speech, he was considered very choice and eloquent. The City of Rome, being entirely unsettled and as if struck down by these great men and Doctors, placed little hope or trust in her weapons and took refuge in her artful cunning. Through this, she intended to bring them both to ruin and to seize them deceitfully, proposing to give them wealth, honor, and great Titles, so that she might thus draw them into Saint Peter’s fishing-basket <note>A metaphorical reference to the Papacy's attempts to bribe or lure Reformers back into the Roman Catholic fold.</note> for her own service. And just as

<summary>This page introduces Huldrych Zwingli as the Swiss counterpart to Martin Luther, detailing his early education under Thomas Wittenbach and his initial preaching against indulgences in 1516.</summary>
<keywords>Huldrych Zwingli, Martin Luther, Swiss Reformation, Zurich, Thomas Wittenbach, Indulgences, Basel, Rome, Alpine mountains</keywords>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with a discussion of the early Reformation; this page continues the narrative regarding the attempts of the Roman Church to influence the reformers and the subsequent theological rift between Luther and Zwingli. The catchword "doot" (death) from the previous page connects to the opening sentence regarding the fate or actions of the reformers.</meta>

-># U L D R I C H<-

just as they <note>The Roman Catholic authorities</note> had wished to make **Luther** their own through cunning practices; so too did the Roman Cardinals (who at that time carried out their embassies to Zurich) approach **Zwingli** quite craftily in the first beginning of the Evangelical Preaching. They sought to win him over and seduce him through money and property (things they otherwise hated to give); but their designs in this were frustrated and came to nothing. For these men, driven by God’s Spirit, despised the world and these Roman *Carnales* <note>original: "Carnales"; meaning fleshly or worldly-minded men</note> or carnal teachings. 

Now, while these two Master Builders were busy faithfully laying the foundations of the Kingdom of the Gospel, a certain discord arose between them. In the continuation of that great work, a heavy conflict emerged over the meaning of the Lord’s words spoken at the Last Supper—specifically the words of Him whom they both proclaimed as the sole source and Master of our Salvation. And although **Luther** was awakened by the Spirit of God, he nevertheless, in the interpreting of those words, insisted upon and maintained I know not what kind of bodily and natural presence and partaking of the Body of Christ, which is entirely contrary to the nature and property of the Sacraments. 

To justify and uphold this opinion of his, he used—sometimes like the Pope, other times using new and strange weapons—arguments by which he defended and stood for the idea that Christ’s body was present everywhere and in all places, just as His Divinity is. This dispute, after the death of both men, was driven with such discord and such violent frenzy by the false **Lutherans**. They not only championed **Luther’s** strange understanding or opinion, but many other more gruesome opinions were devised and invented to protect *Ubiquity* <term>Ubiquity: the theological doctrine that Christ's human body is present everywhere at once</term> or the "being-everywhere" of the body of Christ. Indeed, they did this in such a way that they have nearly uprooted the foundation stones of the Faith, which had been laid by **Luther** with such faithfulness and steadfastness (on account of the hatred they bear toward the **Zwinglians**). 

At this, the Papists <note>A period term for Roman Catholics, often used polemically</note>, being spectators of this strife, laugh heartily. God grant that this dispute of *Ubiquity* or the "being-everywhere" once being taken away, we may all together approach and embrace Him everywhere. That you, **Zwingli**, were the most courageous champion of Religion and the Fatherland, both Religion and the Fatherland express clearly enough regarding you. You did not wish to desert your people (who were wrongly provoked to battle), fearing neither danger nor

<summary>This page describes the failed attempts by Roman Cardinals to bribe Zwingli and details the theological schism between Luther and Zwingli regarding the nature of the Eucharist and the doctrine of Ubiquity.</summary>
<keywords>Huldrych Zwingli, Martin Luther, Eucharist, Ubiquity, Protestant Reformation, Zurich, Lutheranism, Zwinglianism, Sacraments</keywords>

--- Page 109 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographical account of the Swiss Reformer Huldrych Zwingli, specifically detailing his death at the Battle of Kappel and the subsequent cataloging of his theological works.</meta>

->ZWINGLI<-

death, as befits a faithful shepherd and Teacher, you did not fear. But having awakened and strengthened the spirits of godly Christians—who fight for Religion and freedom—through good hope, exhortations, and consolations, and finally falling dead at the head of the army, you have truly shown yourself to be one who shed his blood for the truth and the common welfare. As often, *Zwingli*, as your adversaries slander this deed of yours, so often do true and godly reasons praise and commend the same as the prompting of your necessity. But the excessive cruelty of your adversaries was noted in this: that they cut the fallen body into four parts and thereafter burned the same to ashes; by which body's living and zealous voice, everyone's hearts had been ignited and stolen (as if by the flame of the Gospel's preaching). The Battle in which *Zwingli* fell occurred in the Year 1531, the 11th of October, he being 44 years old.

munity, and the Church of Zurich.

Exhortation to the oldest Community of Switzerland, so that they should guard themselves against the friendship of Princes and their cunning gifts or bribes.
On the Certainty and Clarity of the Word of God.

Complaint to *Fridolin Lindauer*.
Answer from those of Zurich.

### HULDRYCH ZWINGLI.

> It is said Huldrych claimed two things as his own:
> Namely, above all, the Word of God; thereafter, his Fatherland.
> Both of which he was seen to fulfill entirely faithfully;

*Was Zwingli: who united the love of God's word and the land.*
*Whose understanding first prepared these for their defense.*
*The godly mourn the Shepherd; the country mourns the Man.*

# JOHANNES OECO-
# LAMPADIUS. S.

<image-desc>A large ornamental woodcut initial letter 'N' decorated with intricate foliate scrollwork and vine patterns.</image-desc>

Next to his *Zwingli*, with all right, *Oecolampadius* <note>The name is a Greek translation of his German name, Hausschein, meaning "house lamp."</note> takes his own place here, the first true Shepherd and Reformer of the Church of Basel. In learning, in diligence, in stature, position, and age, they were equals to one another; between whom there was such unity of spirit that hardly ever was a holier or more constant friendship, joined through godliness, found between any men. By nation, *Oecolampadius* was a High German, his fatherland being *Weinsberg*, a city in Franconia. At Heidelberg, he became a Master of the Liberal Arts; he studied Imperial Law at Bologna. From *Capnio* or that *Reuchlin* <note>Johannes Reuchlin (1455–1522), a famous humanist and Hebrew scholar often called "Capnio."</note>—whom the Sophists <note>A derogatory term used by Reformers for traditional scholastic theologians.</note> and speakers of "kitchen Latin" <note>A term for the unrefined, non-classical Latin used by medieval monks.</note> could not look upon with good eyes—he learned the Greek language, and from a certain Spaniard, the Hebrew language, at roughly the same time. When he was chosen as a Shepherd or Preacher in his fatherland, he was called to the aforementioned Basel: where the famous *Erasmus* of *Rotterdam* used his advice and joined him to his own work in the printing and publishing of the *Annotations* or notes on the New Testament. He [Erasmus] freely acknowledged (to use his own words):

> *That he was helped in no small way by the labor and assistance of him who was not only gifted with godliness, but also, through great experience in the three principal languages <note>Latin, Greek, and Hebrew.</note> as well as in Theology, stood out greatly above all other lights.*

He permitted people to call him "Doctor," to which he was sufficiently urged by his friends. The Sophists of Basel, and those there who love dark and obscure teachings, could neither tolerate nor endure him. For he was *Oecolampadius*, that is, the lamp; namely, by bringing forward the Word of God, he uncovered the errors and blindness of the Sophists. Although they opposed him, nevertheless, with the will of the Authorities, he publicly lectured upon and explained *Isaiah*. While he was now exercising the office of Preacher in the Church of God—namely the House of God—he brought the Word of God into it like a lamp for Christians. At which time he [translated] *Chrysostom* on Ge-

<meta>This page continues the biographical account of the Reformer Johannes Oecolampadius, following the discussion of his scholarly contributions and his relationship with other key figures of the Reformation.</meta>

# JOHN

<note>The header refers to Johannes Oecolampadius (1482–1531), the primary Reformer of Basel.</note>

...as well as *Theophylact* <note>Theophylact of Ohrid, a Byzantine biblical exegete</note>, like a little bee picking out the flowers of *Chrysostom* <note>John Chrysostom, an early Church Father known for his eloquence</note>, which he published in Latin. Great and unspeakable labor did he bear for the Church of Christ in those times. Against *Johann Eck* <note>original: "Fabrum Eccium"; a prominent Catholic defender against the Reformation</note> he fought alone for the truth; how faithfully and skillfully he carried that out is sufficiently testified by the Acts published by his adversaries, which are more credible than others. So much so that many cities (which at that time desired to see Christ as the light of the world) desired to enjoy and read his services and books as those of a true Reformer and Doctor. Of this, those of *Bern* and those of *Ulm* were the first to testify. He was very skillful and fortunate in settling and removing disputes and discords. In writing, interpreting, teaching, and refuting all kinds of adversaries, as well as in much translating, he gained a great name for himself and much fruit for the Church; his labor on some of the Prophets is highly praised by the learned. In character, he was a very orderly and learned man, also very useful and capable in handling spiritual matters, but he reached only a modest age (though he was worthy of a longer life): and followed his *Zwingli* <note>Huldrych Zwingli, the Swiss Reformer</note> after only a few weeks, for toward the end of November he passed away from this world in *Basel*; he was forty-nine years old. His godly final departure was recorded by *Simon Grynaeus*, a certain and present witness, in a letter written to *Wolfgang Capito*. And truly *Basel* mourned the true Reformer of her religion no less than *Zurich* wept for her *Zwingli*: the great fellowship and joining of whose studies and labors wonderfully advanced the course of the Gospel. So much so that, not unsuitably, many things in the books of *Zwingli*, and again in various books of *Oecolampadius*, are brought together and united. And thus the life and passing of both are properly described for us by *Simon Grynaeus*, *Wolfgang Capito*, and *Oswald Myconius*.

### Translated books from the Greek.

All these things this very learned, godly, and over-laboring man has left to us, dying in the best part of his life.

## JOHANNES OECOLAMPADIUS.

Oecolampadius, like a lamp of God, brightly
Shining / has driven the darkness far away.
So that the world has scarcely held such a light within her
As this pious man's virtue / Faith and Hope in life.

The dark night, and the night's ghosts he has driven off,
Oecolampadius, setting forth God's command.
Whose lamp neither the Pope's nor the Sophists' <note>A derogatory term used by Reformers for scholastic theologians who used complex logic to defend traditional doctrines.</note> hate could extinguish,
Since his light is helpfully accompanying every man.

## SIMON GRYNAEUS <note>Simon Grynaeus (1493–1541) was a German scholar of Greek and a theologian who helped reform the University of Basel and was a close friend of Erasmus and Melanchthon.</note>

# SIMON GRYNÆUS

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'H' featuring figures within a landscape and classical architectural elements.</image-desc>

The solid and pleasant character which *Simon Grynæus*, a *German* man, displays here, corresponds entirely with his virtues and intellect, which the observer can readily perceive. Among the *Baslers* <note>Citizens of Basel, Switzerland</note> he was a true *Basler*: That is, he was godly, upright, faithful, kind-hearted, and hospitable; he who, above all, greatly loved the simplicity of manners and composure inherent to true *German-Swiss* men. But as he was a *Philosopher*, as well as—I know not whether I should speak according to common opinion—a *Philologist* <note>original: "Philologus"; a lover of words/literature</note>, or a master of the elegance of all arts. Someone might ask why he then occupies a place here among the *Theologians*: But we shall place him in the Holy gallery because he lived holily and in friendship with holy men as a *Theologian*: and observed and described their good customs and teachings. He also publicly taught not only *Philosophy*—though that for the most part—but also *Theology* at *Basel*, and left behind such writings as are worthy of the name of a *Theologian*. He was also joined with *Melanchthon*, *Capito*, *Bucer*, *Calvin*, and many others traveling to the famous Colloquy or conference at *Worms* in the year 1540. And as this man had practiced himself very well in the liberal arts as well as in languages, and through the reading of many authors, both *Greek* and *Latin*, had understood and learned much; it happened that he often gave his judgment thereon, when in those times books by authors of both those languages in all kinds of arts and faculties were being published. Whose advice and judgment even the book-printers themselves seem to have frequently used: And in the planning and printing of their books (which our *Basel*, like a fertile mother, has birthed and brought forth in great multitudes), they greatly desired this man's judgment, as if it were a *Grynæan Apollonian Oracle* <note>A reference to the Oracle of Apollo, implying his word was considered divinely wise and definitive</note>, as is testified by his prefaces and letters placed before nearly all kinds of authors: through which this *Grynæus's* name has been made great and renowned among posterity. Had he lived longer, he would have advanced Literature and *Theology* in no small measure through his fine labor, brave intellect, learning, and judgment. Wherefore he, just like *Zwingli* and *Oecolampadius*, in the flower and the best of his life...

<summary>This page provides a biographical tribute to Simon Grynæus, a German-Swiss scholar at Basel, highlighting his transition from a philosopher and philologist to a respected theologian and advisor to the great Reformers.</summary>
<keywords>Simon Grynæus, Basel, Reformation, Humanism, Colloquy of Worms, Theology, Philology, Book Printing, Melanchthon, Calvin, Zwingli, Oecolampadius</keywords>

# SIMON

life of this world has passed away, being 48 years old, and in the year of our salvation obtained in Christ 1541. In the month of August, at the time when the Emperor Charles <note>Emperor Charles V</note> was firmly equipping his naval fleet or Armada to send to Africa, and the Plague in Germany increased greatly, especially along the region of the Rhine: by which, among others, our *Grynaeus* was snatched away at Basel, and *Capito* <note>Wolfgang Capito, a Reformer</note> at Strasbourg: both excellent in learning; the latter a Theologian by profession, but the former a Philosopher, and both entirely devoted to the Holy Scriptures. Regarding his death, in the preface of the Greek books of Theophrastus, to which is added the praise of his life, the very learned, eloquent man gifted with a diligent judgment, *Joachimus Camerarius*—the light of Germany—has written.

# SEBASTIAN
## MÜNSTER.

SEBASTIAN MÜNSTER is not improperly added here alongside Grynaeus, as he also taught in the Basel Academy or High School. By nationality he was a High German; his fatherland was Ingelheim, and he was a Professor of Theology and the Hebrew language. From a Franciscan <note>A monk of the Order of Friars Minor</note> he became a Christian <note>The author implies a conversion to the Reformed/Protestant faith</note>. He was so learned that he no longer wished to keep house with the "Little Brothers of Ignorance" <note>original: "Broerkens der onwetenheyt" — a derogatory term for his former monastic order</note>; truly a man of vast knowledge, as his books sufficiently demonstrate to everyone. As one who was well-experienced and deeply steeped in all kinds of arts and sciences, as well as Mathematics, his labor in interpreting and explaining the Hebrew and Chaldean <note>term: Chaldean — an older term for the Aramaic language</note> languages was infinite. To print these works during Münster's life, the printing houses of Heinrich Petri and Froben labored to the point of sweating; and he was the first who brought forth for us the Chaldean grammar. Happy indeed are the Basel Doctors and Scribes who, frequenting the print shops daily through their literary works, see the presses with their own eyes and behold their written books printed upon them in great multitudes. By means of this Art of Printing, Basel is today no less renowned throughout all Christendom than Augusta <note>referring to Augusta Raurica, a Roman archaeological site near Basel</note> near Basel was in former times, having sprung from the Romans as a magnificent city (as it appears). And just as Basel delights in *Münster*, so also *Münster* rejoices in Basel, beholding it joyfully; as he has shown and provided us with the depiction of the City of Basel, along with the description of the same and the surrounding lands. There are two singular works among others that have earned *Münster* honor and a name: namely, that Hebrew Bible with a fine, new, and very clear translation, featuring the oldest and best authors in those languages. Likewise, he added annotations drawn from the Rabbinical commentaries or explanations. The other is the *Cosmographia* or Description of the World, dedicated to Emperor Charles V, which was afterwards printed in the languages of many nations, and [won] for himself the

## AMBROSIUS BLARER <note>Ambrosius Blarer (or Blaurer) was an influential German-Swiss Reformer and a former Benedictine monk who led the Reformation in the city of Constance.</note>

->Blarer had been imbued with the ambrosial fragrance<-
->Of the Divine word; this man is entirely Ambrosius.<-
<note>original: "Ambrosio imbutus fuerat BLAVRERVS odore / Diuini verbi, totus hic AMBROSIVS." The poet uses a clever wordplay on the name "Ambrosius" and "ambrosia," the food or scent of the gods, to suggest Blarer’s character was saturated with the sweetness of the Gospel.</note>

->Fickle Constance drove out so great a citizen:<-
->But he, in turn, turned his back on his ungrateful homeland.<-
<note>original: "Inconstans pepulit tantum Constantia ciuem: / Ingratum patriam spreuit at ille suam." This contains a Latin pun on the name of the city "Constance" (Constantia) and "inconstant" (Inconstans). It refers to the historical event where Constance was forced to surrender its status as a Free Imperial City and return to Catholicism in 1548, leading to Blarer's exile.</note>

->With privilege.<- <note>original: "Cum priuil." An abbreviation of "Cum privilegio," indicating the printer had legal protection or a monopoly to publish the work.</note>

# AMBROSIUS
## BLAURER

<image-desc>A large decorative drop cap 'D' at the beginning of the text, featuring ornate floral scrollwork and a profile of a bearded man's head integrated into the design.</image-desc>

THE City of Constance, which took the life of our <note>Jan Hus (c. 1369–1415), a Czech reformer burned at the stake during the Council of Constance.</note> Hus, has in turn brought forth and given to us **Ambrosius Blaurer**, a man descended from an honorable lineage. Drawn by zeal for religion, he previously embraced the monastic life at Alpirsbach; and since he was gifted with a good spirit, having read the books of *Luther* <note>original: "Luteri"</note>, he underwent a change of mind and returned to his own people. That college <note>The monastery of Alpirsbach</note> is situated on the borders of the territory of the Duke of Württemberg. And as the Abbot was fruitlessly urging those of Constance to force him to return to his Order, *Blaurer* broke and frustrated the Abbot’s troublesome persistence and persecution through an excellent piece of writing. Thus, he remained in Constance and thereafter preached the Gospel in his fatherland; the Canons <note>Members of the cathedral chapter</note> gnashed their teeth, for they, being exceedingly wealthy, had already been restrained by the Magistrate. He was present with *Zwingli, Oecolampadius,* and *Bucer* in the famous assembly of *Capito* and at the Disputation held at Bern in the year [15]28. The outcome of which disputation was brought so far and succeeded so well—not only in Bern but also in some surrounding places by order of the Legislators—that the Masses, Altars, Images, and all the idolatrous Roman shops and trinkets were together overthrown. In his fatherland, he served his faithful pastoral office until the time that the Decree or Command of *Charles V* <note>original: "Caroli V."</note> regarding the acceptance of the *Interim* <note>The Augsburg Interim (1548), a temporary religious law intended to reintegrate Protestants into the Catholic Church.</note> or "In-between" was proclaimed—which the godly interpreted as the *Interitum* <note>Latin: "destruction" or "ruin." A common pun among Reformers who saw the Interim as spiritual death.</note> or "Undoing"—and finally took hold even in Constance. This Command threatened all resistors with much suffering. Those of Constance, although for a long time they—foremost among all the High Germans—repelled the *Interim* as a "German Adultery" and appeared more steadfast in this than others, were finally [confronted by] the Emperor’s punitive orders; and a published book, as well as the sharpened swords of the Spaniards (whose cruelty they had previously experienced) directed against them, [causing] such prestige and terror

<summary>This page recounts the early life and reform work of Ambrosius Blaurer, a former monk who became a key reformer in Constance and participated in the Disputation of Bern. It describes the local resistance to the Augsburg Interim and the eventual pressure exerted by Emperor Charles V.</summary>
<keywords>Ambrosius Blaurer, Jan Hus, Constance, Alpirsbach, Martin Luther, Disputation of Bern, Huldrych Zwingli, Augsburg Interim, Charles V, Reformation in Germany</keywords>

--- Page 128 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographical sketch of the Reformer Ambrosius Blaurer, focusing on his exile from Constance and his later works.</meta>

# AMBROSIVS BLAVRERVS.
<note>original: "Ambrosius Blaurer" (1492–1564), a key figure in the Reformation in southern Germany and Switzerland.</note>

[...it was] their choice that they accepted it <note>referring to the Interim, a temporary religious decree imposed by the Emperor</note>, and thereafter adapted and conformed themselves in matters of Religion. Because Blaurer was in no way willing to allow this, he left his Fatherland: and proved in deed that he possessed a more constant spirit than Constance <note>A wordplay on the city's name, "Constantz," and the virtue of constancy</note> could contain. He allowed his Fatherland to stand before him as the place where the path was shown through which we can reach the true and eternal fatherland. Consequently, his labor and service were very useful to other Churches, in the establishing and well-building of which he persevered with great fruit and praise into his advanced years, remaining a man of God. He departed this world in the 67th year of his age. His learning, piety, godliness, modesty, and steadfastness are highly praised; whom also *Calvin* <note>John Calvin, the influential French theologian</note> held in great esteem for the sake of his excellent learning and experience (so that the testimony of such an excellent and beyond-praise man might be added to this).

->He has published:<-

<columns>2</columns>
*An advisory letter to the Church of Constance.*

*The Spiritual Treasure.*

*A book of consolation, containing the*

<column-break/>

*manner in which a Christian man shall prepare himself for death, and to endure all kinds of sorrow.*

*A Sermon delivered at Bern, with the Acts of the Bohemian disputations.*

---

### AMBROSIVS BLAVRERVS.

**With Ambrosian sweet Scent was Blaurer infused** <note>A pun on his name: "Ambrosius" relating to ambrosia/sweet scent</note>
**When Ambrosius was enlightened by God's eternal word /**
**Constance, the inconstant, moved such a Citizen:**
**To depart from the ungrateful city that had given him birth.**
*The famous City of Constance knew Blaurer as her citizen,*
*Who was first a Monk, and later taught God's pure word:*
*Whose godly voice brings forth a Scent sweeter than Amber.*
*Following Christ's example as a scent of Salvation for all.*

## JUAN DÍAZ, THE SPANIARD

->While he hastens to renew the doctrines of the faithful Father,<-
->He does not allow the permitted crime to happen beforehand.<-
<note>original: "Dogmata dum properat fidi renovare parentis / Permissum fieri non sinit ante nefas." This refers to Díaz's efforts to spread Reformation theology before his life was cut short.</note>
->Oh, what a horrific crime! A Canon, whose head was covered by a cowl,<-
->For profane Rome was the high priest of crimes.<-
<note>original: "Pro scelus horrendum! Canon, cui tecta cucullo, / Nam scelerum antistes Roma profanus erat." This is a scathing critique of the Roman Catholic Church, specifically referring to Juan's brother, Alfonso Díaz, a lawyer at the Roman Curia (the "Canon"), who orchestrated Juan's murder in 1546.</note>

--- Page 131 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographical accounts of Protestant martyrs and reformers, specifically focusing on the early life and conversion of the Spaniard Juan Díaz.</meta>

# JOHN DIAZ

Observe this JOHN DIAZ <note>original: "IOANNEM DIAZIVM"</note>, a Spaniard from the heart of *Spain*. After he had diligently embraced the study of literature and *Theology* for many years in *Paris*, he also read the books of *Luther* and many others, as well as the *Holy Scripture*, with all due attention; viewing the *Sorbonne* <note>The theological faculty in Paris, then a stronghold of Catholic orthodoxy.</note> there as filthy and like a *pool* of impurity. In order that he might further advance in the *Holy Scripture* and, with joy alongside others, investigate and present its teachings, he first traveled to *Geneva*. Having stayed there for some days and spoken with *Calvin* (to whom he was very pleasing), he finally came to *Strasbourg*. There, *Martin Bucer* <note>A leading German Reformer based in Strasbourg.</note>, particularly noting the man's learning, care, and diligence, advised the *Magistrate* to appoint this same *Diaz* as a *companion* and assistant in his labors, to help advance the matters that were to be handled in the assembly of *Regensburg* in the year *1546*.

When he arrived in the aforementioned *Regensburg* in the month of *December*, *Pedro Malvenda* <note>original: "Petrus Malvendam"</note>, a *Spaniard* and agent for the *Pope's* affairs in *Germany*, arrived there as well. When he saw *Diaz* in the company of *Bucer* and the other *Evangelicals* <note>A term used for early Protestants.</note>, he stood as if in wonder, seeing how greatly he had changed since he had known him in *Paris*. *Malvenda* meanwhile tormented him greatly therein, knowing that the *Protestants* would boast and exalt more over one *Spaniard* brought to their opinion than over several thousand Germans. Therefore, *Malvenda* spared no effort that might serve to bring his countryman back to the bosom of the mother *Church*: presenting to *Diaz* now hope, then fear, then prosperity, then punishment, persisting equally with prayers and threats. 

But being able to accomplish nothing by those means, *Malvenda* took flight toward evil arts and practices, preparing himself for them; for he imagined that a *Spanish Evangelical* was something rare and strange. *Alfonso*, *Diaz's* brother, an Advocate of the *Roman Rota* <note>The highest appellate tribunal of the Catholic Church.</note>, traveled from *Rome* to *Regensburg* as if enraged and with great speed: with the intent that he would either bring *John* his brother back to the *Roman Church*, or certainly him—

<summary>This page recounts the conversion of the Spanish scholar Juan Díaz to Protestantism and the subsequent efforts by the papal agent Malvenda and Díaz's own brother, Alfonso, to force him back to Catholicism.</summary>
<keywords>Juan Díaz, Martin Bucer, John Calvin, Pedro Malvenda, Regensburg, Spanish Reformation, Sorbonne, religious conversion</keywords>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of the tragic betrayal of Juan Díaz by his brother Alfonso, following the theological tensions previously established.</meta>

# JOHN

likely to bring about his death; and in order to accomplish this, he devised every kind of cunning in his heart, just as he had been instructed in Italian tricks and flatteries, so that he might more easily deceive his simple and Christian brother. What more shall I say? Among all villains the most villainous brother, while his upright brother was reading his brother's letter with great zeal, had him murdered in a godless, cruel, and unheard-of manner on the twenty-seventh of March, through the service of a piece of a rogue armed with an axe. This horrible deed took place at Neuburg, situated on the Danube twelve miles above Regensburg. <note>Juan Díaz was a Spanish Protestant convert murdered in 1546 by his own brother, Alfonso, a fanatical Catholic.</note> And although *Alfonso* and his godless servant—hellish monsters—were caught in flight and placed in prison, and although the *Palatine of the Rhine, Otto Henry*, to whom *Neuburg* belonged, and others concerned with this act desired to punish these villains, nevertheless no judgment or justice was carried out against these evildoers (since they had great and powerful advocates and patrons). Having been at last released, or more likely set free, they were defended and praised by the Spaniards; indeed, they were exalted to the very heavens for having undertaken such a manly deed in murdering the heretic *Díaz*. From this we can well understand that the Papists have their Cains—yes, many worse than Cain ever was—and alongside them also the Jesuits, who like great trumpeters of praise, defend and highly preach this and similar horrors as a heroic or Roman deed. You, *Malvenda*, <note>Pedro Malvenda, a Catholic theologian who had previously debated Juan Díaz at the Regensburg Colloquy.</note> and you, *Alfonso*, have not so much killed the Christian *Díaz* through your wicked arts and godless weapons, as you have endowed him with a longer life: he who is praised here in great honor and lives eternally with God. As often as this history (which *Sleidanus* <note>Johannes Sleidanus, the famous chronicler of the Reformation.</note> also relates very clearly among other things) is read, so often are the memories of *Alfonso*—sprung from the blood of Cain—and of Spanish cruelties, which are infinite, renewed and brought to mind by those devoted to godliness and the freedom of the Fatherland.

<summary>This page details the 1546 murder of the Spanish Protestant Juan Díaz by his brother Alfonso in Neuburg, highlighting the lack of justice served and the subsequent glorification of the act by Catholic contemporaries.</summary>
<keywords>Juan Díaz, Alfonso Díaz, Pedro Malvenda, Neuburg, Regensburg, Johannes Sleidanus, Otto Henry Elector Palatine, martyrdom, fratricide, Reformation history</keywords>

# JUAN DIAZ

Tell me, you Papists <note>A historical, often derogatory term for followers of the Roman Catholic Church</note>, where a brother is freely permitted to murder his own brother: what spirit drives you there? Oh, what a gruesome deed! I issue a warning to all places: guard yourselves against shameful Rome, which loves the craftsman of wickedness.

*Observe this good man, this is the virtuous Diaz,*
*Whom one saw hating the Pope's entirely evil and very godless company.*
*He whom boastful Spain first gave to us: thereafter, a mad*
*and godless Spain took him back from us, to their own small gain.*

## MARTIN BUCER <note>original: "MARTINVS BVCERVS"</note>

> Germany boasts of your birth, Bucer, <note>Martin Bucer (1491–1551) was a key German Reformer based in Strasbourg who influenced Lutheran, Calvinist, and Anglican doctrines.</note>

--- Page 135 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographical sketches of prominent Reformers, moving from Martin Luther to his contemporary, Martin Bucer.</meta>

# MARTIN

## BUCER.

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap initial 'H' featuring floral patterns and scrollwork within a square frame.</image-desc>

Although MARTIN LUTHER’S name is widely praised, you, MARTIN BUCER, are to be considered no less than Luther on account of your piety, learning, labor, care, alertness, and writings. Bucer is a singular instrument of God, as was Luther: both Germans; the latter from Eisleben, the former from Sélestat: both formerly Monks; the latter an Augustinian, the former a Dominican. <note>Bucer was a member of the Dominican Order before converting to the Protestant cause.</note> Awakened by Luther’s voice—speaking boldly before the Emperor at Worms and preaching the Gospel—he was transformed from a Dominican into a Christian. How much labor he devoted to the expansion of Christ’s Kingdom is sufficiently witnessed by his surviving writings and by those who have read them and exercised themselves therein. What great judgment in writing, boldness in counsel, success in the building up of the Church, skill in settling and laying down disputes, and moderation in Disputation he possessed and utilized, his works and Church history abundantly show us. For although he was the Pastor of the Strasbourg Church and had taught Theology continuously for twenty years in that same City, nevertheless, the Churches of other Cities enjoyed this man’s presence, works, and diligence in public colloquies, assemblies, and gatherings. Through his unceasing labor and industriousness, the Kingdom of Christ rose and increased more and more daily. Oh, if only the dispute over the Lord’s Supper <note>The "Marburg Colloquy" and subsequent debates regarding the nature of the Eucharist.</note>—so pleasing to the Papists—between Zwingli and Luther, for the reconciliation of which he performed so much labor, could have been removed by him! And also that those of Cologne, after the frequent and earnest persistence of the Christian Archbishop Hermann von Wied, had accepted this man and had rooted out the capital city of Roman Idolatry among the Germans through this man’s godly services! Which might well have made progress, had the Westphalian Gropper <note>Johannes Gropper (1503–1559), a Catholic theologian and opponent of Bucer in Cologne.</note> remained sitting in his pig-sties, and had he not appeared in the court of the godly Prince.

<summary>A biographical tribute to the Reformer Martin Bucer, highlighting his transition from a Dominican monk to a leader of the Reformation in Strasbourg and his efforts to mediate theological disputes.</summary>
<keywords>Martin Bucer, Martin Luther, Reformation, Strasbourg, Cologne, Hermann von Wied, Johannes Gropper, Huldrych Zwingli, Eucharist, religious disputes</keywords>

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographical account of the reformer Martin Bucer, specifically focusing on his transition from the failed reformation in Cologne to his influential period in England under King Edward VI.</meta>

# MARTIN

who betrayed, imprisoned, and enticed the Archbishop and *Bucer*, with whom he had previously been in agreement, through the unusual delicacies of the Roman whore <note>A common Reformation-era polemic referring to the Roman Catholic Church</note>, which he swallowed so greedily that he could not digest them in his *Westphalian* stomach. That which this Archbishop of Cologne <note>Hermann von Wied (1477–1552), who attempted to reform Cologne but was deposed</note> most earnestly desired but could not accomplish, was carried out by the other Archbishop of Canterbury, *Thomas Cranmer*, Primate in England, a man most distinguished in godliness and learning. He applied all diligence to draw *Bucer* above all, and also *Paul Fagius* of Strasbourg, into England, to which end he wrote them many letters, all urging their arrival there. At that time *Edward* VI reigned, who from his youth was raised in many languages and learning, and well-instructed in godliness through the care provided by the great *Cranmer*. Truly (as the historian himself mentions) *Europe has not had a King of such great hope and expectation in many centuries.* That *Bucer*, who had now rejected the old Roman Colony (or Cologne), was received and embraced with great desire into the new Colony of Christ (or the expansion of His kingdom), namely England, in the spacious gallery of the Canterbury High School <note>referring to the University of Cambridge, where Bucer taught</note>, in the year [15]49. Where, having now taught with great attendance and great fruit for barely two years, he departed this world on the next-to-last of February in the year [15]51, after he had lived 61 years. And he was buried there most honorably and honored with the *Epitaphs* or funeral praises of many learned people. Nevertheless, five years later (after *Edward* had died and the *Marian* storm <note>The reign of Queen Mary I and her restoration of Catholicism</note> was then raging), his body was exhumed and burned to ashes in Canterbury <note>Bucer was actually buried and later exhumed/burned in Cambridge; the author likely conflates the location with Cranmer's see</note>. But four years after that, with *Elizabeth*, the *Most Fortunate among Princesses*, now reigning, the godless and *Marian Edicts* proclaimed against *Bucer* and all other godly men were nullified. The death of this most faithful man damaged the Christian Church not a little, as *Calvin* himself testifies; and how much he attributes to *Bucer* is not unknown to those who have read *Calvin's* writings. Among all, he writes in a letter to *Viret*: *The Church has suffered such immense damage through Bucer's death that as often as I think of it, I feel my heart tear apart.* From this, observer and reader, you may note what kind of man *Bucer* was.

<meta>Catchword: OF</meta>

<summary>This page details Martin Bucer's move to England under the patronage of Thomas Cranmer and King Edward VI, his death in 1551, the subsequent desecration of his remains under Mary I, and his posthumous rehabilitation under Elizabeth I.</summary>
<keywords>Martin Bucer, Thomas Cranmer, Edward VI, Paul Fagius, Elizabeth I, John Calvin, Pierre Viret, Canterbury, Cologne, Reformation, Marian Persecution, Westphalia</keywords>

->O iij<-

*Defense against the false slanders of Johann Gropper.* <note>Gropper was a Catholic theologian and former collaborator of Bucer who became a fierce opponent.</note>
*Against the request of Pope Paul III's Legates, and the same Pope's Bull conspired against the Evangelicals.*
*Godly exhortation to the Emperor, the Princes, and the Estates of the Empire assembled at Worms. On the earnest Reformation of the Churches.*
*Against the re-introduction of the Mass, and the other Sacraments and ceremonies of the Papists.*
*On the obstacles by which the Reformation is not permitted.*
*Against the Cologne Sophists.*
*On the colloquy at Regensburg.* <note>original: "Ratisbonen." The 1541 Diet of Regensburg was a major attempt to reconcile Catholics and Protestants.</note>
*The explanation of the 120th Psalm, to the Church of Bonn, together with the refutation of certain anonymous libelous booklets issued against the Godly Reformation and Bucer.*
*That the heavy temptations of our time are not to be wondered at, etc.*
*Refutations of the slanders by which a Munsterite free-spirited doctrine is attributed to the Ministers of the Church of Strasbourg.* <note>Bucer is defending his colleagues against accusations of being radical Anabaptists like those in the Münster Rebellion.</note>

The titles reflect the intense theological debates between Protestants and Catholics regarding church property, the role of magistrates, and the legitimacy of the Mass.</summary>

If all of this man's Latin and German Writings were joined together, they would amount to nine great volumes or parts.

From the aforementioned books, arguments, and titles, you can well perceive and note, beloved Reader, what learning, care, diligence, and experience, as well as skill in persuasion, and what prestige in Reforming and uniting the Churches, resided in Bucer.

## MARTIN BUCER.

**Your birth, Bucer, Germany boasts of greatly,**
**Happy to have you as a native son.**
**Who, and how great you were, your writings boast even more,**
**Showing the way as one tracks the earth.**

*Behold here Bucer’s face, honorable and diligent.*
*Much care and great labor make the man distinguished.*
*Through learning, habit, and order you are ever bold;*
*Great was this man in the eyes of the Great Calvin.*

# PAULUS FAGIUS. 57

-><image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'G' depicting a fountain or architectural structure within a garden or courtyard setting.</image-desc><-

**P**AULUS FAGIUS can in no way be separated from *Bucer*, for it was he who, by virtue of his office, was *Bucer’s* colleague, his traveling companion on the road, and his supporter in times of danger. Both of them, departing from the same city at the same time, traveled together to England, the most flourishing and fortunate island in the world. 

But this *Fagius*, although he was born in a humble hamlet—namely Rheinzabern, which is a small town in the Palatinate—and his means were very meager, acquired great knowledge through infinite labor and diligence. He overcame all his misfortunes and earned a great reputation through his wonderful experience and knowledge of the Hebrew language. He had learned this in his early years with great industry, partly from *Capnio* <note>Johannes Reuchlin, often referred to by the Greek form of his name, Capnio.</note> and later partly from *Capito*, a professor of that same Hebrew language in Strasbourg. In these languages, he progressed so much that it is believed no one more learned than FAGIUS had been found in many years prior. 

Because he was an eloquent and very learned man, he was called from Strasbourg to Heidelberg by the Elector Palatine (when he undertook to establish the Church), so that he might show others a certain way and means of spreading the teaching of the Gospel, which was happily begun there. But later (due to the Emperor’s Fortune, then triumphing sufficiently in the war of the *Interim* <note>The Augsburg Interim (1548), a temporary decree ordered by Emperor Charles V to integrate Protestants back into the Catholic Church.</note> or "in-between state"), it completely collapsed. 

At that time, the Church of Germany was in a very sorrowful state, while in England, the Church presented itself with a joyful face. While the Emperor was forcing that harmful *Interim* upon Germany, Roman Idolatry was meanwhile being driven out of England. Thus, these Doctors (pleasing the Emperor the least) were chosen by *Edward*, that most excellent King, and were very acceptable to nearly the entire Nobility, as well as the Common people. But those of Canterbury were only able to hear this learned theologian *Fagius* with wonder for a few months. For after traveling from Strasbourg, where he was a Pastor, to England with his companion, he passed away in November of that same year from a four-day fever, and

This is not so strange to those who, by some poisonous and pestilential teaching, poison the soul, and easily poison and kill the bodies of those whom they know will discover and abhor the poisonings and sorceries of the followers of Simon Magus. <note>A biblical figure associated with sorcery and heresy; the author implies the Reformers' enemies used both spiritual and physical "poison."</note> Thus, *Bucer's* fellow brother, companion, and assistant *Fagius* <note>Paul Fagius (1504–1549), a Hebrew scholar and Reformer who worked closely with Bucer.</note> felt the same *Marian* cruelty <note>Refers to the persecutions under Queen Mary I of England ("Bloody Mary").</note> against the Godly, not only in life but also after his death, as *Bucer* had experienced, and thus had everything in common with his *Bucer*. From the Hebrew language, these books were translated and printed by *Fagius*:

*Thisbetes Helia*, which book is called *Thisbi* by the author, named for the multitude of 712 words that are explained in this work. <note>A dictionary of post-biblical Hebrew by Elijah Levita.</note>

*Tobit* in Hebrew, recently sent over from the City of *Constantinople*.

Prayers in Hebrew, which the Jews use on their greatest Feast days, from which one can see some trace of

A book on the truth of the Faith, which a learned Jew published many years ago, to perfectly prove the Christian Faith.

<header>THOMAS</header>

# THOMAS CRAN-
# MER.

<image-desc>A large decorative square drop cap "O" containing ornate floral and vine-like motifs.</image-desc>

Among the English, the great THOMAS CRANMER, Archbishop of Canterbury, was distinguished in prestige, learning, godliness, and all manner of virtues. Since he was the Primate—or the first—of England, he was also the first in building up the Church of God, as befits an Archbishop, and was certainly not the least among them. With what diligence and industry, and also with what faithfulness he promoted and stood for the Evangelical cause <note>The "Evangelical cause" refers to the Protestant Reformation movements based on the Gospel</note>, is sufficiently evident and expressed throughout his entire life. Here you see a true Bishop or overseer of the Church of Christ, and an outstanding Shepherd, who rose up against the Roman *Antichrist* <note>A common Reformation-era epithet for the Pope</note> as if against a wolf and tyrant, having provided himself with all those weapons by which he intended to restrain and silence the same's cruelty, tyranny, and horrible howling. Under *Edward* VI, a very virtuous King, this most godly Archbishop *Cranmer* possessed great influence, as one who greatly advanced the building up of the Kingdom of Christ through the instruction and counseling of King *Edward*. And as fortunate as England was through King *Edward*, so it was also through Archbishop *Cranmer*; for through the one as the Shepherd of the People, and through the other as the Shepherd of the Lord's Flock, it flourished in both Learning and Godliness. Through the care and persistence of *Cranmer*, *Bucer, Fagius, Lasco, Martyr* <note>Refers to Martin Bucer, Paul Fagius, Johannes a Lasco, and Peter Martyr Vermigli, prominent continental Reformers invited to England</note> and many others were called to England from other lands, so that England—which seemed divided from the Roman world—might also be separated from the Papal Decrees and dominion, and would be subject to Christ as the head of His Church, but to the King as the defender of the Faith, lawfully—that is, according to the laws of God and the Realm. Now that this exceedingly good King has passed away, and that not without suspicion of poisoning, oh, what changes have befallen the affairs of England! For that happy and peaceful State was immediately followed by the raging *Marian* tempest <note>The reign of Queen Mary I, known for the restoration of Catholicism and the persecution of Protestants</note>, which so troubled the island surrounded by the Ocean Sea through the raging blasts of the Roman *Aeolus* <note>In mythology, Aeolus is the keeper of the winds; here it refers to the Pope inciting the "storm" of persecution</note>, mixing together Heaven and earth,

# T H O M A S
<note>Thomas Cranmer (1489–1556), a leader of the English Reformation and Archbishop of Canterbury.</note>

that nothing remained free nor unviolated, except only that which the **Antichrist** <note>A common Reformation-era epithet for the Pope.</note>, shut up in his seven-hilled Roman high castle, desired to be free—the King of that Storm. This same power also snatched **Cranmer**, a man entirely upright, kind-hearted, and peace-loving, and threw him into a terrible dungeon. And so in darkness, filth, and want, **Cranmer**, that English light, was deprived of the light for two and a half years. Finally, in London, he was taken up by the cruel flames of the fire, in the year 1556, on the 21st of March. An orthodox and very godly man, and a steadfast martyr, he was burned in the 68th year of his birth. And although his flesh was roasted by the flames, indeed quite consumed, and his bones brought to ashes, yet his heart (according to the writings of **Foxe** <note>John Foxe, author of 'Actes and Monuments' or 'Foxe's Book of Martyrs'.</note>) remained unviolated by the fire. What sort of man and how great a man **Cranmer** was is expressed to us by two excellent witnesses, namely **Andreas Osiander** and **Peter Martyr**. Which **Osiander** wrote thus to **Cranmer**, and that during the time of **Henry VIII**, in the preface to the *Harmony* or comparison of the Evangelists: *I love you both for what you have in common with others—the nobility of your lineage, the dignity of your appearance, the unbelievable sweetness of your manners, your uncommon learning, kindness, and generosity, which you show to all, but especially toward those who love learning—and even more for the inner heroic virtues of your spirit, such as are seldom found in this age. These have been found by me through clear reasoning, namely: wisdom, prudence, courage, temperance, justice, singular affection for the fatherland, exceeding loyalty to the most illustrious King, contempt for earthly things, burning affection for heavenly things, the truth of the Gospel, upright religion, as well as burning zeal for Christ's honor, and finally, an invincible spirit that bears all burdens.* But **Peter Martyr**, in the dedication or dedicatory letter of [his book on] the *Lord's Supper*, writes of him thus: *Whom else could I have chosen for the dedication of this my book, as a champion of the Evangelical truth and of this Sacrament of the Holy Lord's Supper, who would surpass you in godliness, learning, and steadfastness? Truly, no one. There is truly no one among the ancient Fathers whom you have not most diligently studied. There are no books, neither of the ancients nor of the moderns, in which I have not seen with my own eyes, noted by your own hands, everything that serves this entire disputation. Councils, Canons or Rules, the Pope's Decrees or institutions that pertain to this, you have brought together in the most principal parts through such great labor (as far as this matter is concerned); so that I, this...*

# CRANMER.

If I had heard it from others, I would not have easily believed it, had I not, as an eyewitness, found it to be so immediately. Consider now, pious observer and reader, what an excellent Shepherd those Roman Catholics—that is, the greedy, evil wolves—have snatched away from us.

Among his manifold burdens and labors, he composed and published these writings:
The Catechism of Christian Doctrine.
Ordinances of the Reformed Churches.
On the Institution of Ministers.
On the Sacrament of the Lord's Supper, treated with Luther. <note>Refers to Cranmer's evolving views on the Eucharist and his dialogue with Continental reformers.</note>
Defense of the Catholic or Universal Doctrine.
To the Proclaimers of Truth.
The Ecclesiastical Laws in the time of Edward. <note>King Edward VI, under whom Cranmer implemented major reforms.</note>
Against Gardiner’s Sermon. <note>Stephen Gardiner, Bishop of Winchester and Cranmer's primary theological rival.</note>
Five books on the Lord's Supper.
Ordinances or Common Collections from the Doctors in twelve books.
Christian Sermons.

<column-break/>

On the slanders of Richard Smith.
Refutations of Unwritten Verities. <note>A critique of traditions not found in Scripture.</note>
Two books on not marrying a brother’s wife. <note>Relates to the "Great Matter" of Henry VIII’s divorce from Catherine of Aragon.</note>
Against the Primacy or Supremacy of the Pope, two books.
Against the Pope’s Purgatory, two books.
Two books on Justification.
Godly Prayers.
Letters to Learned Men.
He wrote from prison:
Against the Sacrifice of the Mass.
Against the Adoration of the Bread. <note>Refers to the elevation and worship of the Host in the Catholic Mass.</note>
To Queen Mary and several others.
He corrected the English translation of the Bible in many places and provided it with prefaces.

## JOHANNES A LASCO, POLE <note>Jan Łaski (1499–1560) was a Polish nobleman and a significant figure in the European Reformation. He was invited to England by Archbishop Cranmer to help organize the "Stranger Churches" for Protestant refugees in London.</note>

# JOHANNES LASKI.

Here he presents himself to you, beholder: JOHANNES LASKI <note>original: "Ioannes Lascus"</note>, a Pole and a Lord of the Banner <term>Baender-Heere: a high-ranking nobleman entitled to lead men under his own banner</term>, brother of *Hieronymus Laski* (the Envoy of King *Ferdinand* to the *Turk* <note>Referring to the Ottoman Empire</note>), a man of understanding (as *Sleidanus* <note>Johannes Sleidanus, the historian of the Reformation</note> writes) most excellent. Observe this man of ours, eminent in body, in spirit, in learning, in godliness, and in many other Christian virtues: who, in order that he might proclaim Christ, left his entire fatherland; and this godly man preferred to spend his life in exile to preach God's grace, rather than to remain among his own people in favor and high esteem. Such love did he bear toward Christ and His flock, that he tracked down the scattered and wandering sheep—those who were either disturbed from their folds or snatched by the teeth of the devouring Roman wolf—and drew them to himself with a lovely voice, and fed them through the word of God, as becomes a faithful shepherd. Happy are those sheep who encountered their shepherd *Laski* in that time. For when they were wandering in foreign lands, having just escaped the tearing fangs of the hungry and gluttonous wolf, they would very easily have fallen among the thistles and thorns, or some other kind of horrible thieves and murderous snakes, had they not been brought over to the open, lovely pasture of God's word through the labor and care of the vigilant Shepherd *Laski*, and brought into order by a shepherd’s staff—that is, by the laws of Church discipline. Germany is a witness, as is England, which have sufficiently demonstrated the virtues of this shepherd to us. He was called to England by the eminent *Cranmer* <note>Thomas Cranmer, Archbishop of Canterbury</note>, Primate or first of the Kingdom, a learned, godly, and steadfast man: who so advocated for the Church of foreigners <note>The "Stranger Churches" in London for Protestant refugees</note> in that Kingdom, that he bore the abundant fruits of his labor through his godly teaching and good instruction. When the *Marian Tempest* <note>The persecution of Protestants under Queen Mary I of England, 1553–1558</note> would tolerate neither the native nor the foreign Christians, an exceedingly great multitude followed him by water and by land through many dangers, not knowing in what region they should settle. But when *Laski* chose Denmark, he [offered] their small kindness...

<summary>This page provides a biographical sketch of the Polish Reformer Johannes Laski (Jan Łaski), highlighting his noble origins, his leadership of refugee "Stranger Churches" in England under Thomas Cranmer, and his flight from the Marian Persecutions.</summary>
<keywords>Johannes Laski, Jan Łaski, Thomas Cranmer, Reformation, Poland, England, Marian Persecutions, Stranger Churches, religious exile</keywords>

--- Page 152 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographical account of the reformer Johannes a Lasco, specifically focusing on his theological contributions and his role in establishing the Dutch refugee church.</meta>

# JOHN

experienced [this] very shortly thereafter, as did some of the Lower Saxon cities, and that because of the differing teachings on the Lord's Supper; for he published a very clear treatise on the Lord's Supper (just as *Cranmer, the Archbishop* <note>Thomas Cranmer, Archbishop of Canterbury (1489–1556)</note> did at the same time), both of which they dedicated to King *Edward* <note>King Edward VI of England</note>. These two men were not the least among those who presented this doctrine of the Supper uprightly, clearly, and simply—indeed, in my opinion, better and more aptly than those who, by conceding too much for the sake of maintaining the Church's peace, have gained some praise in this matter. This so-driven *Lasco* was finally accepted by Emden, a city of no small renown in East Friesland: where he settled himself and taught the faithful Christians, especially the Netherlanders, with great loyalty and steadfastness. And in those turbulent times, Emden was like a refuge for the Netherland Christians, the first cradle or nursery of the Netherland Church: from which afterwards, as if from one great congregation, many others sprouted; and the Christian religion, like a fruitful Evangelical seed, spread and grew very strongly and abundantly throughout the Netherlands. Which city and its province so embraced and promoted the Christian religion that *Calvin*, moved by this zeal and love, dedicated his Catechism—such an excellent work—to them. Meanwhile, *Lasco*, requested and recalled by his own people, returned to his fatherland from which he had previously been cast out: in which, after he had taught for a short time, he—whose life was truly nothing but an exile—departed for Heaven, the true fatherland and the resting place of all the blessed. In the year 1560.

## JOHANNES LASCO THE POLE.

-> In peaceful times, England nurtured you gently, Lasco, <-
-> As a Nobleman who sincerely loved the Noble. <-
-> Who promoted godliness and brought it back to his land <-
-> Healthy, as your people's light and love, free and not lowly. <-

-> In exile, ungrateful Poland drives you out, <-
-> Your Fatherland; yet you are a lovely Teacher to God's exiles. <-
-> Though your country enlarges the World, Poland rises even higher above it, <-
-> Since there you love the World's Ruler even more highly in rich virtue. <-

<meta>This page continues the biographical sketches of prominent Reformers, focusing here on the Scottish leader John Knox.</meta>

# JOHN KNOX.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'S' depicting ornate scrolling foliage and floral patterns.</image-desc>

Scotland's light is here, JOHN KNOX, as one who, in the midst of the Papal—that is, in more than Egyptian—darkness, carried the Torch of God's word before his people and enlightened Scotland. In former times, he had a master in the midst of that darkness (not unknown), namely *John Major* <note>original: "Ioannem Majorem"</note> the Scotsman, very renowned for his subtlety and sharpness of mind: of whom many writings (presented for the display and boasting of his intellect) still remain. In this *Anthropotheology* <note>A term used here to describe a human-centered or scholastic approach to divinity</note> or "mingled godliness," *Knox* being very well practiced, taught the same with great prestige; and in many things he was considered more fortunate than his Master (if such sharpness of mind brings fortune). But earnestly reading through the writings of *Jerome* and *Augustine*, he found in them another Theology: whereupon he regarded that former sharpness of mind as mere dullness and madness, and embraced the simplicity which Christ so earnestly commanded us, as did Paul and the other Apostles. Finally, he felt that the excessive Scottish sharpness of mind was an enemy to both the world and the Gospel, and a Sophistical chatter unworthy of a Christian man. And so, driving from him all darkness, he acknowledged and worshipped Christ—that light shining in the darkness—and issued a very clear confession of Faith: which the mitered, shaven, and light-shunning Bats <note>A derogatory reference to the Catholic clergy, implying they hide from the "light" of the Reformation</note> sought to snap away with their foul jaws and wished to see extinguished. *Knox*, now beloved by many, was captured and destined for certain death. But, God willing it so, he was delivered by a singular means and crossed over into England, where he defended and championed the *Truth* everywhere with great service and fruit, through which *Knox* became very famous. But as *Edward VI* then ruled the English, who held this *Knox* in honor and accompanied him with his favor, a Bishopric was offered to him; *Knox*, being disturbed by this, not only refused and rejected that honor, but also with an earnest and stern oration spoke against that name or title, as having something in common with the *Antichrist*, which he would not allow in the Christian Church: [praising] godliness, modesty, and

<sig>Q iij</sig>

<meta>This page continues the biographical sketches of prominent Reformers, focusing here on the life and works of John Knox (Cnoxus) and his role in the Scottish Reformation.</meta>

# JOHN

and humility, while being very loving. And when the *Marian* Tempest <note>The "Marian Tempest" refers to the persecutions of Protestants in England under Queen Mary I (r. 1553–1558), often called "Bloody Mary."</note> arose at that time, he chose a more secure and stable place in Frankfurt, but finally found his harbor in Geneva. There, teaching and comforting his own, he rightfully performed his office and enjoyed the familiar company of *Calvin* <note>original: "Calvini"</note>. But when *Knox* <note>original: "Cnoxus"</note> returned to Scotland in the year 1559, it is unbelievable with what great wonder this man, awakened by the Holy Spirit, enlightened Scotland through the clarity of the Gospel. And although the French turmoils disturbed the Kingdom, and Satan wrought gruesome things in the innermost parts of the Church; nevertheless, having overcome, he remained standing through his courage in that clear, appearing light. Therefore, he is rightly called here the light of Scotland, who proclaimed Christ, the light of the world, in such thick darkness. In order that this light might always be preserved and held in honor by the Scots, he considered that godliness, modesty, humility, and other Christian virtues must be loved and actively practiced: which also requires Church Discipline <term>Church Discipline: the system of oversight and moral correction used by the Reformed church to maintain order and holiness</term> as the best proponent of that same light. Which the Scots even today (thanks to *Knox's* care) maintain entirely, wholly, and perfectly. Which *Knox*, leaving Scotland, departed toward that light of Christ proclaimed by him, on the 24th of December in the year 1572. <note>The text says 26th of December, though historical records usually cite November 24th.</note>

He left behind these writings:
*To those of London and others.*
*To the proclaimers of the Gospel.*
*How one ought to pray.*
*On the Psalm, dedicated to his mother.*
*Against the Papist Mass.*
*The doctrinal or instruction of the Mass.*
*Of the faith of the Lord's Supper.*
*To the distressed Churches.*

*To Mary, Queen of Scots.*
*Counsel in these present Distresses.*
*The first blast of the trumpet.* <note>original: "Des hoornes eerste gheblaes," referring to his famous polemic "The First Blast of the Trumpet Against the Monstruous Regiment of Women."</note>
*Appeal or revocation of the sentence of the clergy.*
*To his countrymen.*
*Sermon on Genesis and some others.*

### JOHN KNOX, SCOT.

The Scottish congregation, Knox, first heard your teaching,
And through your advancement was brought into its true being.
To that end, above all, your godliness and heavenly doctrine drew them,
And your praised love for the true <term>Religion</term> <note>The term "Religion" here specifically refers to the Reformed or Protestant faith as opposed to Roman Catholicism.</note>.

*Just as the night-ravens hover through the darkness of night,*
*And the bats flee from the clear dawn of day;*
*So also were the dark Sophists <note>original: "Sophisten"; a derogatory term used by Reformers to describe scholastic theologians who used complex logic to defend traditional Church doctrines.</note> driven away*
*By Knox out of Scotland, truly acting as the light of his fatherland.*

# ANDREAS HYPE-
# RIUS.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial letter 'O' inside a square frame, featuring intricate floral and scrollwork designs.</image-desc>

Among all the Netherlanders, **ANDREAS HYPERIUS** stands here as a select theologian, whom Ypres—a renowned and powerful city in Flanders—brought forth to us. His father was a jurist. In our *Andreas*, much is remarkably evident to behold: namely courage, virtue, intellect, studiousness, erudition, and experience in many matters, all of which present us with his singular praise. When he was a youth, he grasped—not without the wonder of many—through his outstanding and bold intellect and infinite labor, the most excellent languages and literature, as well as the <term>Encyclopedia</term> <note>original: "Encyclopedie"; here meaning the full circle of universal knowledge or the "perfection" of all arts</note> of almost all arts. This was achieved through the constant maintenance and company of Joachim Ringelberg of Antwerp, a young man wonderfully skilled in all kinds of arts and, if I may say so, experienced to the point of a miracle, who praised *Hyperius* in an excellent, still-extant oration to the Council of Paris. After he had studied in Paris and Leuven, he traveled through Italy, France, Germany, Spain, and England. Finally, having been appointed as a Professor in the Academy or High School of Marburg, he settled there; the school enjoyed his presence first in Philosophy and thereafter in Theology as a select Doctor. How deeply experienced he was in all Philosophy is expressed by his own writings: above all, the very brief, clear, and learned exposition of the *Ethics* <note>original: "Ethica"</note>, or the manner of living virtuously, by Aristotle. This work is very pleasing and useful to read for those studying Theology as well as Philosophy, which he read aloud to his disciples in the same Academy. With what diligence, faithfulness, steadfastness, and how fruitfully he publicly taught upright Theology is clearly demonstrated to us by his labor and life, as well as by the eulogies attributed to him by renowned men; he was one well-instructed in the arts which are commonly called the handmaidens of Theology. This man's writings, on account of their appropriate division and singular organization, teach clearly, and through their eloquence and elegance of words, greatly move and enlighten the reader; so much so that he also the

<summary>This page provides a biographical account of Andreas Hyperius, a Flemish theologian and professor at Marburg, highlighting his vast education under Joachim Ringelberg and his influential commentaries on Aristotle and Theology.</summary>

# ANDREAS

considered very beneficial for Scholastics and Preachers. What *Melanchthon* was in *Saxony*, *Hyperius* was in *Hesse*: he who was very experienced in both political and spiritual histories, the Church Fathers, and also in the writings of the Scholastics and Sophists <note>Sophists here refers to academic philosophers of the Middle Ages</note>. Yet he was mostly an exceedingly diligent reader of the Father *Chrysostom* <note>John Chrysostom, an early Christian Church Father known for his eloquence</note>, with whose sayings, parables, and divisions he frequently adorned and confirmed his fine books. Because of this, his writings are highly esteemed by those who wish to teach many people; also this man's modesty, patience, and steadfastness are viewed by many with great praise. Which his books also testify, in which a spirit appears free from those affections or inclinations that commonly lead men astray, as he also writes of himself in the Treatise on the Investigation of Holy Scripture, never to cease: *This is my art, this is my armor of war, these my war-councils, through which I protect myself against my encroaching enemies, withstand them, and commonly overcome them: I* **KEEP SILENT**, *I* **ENDURE**, *I* **HOPE**. This is the praise of *Hyperius*, who is praised as a never-to-be-fully-prized Theologian by a certain Theologian of no small name. This man, worthy of praise in all times, died in the Year of Christ 1564, the first of February, at the age of 54, after he had taught at *Marburg* for 22 years. Of which this little verse, being short and elegant, expresses the year of his passing:

->*Flandria quem genuit Hassia nostra tegit.*<-
<note>original Latin: "Flanders bore him, our Hesse covers him."</note>
->That is:<-
->*Whom Flanders brought forth, the Hessian earth covers.*<-

He is buried in the same place where his very great friend lay while he lived, *Gerardus Noviomagus* <note>Gerard Geldenhouwer, a Dutch humanist and historian</note>, according to the same gravestone at *Marburg*:

->*Here rest the bones of Gerard of Nijmegen:*<-
->*As also those of Andreas Hyperius opposite them.*<-

### ANDREAS GERARDUS HYPERIUS.

**Not Ypres, Hyperi, formerly your fatherland,**
**Gave you the name that you received much earlier from God:**
**Much less did light fate cast such a name to you by hand,**
**But Virtue, *Andrea*, names you according to her full decree.** <note>The poet plays on the Greek word 'Andreia' (manly virtue/courage) and the subject's name, Andreas.</note>

*Manly virtue, Andrea, gave the name strongly,*
*Which womanish Rome considers weak and adulterous.*
*The city of Ypres, fertile in men, gave this one from her bounds*
*As a mighty man: because of which he now lives peacefully.*

# WOLFGANG MUSCULUS.

> *Such was the face of Wolfgang Musculus in days of old,* <note>Wolfgang Musculus (1497–1563) was a German theologian who converted from Benedictine monasticism to the Reformed faith, eventually becoming a professor in Bern.</note>
> *From whom unholy Rome herself feared for her safety.* <note>original: "Roma prophana." This refers to the Roman Catholic Church, which the Protestant Reformers often characterized as "profane" or worldly.</note>
> *As he feared not the swift talons or the predatory cats,* <note>original: "Illius vt rapidos unques, catos que rapaces." This is a clever pun on the subject's surname. In Latin, *Musculus* means "little mouse." The poet suggests that unlike a normal mouse, this "Little Mouse" did not fear the "cats" (his opponents in Rome).</note>
> *So, in turn, was he himself a man to be feared.*

->With privilege.<- <note>original: "Cum priuill." This is an abbreviation for the Latin *Cum privilegio*, indicating that the printer had been granted a legal monopoly or official license to publish the work.</note>
