# BULLINGER. 126

*Recently, when Death covered the eyes of BULLINGER in a long night,*
*stiffening his limbs with a sweet sleep:*
*The troubled HOMELAND <note>original: PATRIA; referring to Zurich or Switzerland</note> veiled its mourning face:*
*And was entirely dissolved into pious tears.*
*Alas, where shall I turn? she said: he has fallen, in whom alone*
*all our hope and safety was hitherto fixed.*
*For we commit to the tomb one, than whom no other*
*bore the glittering standards of Christ more unyieldingly in this age.*
*He indeed rests, composed in placid peace:*
*But a wide field of cares lies open before me.*
*Momus <note>The Greek god of mockery and criticism; here used to represent Bullinger's detractors</note>, why do you snarl? Put away your shame along with your brow:*
*Let neither hour nor place be free from your insults:*
*Yet no oblivion shall ever wear away this man’s praised name,*
*and what manner of man he was, the aged parchment shall speak.*

->That is:<-

*Lately, where Death covered Bullinger’s eyes with a long night,*
*and stiffened his limbs through such sweet sleep:*
*The troubled Fatherland wrapped its sorrowful countenance:*
*And in godly tears it almost overflowed.*
*Ah, where do I remain? it said: He has fallen, who stood firm,*
*in whom was our hope and the general welfare.*
*For we carry to the Grave him who, before all others, held in glow*
*the radiance of Christ in the waves of his light during this Time.*
*Therefore he rests now in the peace of the eternal truth:*
*But the place of my cares has spread itself wide.*
*Momus, what do you mock? Bold one, diminish your shame:*
*And let neither hours nor places be covered by your stains of disgrace.*
*For the great praise of this name, no forgetfulness shall*
*wear away or vanish: but ancient Writings shall express it.*
*Thus spoke the Fatherland, where it had to bow under God.*

# JOSIAS SIMLER.

<image-desc>Decorative initial 'G' featuring a coat of arms with a central lion-like figure and floral ornaments.</image-desc>Just as the Swiss are greatly praised for their piety and godliness, so too are those of Zurich to be highly commended among them: who, as they are the first among the Confederates <note>The Swiss Confederacy</note>, have also proven themselves not to be the last in the earnestness of virtue. In many ways, Zurich is fortunate and renowned. But the labor, virtue, godliness, and learning of select men brought into the same have made this city abundantly and very famous: who, as they are citizens of Zurich and there in great numbers, one may truly say of them that from the city of Zurich—as if from the Trojan Horse—many more have descended into Italy to contend with those who consider their lineages to have come from the Trojans and Romulus (reared by the She-wolf), than from many other cities that are well-known and widely famed regarding Evangelical teaching. Among those of Zurich, JOSIAS SIMLER is to be considered as not among the least: who, through his published writings, has made not only his own name but also the names of others very great, and especially those of Zurich, whose names and works he points out to us in his *Bibliotheca* <note>referring to his work 'Bibliotheca Instituta', a massive universal bibliography</note>. He was a Professor of Theology in the Zurich School, as well as an excellent defender and champion of the glory and honor of Christ: which his printed books against the Arians, Nestorians, Macedonians, Tritheists, Samosatenians, and Eutychians <note>various groups deemed heretical by the Reformed church for their views on the nature of the Trinity or Christ</note> demonstrate to us. With what great learning and judgment he was gifted, we can well understand from those same books published against the new Polish Arians and German Eutychians. In addition, he was an exquisite mathematician and a very outstanding historian, who with great diligence and industriousness described not only Switzerland, but also the state and government of the Swiss community. He acquired a widely-famed name for himself through the publication of the books on the *Swiss Republic*: in which the Swiss histories and the deeds performed both inside and outside the country, from the reigns of Rudolph to Charles V, are recounted. Which books are so greatly desired and read, that they are also in...

It emphasizes Zurich's role as a center for Reformation learning and Simler's defense of orthodoxy against various heresies.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the introductory matter or dedication of a work concerning Josiah (Josias), likely focusing on civic virtue and the preservation of liberty through historical examples.</meta>

-> J O S I A S <-

translated into the languages of many different peoples. These books stir the minds of readers to advocate for serious matters and to embrace the best of good and upright liberty: which, when used and possessed according to just laws, is to be held as a priceless thing and above all value. Therefore, it must also (according to the sentiment of all good Statesmen <note>original: "Politicquen"; referring to those skilled in political science or governance</note>) be advocated for and maintained with every power of virtue and well-doing. Just as we have seen fit to set down here our own steady judgment on this matter—taken from the Preface to those of St. Gall <note>A city in Switzerland known for its Reformation history</note>—for the sake of all who love true Liberty, in these words: *And if in any way our century fails, and the very best and most moderate manner of Reasoning in our Community-Government is departed from, such is to be attributed to our defects, we who do not retain the Customs of the ancestors nor the institutions of the most prominent men. And oh, that we might complain unjustly, as Africanus <note>Scipio Africanus the Younger, a Roman general and statesman</note> does in Cicero's writings in this manner: Our age, he says, having received our Community like a beautiful or well-made Painting—though one faded by the length of time—has not only neglected to renew it with the same colors as before, but has not even cared that its unique form or outer outline should remain or be preserved. What remains of the ancient customs, in which he said the Roman state consisted, which we see so diminished through forgetfulness that they are not only not loved, but are themselves unknown? For good customs have perished for lack of men: for which great evil we must not only give an account, but also, as if having forfeited our lives, it is permitted to declare the cause thereof in some way; for through our faults, and not by any chance, we have the Republic in name, but in deed we have long since lost it.* I have wished to include the words of this Steady man here, not because I consider our affairs to have reached the ultimate extreme, but as it appears that good customs have sufficiently decayed, it must above all be prevented—and provided for in time by men of understanding—that matters do not lapse into a worse state, bringing upon us the same fate which Cicero rightly predicted for the Romans: who, having abandoned the old customs and men, lost both the Republic and their freedom together. But so that we may retain the Old customs and improve those that have fallen, the pious deeds and institutions of the Ancients must always be set before our eyes and well-confirmed, so that they become known to everyone. Of this, Zwingli <note>Huldrych Zwingli (1484–1531), a leader of the Reformation in Switzerland</note> and other Swiss men have also spoken and written no less godlily than excellently: among whom—

# SIMLER

The number of those whom I remember to have spoken gravely on this matter is small—namely, concerning the decline of ancient Swiss customs, which are considered better the older they are. But I return to our Simler, whose studies and labors so delighted him that he sought in them the solace and remedy for his ills and sicknesses. For although he was greatly tormented by two violent tyrants, namely the Stone <note>kidney stones</note> and the Gout <note>original: "Flercijn," a historical term for gout or aching joints</note>, he was nevertheless not overcome by the pain and torment. Indeed, even when pressed and as if bound by the "stone" in both hands and feet, he considered himself much happier than those who, seated like a king upon the heights of the Tarpeian Rock <note>A cliff in ancient Rome used for executions; here used as a metaphor for precarious worldly power</note> and exerting power like a mighty hunter, are quite swift and ready for robbery and plundering. When he was about 45 years old, he completed his life; which, if one looks at the pain, was a cross to him, but looking at his labor, it was a delight. In the year of our Lord 1576. Upon him, as a true zealot for godliness and the fatherland's freedom, this Epigram is read:

> O happy Zurich, O happy in this seed, the people
> To whom he restored both Christ and the Fatherland so greatly.
<note>original Latin: "Felix o Tigurum, o felix hoc semine, genti / Quo tantâ & Christum reddidit & Patriam."</note>

->That is:<-

> O happy Zurich, O happy people of this seed
> Which poured forth Christ and the Fatherland like a cloud.

---

### THESE BOOKS Simler wrote:

On the Eternal Son of God our Lord Jesus Christ, against the new Arians, Tritheists, and the Samosatenians <note>various groups deemed heretical by the reformed church for their views on the Trinity</note>, in 3 books.
Likewise against the same concerning the Holy Spirit, 1 book.
The account of the old disputes concerning the Person and the two Natures in Christ our Savior, together with a collection of the disputes of our time,

<column-break/>

printed with the Latin writings of the Ancients concerning these same divisions, which were overseen by him and explained with notes and joined together in one book. The writings of the Ancients are these: The Edict of the Emperor Justinian concerning the true faith. Boethius on the two natures in Christ. 7 Books of Cassian on the Incarnation. The writings of Maxentius. Two letters of Pope Leo II. The book of Pope Gelasius against Eutyches and Nestorius. 3 Books of Fulgentius to

# JOSIAS 2

->King Thrasimund. Five books by Vigilius against Eutyches <note>Vigilius of Thapsus was a 5th-century bishop who wrote against the Eutychean heresy, which denied the human nature of Christ.</note>. The Dialogue of Rusticus the Deacon.<-
The answer of Christ our Mediator, against the cursed book of Franciscus Stancari <note>Stancari was an Italian theologian whose views on Christ's mediation caused significant controversy in the Reformation.</note>.
Scriptural explanation of the true human nature of Christ's presence here on earth.
His own answer to the two Disputations of Andreas Musculus.
Answer to Jacobus Andrea <note>Jakob Andreae was a primary author of the Lutheran Formula of Concord.</note> made in the common name.
The Second Answer to Jacobus Andrea.
Confirmation of the two natures in the single person of Christ our Savior, against Simon Budnaeus <note>Szymon Budny, a Polish-Belarusian reformer associated with Unitarianism.</note>.
Lessons on Exodus.
A Dialogue, through which it is disputed whether one ought to pray with uncovered head at the stroke of the clock at midday and in the evening.
Oration on the life and death of the very renowned Theologian Peter Martyr <note>Peter Martyr Vermigli (1499–1562), a highly influential Italian-born Reformed theologian.</note>, to which are added some of his writings on the Lord's Supper, not previously printed.
The life of the widely-famed Physician and Philosopher Conrad Gesner <note>Gesner was a monumental figure in natural history and bibliography; Simler was his close friend and biographer.</note>.
Account of the Origin, Life, and Death of the Reverend Man Dr. Heinrich Bullinger <note>Bullinger was the successor to Zwingli in Zurich and a key figure in the Reformation.</note>: in which are included the principal things that occurred in the Swiss Church.
Has furthermore published Peter Martyr’s explanations of the

<column-break/>

Second book of Samuel: Likewise on Genesis, and his prayer-book from the Psalms.
Has translated from the German language into Latin Heinrich Bullinger’s six books against the errors of the Anabaptists.
Instruction for those who are examined concerning the Faith by the Inquisitors.
Summary of the Christian Religion.
Exhortation to all Church Ministers, to the end that they love unity.
On Persecutions.
Likewise Otto Werdmüller’s Paraphrastic explanation of the Our Father <note>The Lord's Prayer</note>, and of the Apostolic Creed.
Has besides this written:
Two books on the principles of Astronomy.
Notes on the Cosmography of Aethicus <note>A late-antique geographical text.</note>, who seems to have read around the time of Theodosius.
Likewise on Antoninus’s Way-finder or Travel-book <note>The *Itinerarium Antonini*, a register of stations and distances along the roads of the Roman Empire.</note>, Rutilius, and Sequestre.
Two Books on the Swiss Republic <note>Simler's *De Republica Helvetiorum*, a foundational text on the Swiss Confederation.</note>.
The History of Switzerland, which he, being prevented by his death, did not complete.
Valesia <note>The history of the Valais region in Switzerland.</note>, which is, etc. in two books.
The Description of the people of Basel, Tulingers, Latobrogians, etc. in two books.
A book on the Lepontii <note>An ancient Alpine tribe.</note>, in which is treated the Alpine mountains and the roads of those same mountains.
The names of the Numbers, the Weights <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword indicates the next word is "weights".</note>

# RUDOLF
## GWALTHER.

That Diligence is the mother of Virtue and Learning, and that this same quality is entirely characteristic of those from *Zurich*, but especially among them **RUDOLF GWALTHER**, his own labors and works can confirm for us. This man, being very well instructed in the liberal arts and sufficiently experienced in the *Greek* and *Latin* tongues, as well as being as good an *Orator* <note>original: "Oratoor"</note> as a *Poet*; has progressed more fortunately in that study which far surpasses all others <note>referring to Theology</note>, than those who, with unwashed hands, as they say, lay hold of and maltreat *God's Holy Word* (as we occasionally see). And just as this man is to be honored for the uprightness of his life, the gravity of his manners, and the dignity of his Office, so is he also to be loved in respect of his learning. He has been a very faithful Shepherd of the *Zurich Church* for over 40 years, in which Office he so loved that holy labor, that not only *Zurich*, but also those abroad and far-removed have experienced what a faithful teacher and good preacher *Gwalther* was. His *Homilies* or *Sermons* on nearly all the books of the *Prophets* as well as the *Apostolic* and *Evangelical Writings* <note>The Epistles and Gospels of the New Testament</note> clearly enough demonstrate his great labor and excellent learning, through which many *Christians* by reading have been built up in the faith, and the Shepherds of many churches far and wide have been assisted and served: for which some have so esteemed and emulated *Gwalther* that they call him their *Father* and *Teacher*: who, as they were slower or less ready in their studies, have taken their refuge in him as if in a *Statue of Mercury* <note>original: "Mercurii Beelt"; a classical metaphor for a guide who points the way, as Mercury was the god of eloquence and travelers</note> (pointing out the way of learning well and speaking well). And who shall not say that the over-great work of his fine *Homilies* is worthy to be placed in the holy *Library*? In the printing of which the *Froschovers* <note>The famous Froschauer printing family of Zurich</note>, on account of the over-great labor, are worthy of boastful honor: where he applied nearly all his labor and diligence in the printing of *Zurich* books. So much so that in the *Froschover Printing-house* more books by *Zurich* citizens [were printed] than by various other authors in any other through

# RODOLPHVS <note>Rudolph Gualther (1519–1586), a leading Reformed theologian in Zurich and successor to Heinrich Bullinger.</note>

...renowned cities have been published through his press. And since *Froschoverus* <note>Christoph Froschauer (c. 1490–1564), the famous printer of the Zurich Bible.</note> has brought forth mostly theological writings for us, the Inquisitors of heretical wickedness (as they call it) have not without reason scratched him out of their holy Catholic registers; for they had an evil eye and no small suspicion regarding the printer's mark on his books, which depicts a little child seated upon a *frog* <note>original: "Vorsch" (frog). This is a pun on the printer's name, Froschauer.</note> and governing it with a bridle. This has mocked and greatly disturbed the Babylonian whore who sits and triumphs upon her seven-headed beast. This croaking frog, living and refreshing itself in the Zurich Lake or Mere, has many times with a full sound called out to and shamed that thieving and devouring *Wolf* of the Tiber <note>A reference to the Pope in Rome, situated on the Tiber River.</note>. Just as *Froschover* shall live through *Gualtherus*, so shall *Gualtherus* live through *Froschover*. They have also made other learned and notable writings famous beyond the theological ones: such as the *Onomasticon* or Vocabulary of *Julius Pollux*, and the four *Apologies* or Answers and Orations of *Johannes Cantacuzenus*, the King of Constantinople, against the Mahometan <note>Muslim</note> faith, all translated into Latin. From these, one can sufficiently observe how sternly he behaved against the Antichristian forces, and above all against that notable *Antichrist at Rome* who sits in the Temple of God, whom the ready and laboring hand of *Gualtherus* has depicted to the life for us through a special treatise. When he had testified to his Church and the Christian people that he had lived justly, he was accepted into that Church which is in Heaven. He died in the 74th year of his age, and in the year of the last times,

1586.

# LUDWIG LAVATER <note>Ludwig Lavater (1527–1586) was a prominent Swiss Reformed theologian and the Archdeacon of Grossmünster in Zurich. He was a prolific writer and the son-in-law of Heinrich Bullinger.</note>

->*While others overturn statues, altars, and Rome;*<-
->**LAVATER**, *you put to flight the ghosts and dark spirits.* <note>original: "Spectra, nigros Lemures." This is a direct reference to Lavater's most famous book, "De spectris, lemuribus et magnis atque insolitis fractionibus" (1570), which argued from a Protestant perspective that "ghosts" were actually demons or delusions rather than souls from Purgatory.</note><-
->*You are sincere. You chase away the dark monsters, the mockeries of the Pope,* <note>original: "Candidus es." The poet plays on the word "Candidus," which means "white," "bright," or "sincere," contrasting it with the "dark" (nigra) monsters of superstition.</note><-
->*Who so often plays with false images.*<-

<vocab><term>Ludwig Lavater</term>: the subject of the portrait; <term>Specters</term>: apparitions or ghosts; <term>Lemures</term>: in Roman mythology, the restless spirits of the dead, here used to mean malevolent ghosts; <term>The Pope</term>: used here as a metonym for the Roman Catholic Church; <term>Images</term>: refers both to physical idols and the "false visions" of Purgatory.</vocab>

# LUDWIG
## LAVATER.

<image-desc>Decorative initial 'I' featuring intertwining floral and vine patterns.</image-desc>

If anyone is to be considered outstanding in learning, integrity, honor, and labor, it was this LUDWIG LAVATER <note>Ludwig Lavater (1527–1586) was a Swiss Reformed theologian and a key figure in the Zurich church.</note>, who had as a companion *Heinrich Bullinger*: both were one in studies and in spirit; he loved unity and goodwill wonderfully: therefore, such a nature in a companion always maintains friendship. This man translated many of *Bullinger's* writings, partly from *Latin* into *High German*, and again from *High German* into the *Latin* language. Teaching in the Church and the School, he advanced and strengthened the Lord's flock, as well as the youth—the nursery of the congregation—with great faithfulness and care. His labors and night-watches made his fine name renowned from time to time. Among the many writings and notable books of interpretation that *Lavater* published, the most well-known is the History, origin, and growth of the disputes arisen on the matter of the Lord's Supper: which he later expanded upon, with *Stumpf* <note>Johann Rudolf Stumpf, a Swiss historian and theologian.</note> as the commentator, a learned and very good man. Most famous above all these was the Treatise on Ghosts and Night-spirits <note>original: "van Spoocken en Nacht-geesten"; referring to his 1569 work *De spectris...*, a major Reformation-era text on the supernatural.</note>, which little book has been frequently reprinted in *Latin*, *High German*, *Dutch*, *French*, and *Italian*, and has been read with great eagerness, especially by the common man, who is curious and therefore very inclined to read this. Truly, this little book on Ghosts first made *Lavater's* fine name famous: and it unsettled that Father of Shadows, the Pope—who so often deceives simple folk with his false apparitions—more than those who have investigated and disputed much regarding the Pope's false power over Souls and Hell. By uncovering the tricks of this Roman "Bogeyman" <note>original: "Bitebaus," a nursery monster or bogeyman used to frighten children.</note> who changes into many forms, *Lavater* achieved so much that all those Ghosts, Night-spirits, and idle childish terrors have come to be despised by Christians (armed with the shield of Faith). Although this teaching of the Pope regarding Ghosts and Night-spirits, as well as the manner of...

->LUDWIG<-
XXVIII.

to reconcile those souls, which in the Roman practice—or rather the Chancery of *Simon the Sorcerer* <note>original: "Simons des Toovenaers"; a reference to Simon Magus and the sin of Simony, or buying church offices</note>—was the most profitable or money-making endeavor. For the Pope, acting as the Lord of Purgatory and ruling over a kingdom floating in ghosts and shadows, either reconciles the soul through his mediation by means of his "magic masses," or recalls it from the deep darkness and places it in Heaven—specifically those souls by whose shadows the living are tormented. In exchange for this, they hand over a "golden bough" <note>A classical allusion to Virgil's Aeneid, where a golden bough allows entry to the underworld</note> to the master of shadows; by which means everything stood open and was easily accomplished for both the dead and the living. The very nerve of this Pope’s primary power—namely the income that Purgatory richly provides—you, *Lavater*, first loosened and then paralyzed. This expeller of the Pope’s ghosts and Shepherd of Christ’s flock, after he had endured the struggle against the principal Roman Wolves with a steadfast and strong spirit, ascended to Heaven (where the righteous, purified by the blood of Christ, aspire to be) in the year 1586.

*141 Homilies on the book of Job.*

### LUDWIG LAVATER

*A rectangular frame contains a very faint, ghosted portrait of a man, likely Ludwig Lavater. The image appears as a faded print or bleed-through from the reverse side of the paper.*

While others overturn Images and Altars, and Rome itself;
You, Lavater, drive away Phantoms and Night-spirits.
White are you, who banishes black monsters and the Pope’s mocking-doctrines,
Who plays falsely with his images as if at children's feasts.

Of Hercules' lineage is Lavater to be esteemed,
Since he tames the Night-phantoms and gruesome Monsters:
The Pope’s vain shadows are a mockery, the many sorrows of the nights,
Also the heaps of Idols he drives away in countless numbers.

## THEODORE BEZA OF VÉZELAY <note>original: "THEODORVS BEZA VEZELIVS," identifying Beza by his place of birth in Burgundy, France.</note>

# THEODORE BEZA

Behold the venerable face of this most worthy man. But you wonder, Viewer, that BEZA is counted here among the dead. Yet wonder even more at BEZA having become living again; for according to the Jesuits' publication of five years ago, he had not only passed away as a mortal, but as a Roman Catholic, as one who had placed all his hope of Salvation in that Church of which the Pope is held as the head. <note>The Jesuits famously spread a false rumor in 1597 that Beza had died and converted to Catholicism on his deathbed; Beza responded by writing a witty poem proving he was still alive and still Protestant.</note> But above all, Viewer, be amazed at that breed of men who, in deceiving and lying (excepting that master of a thousand tricks, the Devil), are the highest among all. But let those also have a place here, to whom gray old age, immense learning, a renowned name, and other qualities—which he does not share with the rest—ascribe worth and glory. The artist's diligent hand has been applied here, which has expressed BEZA after the life <note>original: "naer d'leven," meaning the portrait was drawn from a living model</note> quite skillfully. This man, famous throughout the whole world, has wonderfully glorified Christ's Kingdom through his virtue, godliness, writings, and holy labor. While still a youth, he was very learned in the fine arts (beyond the capacity of his years); in the Latin and Greek languages, and especially in Poetry (as his surviving writings sufficiently testify), he yielded to almost no one in his time. As his master he had at Orleans, and thereafter at Bourges, *Melchior Wolmar* <note>original: "Meliorem Volmarium"</note>, a High-German, a godly and learned man who was especially well-experienced in the Greek language. To him, in the year 1560, he dedicated the *Confession of his Faith* <note>original: "Belijdenisse zijns Gheloofs"</note> in honor of him as his master and virtually his Father. Before this *Confession of his Faith*, he added a narrative of his earlier life and studies. He attained the degree of Licentiate (as it is called) in Civil or burger law. And as he had two uncles on his father's side—one of whom was a Councilor in Paris, and the other the Abbot of Froimont in France—they each greatly desired their BEZA, being then still a youth, for his excellence. Indeed, even the Abbot, whose Abbey [was worth] over five thousand...

<summary>A biographical tribute to the Reformer Theodore Beza, debunking Jesuit rumors of his deathbed conversion and detailing his early education under Melchior Wolmar.</summary>
<keywords>Theodore Beza, Melchior Wolmar, Jesuits, Reformation, Orleans, Bourges, Paris, Confession of Faith, legal studies, Catholic-Protestant polemics</keywords>

# THEODORE

<margin>Theodore Beza</margin>
...had an income of French crowns per year, he placed and designated this nephew in his thoughts and favor as his successor. At which time, being burdened by two "fat" benefices <note>A "fat benefice" refers to a high-paying ecclesiastical post with few duties</note> and being entirely ignorant of these matters, *Beza* was well provided for by friends, easy days, and money. But through that vain luster and that deceitful flattery, he felt the Devil was laying snares for him. Because of this, mindful of true godliness and of his teacher, and remembering the change of his former life, he fell into a severe illness, which became the beginning of true health. Being now restored, to speak briefly, he abandoned all of that and betook himself to Geneva (as a haven for the godly). In the year 1548, and shortly thereafter to Lausanne, where he was called by the Lords of Bern as Professor of the Greek language in the High School <term>High School: here meaning a university or academy</term>. And from there (after ten years) he returned again to Geneva, as if to his accustomed harbor, dedicating himself entirely to Theology, where for so many years he has endured the office of a faithful Shepherd and Doctor with great labor and fruit for his Church. His many and well-researched writings have made him famous, but especially the *Translation* of our Lord’s New Testament and the diligent and earnestly constructed Annotations <note>original: "Annotatien"</note> worked upon therein have made him most renowned and immortal. May God grant that this thoroughly practiced and exquisite Theologian, now 84 years old—that Christian *Nestor* <note>A reference to the wise, long-lived counselor from Greek mythology</note> and observer and witness of so many things—might one day see his France liberated from Papal Tyranny and boast happily of it. And so that you, beloved observer, may remember and know who in France, as also in other Kingdoms and lands, are the diligent servants and supporters of the tyranny of the Pope and the King of Spain, and are found to be not only the most cunning but also the boldest and coarsest in lying, I have wished to place below for your benefit the Letter and the *Epigram* sent by *Master Beza* to *Clement Puteanus, Jesuit*.

->B E Z A<-

# THEODORE BEZA wishes a better understanding, and at the very least a less shameless and lying conscience, to CLEMENT PUTEANUS <note>Latin: Clemens Puteanus; likely a reference to Jacques Laing, a Catholic polemicist</note>, General Father of the false Jesuit and half-monkish sect residing at Dole in Burgundy.

*WHether your hand-filling <note>likely referring to a bribe or a collection of writings</note>, Puteanus, does a great injustice (which certainly does not resemble the truth), it would be more fitting for your own little brothers whom you skip over, or you have brought such great shame upon them that they should be held as such stinking liars because of you. But herein you appear among your people together as Giants fighting against the truth. I, on the other hand, thank God and Jesus Christ (whose name you carry in such a way that, according to God's righteous judgment, you have denied it, and now falsely attribute it to one another through a highly godless piece of villainy). And I also thank Him for this: that I see myself properly displeasing to you, which still gives me satisfaction through His benefits; both to live, and to be sufficiently prosperous in the body according to my years, and to persevere therein, despite your premeditated lies against God's Word. But you people, through this single invention so shamelessly put forward—wherein you lie that I am dead and have departed from the truth—have openly shown yourselves (and you, Puteanus, in particular) to be from him who is the father of lies and the first murderer of men. You have come forth from that very same most impure pit of the abyss <note>A pun on the name Puteanus, from the Latin 'puteus' meaning 'well' or 'pit'</note> spoken of in the Revelation of John, chapter 9. From there I have also derived your "Creation," and I have wished to set it down below, so that you might find me still to be alive and be convicted of living in that same truth. What more is there to say? Nothing, unless—if anything can be added to that shamelessness—you now also, through that same Satanic spirit, invent and pretend that I am alive again and have once more departed from your false Catholicism? Rather, may God grant that you may acknowledge those whom you have lied about for so long, and begin to teach those to whom such things are attributed, whose shamelessness Satan himself is almost ashamed of. But that you have also been so bold as to lay such false slanders and stains of shame upon the Christian City of Geneva (through God's true benefits); why should that same City answer you, since they do not deem you worthy even to be spat upon?*

# THEODORE <note>Likely referring to Theodore Beza, the successor to Calvin, or a pseudonym used in anti-Jesuit literature.</note>

### The true GENESIS of that most foul excrement of Satan—that is, of that new sect of "One-and-a-Half-Monks" <term>Sesquimonachorum: literally 'monks and a half,' a derisive term suggesting they are more than monks or a strange hybrid</term>—founded by Ignatius the Marrano <note>original: "Ignatio Marrano." A derogatory term for a Spanish convert from Judaism or Islam, used here to attack Loyola's heritage</note>, who falsely assume the most holy name of Jesus while casting aside the name of Christians.

*Christ as man, and Jesus as God, are one and the same,*
*The one holy flock acknowledges Him as its head:*
*Yet the noble name of the assembly of saints is derived*
*Not from Jesus, but from the name of Christ.*
*For in that He is God and the only Savior, Jesus,*
*He claims this name for Himself alone:*
*But in that He is Christ the man, He deigns to share that title*
*With as many brothers as there are godly men in the world.*
*Learn therefore, Reader, by what reasoning recently*
*That cohort obtained its name from Jesus.*
*The wrath of a vengeful God had commanded horrible furies*
*To be summoned into the world from the depths of Hell:*
*As Satan sent them out from the Pit, three and four times*
*He cried out, shouting: "Go far from Jesus, you wicked one!"* <note>original: "procul ab IESV-ITE." The author is using a Latin pun: "Iesu-ite" sounds like "Jesuit" but translates to "Go from Jesus."</note>
*When that famous Father IGNATIUS came running up,*
*Whose name was given from fire mixed with Mischief,* <note>original: "ATE cui admixto nomen ab igne dedit." A pun on Ignatius (ignis/fire) and Ate (the Greek goddess of mischief/ruin).</note>
*"I accept the omen," he said, "Go far from Jesus, my progeny*
*Worthy of your father; go far from Jesus!"*
*From "Go-from-Jesus" <insert>IESV-ITE</insert> they then flew through the mouths of men,*
*And hence it happened that this cohort took its name.*
*And certainly, among such great ranks of false brothers,*
*No cohort goes so far from Jesus as they do.*

->That is:<-

The birth of Satan's stinking filth—that is, the new sect of the "One-and-a-Half-Monks," of which the founder is Ignatius Maranus; who, falsely assuming the most holy name of Jesus, have rejected the name of Christians.

*Christ as man, and Jesus as God, are one at the same time,*
*One holy Community which acknowledges Him as its rich head:*
*Not out of Jesus, however, but out of Christ's name, brought forth,*
*The name of the holy people is truly and gloriously considered.*
*For whereby He is God and the sole Preserver, Jesus,*
*That name alone He claims for Himself in this way:*

### THESE WRITINGS D. Beza has brought to light:

Rhyme-poems, printed by *Henry Stephens*. <note>original: "Henricum Stephanum," the famous humanist printer.</note>

Tragedy or the earnest Poem of the sacrifice of *Abraham*.
In Theology, finely printed:
The New Testament’s new Translation, with the Annotations, in which the reason for the translation is given.

He has published many more other writings with fine, very learned prefaces.

*The Life of Doctor John CALVIN.*

*Five Dialogues of Athanasius on the Holy Trinity; the comprehensive and orthodox exposition of Faith by Anastasius and Cyril—writings never before printed. Likewise, he has translated into Latin the four books of Basil against the godless Eunomius, and has published them in Greek and Latin; to which is also added the book of Phoebadius against the Arians, never before brought to light.*

*His own five books of the Psalms of David and the other Prophets, explained with arguments and paraphrases, and expressed in Latin with many kinds of meter.*

*Annotations on the letters of Paul to the Romans, Galatians, Philippians, and Colossians, published from the sermons of Caspar Olevianus.*

*Icons, that is, the true Likenesses of Men renowned for both learning and godliness, through whose earnestness and singular service, partly the learning or knowledge of the good arts have been renewed and restored, and partly the true knowledge of God has been increased in many kingdoms of the world, both in our times and those of our ancestors.*

# FRANCISCVS IVNIVS.

How courageously and with what diligence the French have advanced the affairs of the Church of God, and defended the purest doctrine of the Gospel against all manner of enemies, is well known to those experienced in Church History and those who pursue the Holy Scriptures. Among them, **Franciscus Junius** of Bourges <note>Bourges is a city in central France, known for its university where many Reformers studied.</note> is to be held in no small regard: a man noble by birth, renowned in virtue and godliness, and considered distinguished in learning and writings. He was a man who experienced so much adversity throughout nearly his entire life that anyone who diligently examines the history of his years would be filled with wonder, and would have to laud and praise God’s providence and kindness toward this man. He was born in the year of Christ **1545**. At thirteen years of age, he devoted himself to the study of Law, in which he spent two years. At that time, in the high school <note>University</note> of Bourges in his fatherland, the very famous and godly jurists *Franciscus Duarenus, Hugo Donellus, Antonius Contius, and Ludovicus Ruffardus* <note>These were preeminent legal scholars of the 16th century who applied humanistic methods to Roman Law.</note> were lecturing on Civil or Citizen’s Law. While he took up one study after another from that time onward, he finally set before himself that which is most excellent of all, and resolved to pursue it forever. For when he arrived in Geneva in the year **1562**, and had exercised himself there in the study of Holy Scripture and languages, making great progress therein, it happened that he was judged to be a useful and capable Minister of the Word. Since those of Antwerp desired a minister for their French Church, Junius—with *Crispinus* <note>Jean Crespin, the famous martyrologist and printer in Geneva.</note> constantly advising him to do so, and the need of the Church requiring it—came to Antwerp in the year **1565**. In that city, many sought those Heavenly treasures with great zeal. With what peril and danger he taught the Gospel there and in other cities of the Netherlands is evident from the Histories and Martyr-books of that time. In that same year, in the assembly of the Nobles at Brussels, he preached Christ there as a faithful Teacher; which sermon being ended, it was

<column-break/>

# FRANCISCUS

of the covenant framed and concluded both within and without <note>referring to domestic and foreign alliances</note> against the tyranny of souls and bodies, namely the Spanish Inquisition; Junius remained silent and listened intently. These first foundations against the **Inquisition** were laid in the House of Culemborg: for that very reason, two years later, that house was razed and leveled to the ground, and salt was strewn over the entire site with explicit curses pronounced thereon; the Duke of **Alva** was then raging in his cruelty in the midst of the Netherlands. During this time, *Junius* performed many good services and brought things to pass through which the course of Religion <note>The Protestant Reformation</note> was advanced. This young man, daily interacting with the Princes and the Heroes of the land, also noticed and recorded much; they served in the rightful place of **Hercules** <note>A metaphor for heroic labor</note> as they desired to tear down and suppress that horrible monster of the **Inquisition**. The same was observed and noted by *Junius* at that time. From him, those who wish to be fully informed of the events of that period have much to learn. In the Palatinate, under **Frederick the Pious**, **Casimir**, and the latter's nephew **Frederick IV**, he taught the Gospel in the Church and the School of Christ for a long time, and explained it through his writings. That frequently reprinted **Translation** of the Old Testament, which is used by many today, has earned him a great name. In addition to his excellent knowledge of Holy Scripture, he was also highly practiced and gifted in foreign and learned languages, the elegance of the Arts, and in Antiquities and Histories: as is shown by his annotations on the authors of both languages <note>Latin and Greek</note>, **Curopalates**, and the remarks on the books of **Bellarmine**, *On the Transfer of the Roman Empire*, published just a few months ago: in which, acting against **Bellarmine** (who therein acts as a Father of the Father, and Scepter-giver—namely, establishing the Majesty and power of the Roman **Jupiter** <note>A critical reference to the Pope</note>), he shows how very great his knowledge of History and Politics is. He lives, writes, and teaches today in the High School <term>University</term> at Leiden, where he has now spent ten years, and has made the same school, as well as Holland, famous through his writings. God grant that, living in this Academy, he may see the end of this heavy war, of which he saw the first beginnings and which he observed and noted as a young man. # THE DESCRIPTION
## OF THE HORRIBLE MON-
### ster of the INQUISITION of SPAIN,
### of which mention was made previously in the
### Oration of JUNE.

Do you wish to know, you who are a stranger in our lands,
What the "Holy" Inquisition might be, and do you desire to understand
That? This wicked Monster, never before seen, was brought forth to us by the
Spanish nature.
From the three-headed Geryon <note>In Greek mythology, Geryon was a giant with three bodies or heads slain by Hercules; here used to symbolize Spanish tyranny.</note>, renowned for robbery and plunder,
Its blood is descended. Where strong Hercules, as if enraged,
Killed him, as was deserved, and took the conquered treasure as booty.
Hellishly-horrible, exceedingly large the Inquisition Monster is,
Three-headed, its jaws wide open, it is fat-bellied, thick and stout,
Its distended belly carries a hundred men hideously,
Its claws are iron-hard, the tail black, coiled and rich in venom.
This Cerberus-like <note>original: "Cerbericq'"; referring to the three-headed hound of Hades.</note> Monster’s first head, which raises itself high,
Is Ambition; the second, near the earth, which seeks and strikes at everything,
Is grasping Avarice; the third maw is cruel Tyranny,
Spewing fire and flame, eyes burning, fast-running and full of grief,
Through the blood and sweat of the poor, it feeds its thick paunch-sack,
Rightly named "Inquisition" <note>The term literally means "inquiry" or "investigation."</note>, which searches Soul, body, and Property.
The milk of its breasts pours out of its own accord, where yellow-Monks <note>likely a reference to the saffron or yellow-tinted robes/insignia of certain orders or the "sanbenito" penitential garment.</note> snatch,
And the Roman She-wolf’s cubs pinch sharply with their claws.
There she hunts great Spoil for the Spanish Tyrant, her Lord.
The Monster awakens in the attack; Grasping, it gains more strength.
A thousand-fold cunning deceit is held within its coiled tail.
With its hard claws, it scratches out the sweetness of the Laws of Freedom.
And its body is draped with these mournful-sorrowful proverbs:
*He who will not become Roman Catholic, let him bow stubbornly under the Spanish yoke:*
*So I will it, So I command it.* <note>original: "Sic volo, sic jubeo"; a Latin legal maxim of absolute tyranny.</note> Again: *It is all the Spanish King’s.*
With a terrifying howl it cries out over the whole earthly dale:

<summary>A vivid allegorical poem describing the Spanish Inquisition as a three-headed mythological monster fueled by ambition, greed, and tyranny. It portrays the institution as a predatory force devouring the wealth and freedom of the Netherlands for the benefit of the Spanish Crown.</summary>
<keywords>Inquisition, Spain, Allegory, Tyranny, Geryon, Hercules, Roman Catholic Church, Dutch Revolt, Oppression, Freedom of Law</keywords>

<image-desc>An engraving at the top of the page features a celestial diagram set against a dark, cross-hatched background filled with leafy vine patterns. The diagram shows the Sun, Moon, and Earth in alignment. At the top is the Sun, depicted with a human face and radiating rays. In the center is the Moon, also personified with a face and shown in a phase where it is between the Sun and Earth. At the bottom is the Earth, shown as a shaded globe. Dotted orbital paths connect the bodies, illustrating the Moon's position in relation to the Sun and Earth.</image-desc>

The Moon, as if joined more closely to her brother <note>The Sun was traditionally personified as the brother of the Moon in classical and early modern literature</note>, seems to the foolish to have utterly perished;
Yet, on that side where she looks upon the lights of the approaching Sun, she shines back far more brightly.
So it is with the godly, who seem to the common crowd to have perished in death—far be it from me to say they have perished!
Rather, having taken hold of Christ himself who is now nearer to them, they obtain that which they have sought.

# DEATH IS THE
## Life of the Godly.

<image-desc>Ornate woodcut initial 'G' featuring leafy scrollwork within a square frame.</image-desc>

G<note>The initial 'G' begins the word 'Ghenoech' (Enough).</note>enough we consider to have been said here now concerning those who, being freed from the body's imprisonment, we believe live with Christ. Foolish and godless are the judgments of mortal men who allow themselves to imagine that the life of Christians ends through death: whereas Death itself is the way to Life, which is far more glorious and excellent than that by which we are currently said to live. Which thing, in his *Emblems* <note>original: "Emblematis"; referring to a book of symbolic illustrations with moral meanings</note>, very wittily conceived, the most eloquent *Theologian* <note>original: "Theologus"; likely referring to a specific church father or contemporary scholar</note> has wished to compare for us through the darkening of that great Light <note>The sun</note> and the Image standing nearest the earth <note>The moon or a shadow</note>. Let them live then, those whom the fools think are extinguished: let those live, I say, (so that I may use the words of *Cicero* <note>original: "Ciceronis"; Marcus Tullius Cicero, the Roman statesman and orator</note>, who not unskillfully among the Heathens disputed concerning the immortality of the soul) who have been drawn out and flown up from the body's bonds or constraints as if from a deep Dungeon: whereas our aforementioned life is a death. Truly, if you wish to see how we are pressed down and scattered in the spirit by this earthen hut (as the *Scripture* <note>original: "Schrift"</note> calls it), with what burdens and torments we are overtaken, by what sad labors and pains we are weakened and broken, what difficulties, deceits, snares, and dangers we are subjected to almost every moment—whoever wishes to note this earnestly, I say; we shall find that he must be called a Fool who names this present life of ours a "life." Of that life I speak not at all here, which is moved by vain things, driven hither and thither as if on the wind: those who lie drowned and smothered in the stinking pool of worldly pleasures, and are counted as dead among the more wise, and judged hardly worthy of a human name. When the Heathen *Philosophers* <note>original: "Philosophen"</note> look upon Heavenly things and those things which are eternal with an earnest observation, and totally despise these earthly things passing by in a moment, so they laugh and mock those who turn all their diligence and contemplation to such vanity: What must we Christians then feel about this, who [do] not a [single]—

<summary>This page argues that physical death is actually the beginning of true life for the godly, contrasting the soul's liberation from the "dungeon" of the body with the "dead" state of those obsessed with worldly pleasures.</summary>
<keywords>immortality of the soul, Cicero, afterlife, body as prison, vanity of the world, Christian philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 320 ---
<meta>The previous page likely discussed the nature of mortality or the limitations of earthly existence, leading into this comparison between worldly life and the afterlife. The catchword "ick" (I) at the bottom indicates the speaker is about to transition to a personal or specific point on the following page.</meta>

imagined or dreamed, like that of the **Philosophers**, but a certain hope of that other life is given: which, if one wishes to compare it to this present one, is merely to be called a shadow of life and essentially a death. On the contrary, this very death, which we commonly call a death, is for the believers only a name, indeed, it is the door or entrance to life: which, having been called, who would pass it by, unless he were senseless and godless, misled by the love of this fabricated life covered over with shadows? And although eternal life is assigned to the good, namely all the Godly or believers, which we call true **Salvation** <note>Dutch: *Salicheyt*</note>; nevertheless, not everyone is prepared or destined for the same level or place of happiness in that excellent, high, and all-accomplishing day of God to come. In such a way that we believe those who, through their labor, diligence, and industry, have taught very many Christians about *Christ* <note>original: *Christum*</note>—the only way of our salvation—and have gone before many with good examples, shall enjoy greater happiness in Heaven than others. Which ought to rouse us all to the defense and maintenance of *Christ's* Kingdom, so that we do not slow down in the course of our lives to proclaim Christ and in Christian conduct, but as if now more strengthened, may proceed therein to the end more diligently and with a braver spirit. Which **Cicero** describes very excellently in the sixth book of *On the Republic* <note>original: *der Ghemeynten Regeringhe*; the translator refers here to the "Dream of Scipio" from Cicero's *De Re Publica*</note>, where he treats the immortality of the soul: which is permitted for us Christians, as those gifted by the spirit of God, to apply to this purpose; and it reads thus according to the sense: *And so that you, Africanus, might labor all the more diligently toward the building up of the community, know this: That for all those who have protected, helped, and enlarged the Fatherland, a certain and specific place is prepared in Heaven, where they shall enjoy eternal happiness. For to that great God who rules the whole world, nothing (among all that happens on earth) is more pleasing than the assembly and unity of men lawfully brought together, which are called Cities. The rulers and protectors of which (having driven this) shall return there from whence they came.* This is of the Heathens: from whom, as wrongful possessors, we take these and similar sayings for ourselves as our own. Without God, they could not please God: and those who do not please Him cannot reach that eternal inheritance. But that all the Godly [possess] the certainty of preservation and happiness, yet, as
I

--- Page 321 ---
<meta>The previous page likely discussed the nature of the afterlife or the rewards of the faithful, leading into this discussion on the varying degrees of glory in heaven.</meta>

As I have said, it appears clearly enough to us that the levels of **blessedness** <note>original: "ghelucksalicheyt," referring to the state of supreme happiness in heaven</note> are distinct. For just as one star differs from another in clarity (as the Apostle <note>A reference to Paul in 1 Corinthians 15:41</note> testifies), so also, without doubt, shall be the resurrection of the holy dead. And as the Angel said to **Daniel**:
> *The Teachers now shall shine as the brightness of the Heaven; and those who point many toward righteousness as the stars forever.* <note>A translation of Daniel 12:3</note>
Which is as much as if the Angel said: That the children of God, who give themselves entirely to God and are driven by the Spirit of understanding, and thereafter also point the way to others, they shall not only all be saved, but also possess an unspeakable power and glory, to which nothing in the world can be compared. Has not the Heavenly Father then deigned to grant the Church’s teachers and ministers of righteousness great honor here? And what could be said or esteemed as greater or more select than this glorious testimony of God, through which He especially adorns the Teachers and Ministers of His Church? For that is the highest thing, to which all our fame and testimonies tending toward praise, originating from the spirit of man, are in no way to be compared. Therefore, those whom the supporters of the **Antichrist's Kingdom** <note>A common Reformation-era term used to describe the Roman Catholic Church or those perceived as persecutors of the "true" faith</note> say are extinguished, enjoy **eternal Life**. For these, freed from the prison of the body, are with **Christ**, whose name they have borne, in whom they have believed, whom they have proclaimed and taught, and to whom they have brought others. So that we believe no one is with **Christ** (except those who live rightly). They themselves also still live among us today while their souls are in Heaven: they speak, they preach, and declare the Holy Scriptures to us through their works and writings. They point out the way of virtue and godliness to us when we read their glorious, left-behind writings and follow in their footsteps. In vain, then, do the Antichristian ones rage and rave over the bodies of the godly, whom they think are extinguished when they have taken them from here through torments and martyrdom. In vain do they pull together the elements and torments for the destruction of Christians, who after their death are gifted with eternal life, and even then live more and more among us and bring forth their strengths. In vain are the counsels, the deceit, the instruments, and weapons of the **Inquisitors** and Tyrants, who foolishly [think] to terrify the Christians with water, earth, fire, and various **executioner's arts** <note>original: "Beuls-consten," referring to the specialized methods of torture and execution used by state or religious authorities</note>, plagues, and torments.

<summary>This page argues that there are different degrees of glory in heaven, specifically exalting teachers and martyrs who guide others to righteousness. It defies the power of the Inquisition, asserting that while persecutors may kill the body, the writings and spirits of the godly continue to live and instruct the faithful.</summary>
<keywords>resurrection, degrees of glory, Daniel 12:3, martyrdom, Antichrist, Inquisitors, eternal life, religious persecution</keywords>

--- Page 322 ---
<meta>The catchword "Clarissis" indicates the transition to the next section, likely a dedication or a list of illustrious figures. This page concludes the biographical reflection on the subject's life and introduces a poetic tribute.</meta>

let themselves be persuaded. Far be it, as *Tertullian says in his Apology* <note>original: "Tertullianus in Apologetico"; Tertullian was an early Christian author from Carthage, c. 155–220 AD</note>, that *by human fire the followers of God should be rooted out, or that they should lament the suffering in which they are tested.* The Palm, or Date-tree, the more it is pressed down by weight, the higher it rises against it. Against the cold the Saffron blooms: the fruits of the earth, covered by frost and snow, come forth better and more joyfully: the darkened and eclipsed Moon appears much clearer on that side where she beholds the Sun and the Heavens: So also the Christians, who strengthen themselves in adversity, spring up, grow, rejoice, and become more beautiful. So that no evil can overcome the Christians here, in view of the joyful and godly hope, as well as the assurance of the eternal and future life: with which we believe those to be gifted, whose Life and Labor we have briefly presented here. And just as they now find themselves truly blessed in Heaven: so also shall they still live and shine for us here below, who are still wandering, through their memories and writings. This has been expressed for us through the learned and elegant Epigram, added to this presented Figure and its explanation, by the very renowned man *Paulus Melissus* <note>Paul Schede Melissus (1539–1602), a famous humanist poet and librarian at Heidelberg</note>, Poet and Librarian to the Count Palatine, writing on the death of the great *Calvin*:

> *O blind Sophist, submerged in the black lake of Hell?* <note>original: "Erebi"; Erebos refers to the primordial darkness or the underworld</note>
> *The golden Sun never perishes in dark shadow,*
> *Nor does the fiery star change its glowing torches,*
> *Though they may hide their rays behind a pitch-black cloud,*
> *And behind mists and dark vapors.*
> *And just as they are not to be thought of as having succumbed to death,*
> *Whose eyes a lingering rest has closed in sleep:*
> *So also does* **CALVIN** *still live in spirit, though in body*
> *He sleeps peacefully until the final day.*
> *Nor do you only live now, but what you once did*
> *You do still, O greatest herald of the great* **GOD**:
> *And with us today speaks, and shall always speak,*
> *The Spirit, which flourishes still in your writings:*

*Your glory shines more clearly and with much more splendor,*

*How do you snap and snore thus, as if the light were taken from us,*

*Where this clear Sun never brought forth a shadow,*

*Your dark mists could never prevail against this light.*

---

**Just as the Moon, having approached her brother <note>The Sun</note> more closely,**
**Seems to the ignorant to be entirely diminished, yes, completely perished;**
**While she nevertheless, in the part which the Sun shines upon,**
**Shines much more clearly: So also do**
**Evil men imagine that the good (being dead) have perished:**
**What do I say? Since they are in communion with Christ,**
**And what they sought here, they find there alone.**

*Christ preserves the people whom Tyrants destroy.*
*The death of Christians is a Godly and good life:*
*Which they enjoy eternally, through the attainment of the Kingdom of Light,*
*Now destroy the body, you light-fleeing ones <note>original: "Licht-vlieders," those who run from the truth</note> who rage there.*

->THE END.<-

<summary>This page contains a poetic eulogy for John Calvin, using celestial metaphors to argue that although he is physically dead, his spiritual and intellectual influence remains a brilliant light against his "Sophist" detractors. It concludes with a defiant message that the death of a Christian is merely an entry into eternal life that tyrants cannot touch.</summary>
<keywords>John Calvin, Reformation, immortality of the soul, Sophists, Morning Star, light vs. darkness, martyrdom, Dutch poetry</keywords>

--- Page 325 ---
<meta>This is a title page for a Dutch emblem book published in 1603. The verses are arranged around a central image, emphasizing the virtue of Patience.</meta>

->Through hopeful desire, PATIENCE bears<-
->The willing yoke, meek and undaunted:<-
->And gazes constantly at the flowing of time’s fountain<-
->Until the stone wears away, and her deliverance is at hand.<-

<image-desc>An ornate central emblem featuring an oval engraving within a rectangular decorative frame adorned with fruit and foliage. Inside the oval, a female personification of Patience (PATIENTIA) is depicted chained by her feet to a stone pedestal. She holds an anchor <note>The anchor is a traditional symbol of hope.</note> in her left hand and a heart in her right. Above her, the Holy Spirit appears as a dove in a radiant sunburst, and a small winged figure or angel descends towards her. In the background, there is a landscape with ruins and a lamb. Circling the scene inside the oval is the Latin motto: True Patience Conquers <note>original: "VINCIT VERA PATIENTIA"</note>.</image-desc>

### IN THE HAGUE,
# By Beuckel Corneliszoon Nieulandt.
### In the year 1603.

<note>The vocabulary terms provided in the source refer to the virtue of Patience, the city of The Hague, the printer's name, and the date.</note>
